- [S5] FTC data spotlight on social-media scam losses, Apr 2026. https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/data-visualizations/data-spotlight/2026/04/reported-losses-scams-social-media-eight-times-higher-2020. Opened: yes.
- Repo: db/schema.sql, app/sitemap.ts, lib/referral.ts, app/me/record/page.tsx; docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json.

## E4. Competitive analyst C: broker-native features, trackers, data vendors (challenges: competitive A, red team)

### P2: Cross-examination by Competitive Analyst C (broker-native, trackers, data vendors)

Member: Competitive Landscape Analyst C. Pass 2, 2026-11-01. Targets: p1-competitive-copy-signals.md (A) and p1-red-team.md (RT).
New sources, all opened 2026-11-01: [N1] SnapTrade Developer Terms of Use, "Last Updated: May 22, 2026", https://snaptrade.com/developer-terms-of-use. [N2] SnapTrade pricing, https://snaptrade.com/pricing. [N3] SnapTrade Application Compliance Policy (updated 2024-09-19), https://snaptrade.com/compliance-policy. [N4] Repo, lib/broker.ts lines 100-113. [N5] A brokerage-list JSON from the vendor that another member saved in a saved copy of SnapTrade's public brokerage list (not committed). I parsed it but did not fetch it myself and its source endpoint was not recorded, so treat it as Medium.

**Correction to my own pass 1:** my C11 cited the developer terms as "updated 2024-09-27". The live page is dated **2026-05-22** [N1]. The clauses I quoted are still there, and the newer text adds more (below).

#### SnapTrade developer terms: red-team C8 checked against the live page

The page has no section numbers, but the numbers can be worked out. The Limitations section says "As provided in Section 4", and the survival clause says "Sections 2 through 17". Counting from Service as §1 gives Limitations on Use = §2, Term & Termination = §4, Usage and Data = §7 and Entire Agreement; Amendments = §12. **RT's section numbers are correct.**

- **§2(d) re-packaging.** RT quotes it accurately: "Not to mine, re-sell, or re-package any End User data obtained via the SnapTrade API to third parties without the express written consent of both the End User and SnapTrade." The clause asks for consent from **both** parties. The trader's opt-in covers only half, so the "trader consents" reading in my C11 is not enough on its own. **STANDS.**
- **§7(a) market data.** RT quotes it accurately: "any market data passed over the SnapTrade API will only be used to serve the End User for which that data is licensed by their broker". **STANDS, and the problem is worse than RT says:**
  - §2(f) adds: "will not store, resell, or redistribute Market Data passed over the SnapTrade API." RT missed this clause.
  - "Market Data" is defined to include "market value, securities or commodities prices" and "security descriptions" that come from third parties [N1].
  - The open-position percent in the repo is computed from the vendor's `price` field, documented as "Last known market price per share" [N4]. Showing that percent publicly to strangers is redistribution of a value derived from Market Data, which falls squarely under §2(f) and §7(a).
  - Closed-trade percents come from the user's own execution prices (activity `price`). Those are End User transaction data, not clearly Market Data, so they fall mainly under §2(d) consent, not §2(f).
  - Revised claim: **open positions (TR-4, F-6) carry the highest risk under the terms, and closed trades the lower risk.** The written request to SnapTrade should ask about the two separately.
- **§2(b), missed by both of us.** "may not sell, license, or purchase any data obtained from SnapTrade … except as expressly provided herein." This is the clause that matters most for the paid viewer tier (F-3). The "viewers pay for tools, not data" argument needs SnapTrade's explicit agreement.
- **§4 and §12.** "terminate or suspend … at any time, for any reason, with or without notice" is accurate. Pricing amendments take effect "at least thirty (30) days" after they are posted online, which is accurate. The brokerage list "is subject to change at any time with or without notice" (§2, also §8). **STANDS.**
- **New: §7(g)** requires deleting "all End User data … upon request from the applicable End User or SnapTrade." Neither target notes that this caps TR-5 ("records freeze on disconnect"): a frozen record must still be deleted when the trader asks. That cuts against RT C2's "freeze deters connects". The deterrent is weaker than RT assumes, because a trader can always have the record erased. It also weakens the trust promise that viewers rely on. Each side is partly right.
- **New: §3/Fees.** A processing fee of up to 3% may be added to invoices, and a Connected User stays billable until all of their connections are deleted [N1]. Both cut against RT C5's cost base (see below).

Overall verdict on RT C8: **STANDS (strengthened).** The conflict in the text is real and covers more clauses (§2(b), §2(f), §7(g)) than RT listed.

#### Challenges to the red team (RT)

- **RT C5, unit economics.** I checked the inputs: $1 per connected user per month on the Daily plan, $100/month platform fee, and Manual Sync "$0.05 per connection per sync" [N2], so RT's "per connection" wording is right and my pass-1 "$0.05 each" was looser. The arithmetic checks out: 0.104 / 8.80 = 1.18% (my own recomputation). Two corrections pull in opposite directions:
  - Traders who leave but stay connected keep billing, which raises cost per payer above RT's figure.
  - Personal/OAuth users are "$0 … for a limited time" [N2], which could lower it if our traders qualify. The vendor gives no end date.

  **STANDS on the arithmetic.** The inputs stay Low.
- **RT C8, incumbent bullet.** RT cites a 2021 regulator report that some brokers let customers show trades. That is now outdated in the direction that hurts us more: a large commission-free app broker opened a verified trade feed to all eligible US customers in Oct 2026 (my C1). **STANDS, and stronger than stated.**
- **RT C4, "two tabs and a notes app".** Viewer-paid tiers at $8.99-$19.99/month already exist in linked-broker social apps, one of them with 7.3K ratings (my C5). That shows some viewers in this category pay for tools on records that are free to view. There is still no conversion data. **WEAKENED (from Medium to Low-Medium).** The fake-door test remains the right way to check it.
- **RT C3** and **A C1/C7** agree with my C2/C8 (rival venues pay traders; forex read-only verification). **STANDS.**

#### Challenges to copy-signals (A)

- **A C11, "No pattern reviewed offers a portable, read-only record".** A sampled only copy and signal venues. Linked-broker social apps already provide (a) multiple brokers and (b) read-only, non-executing records, and a white-label verification layer is already embedded in one broker's forum (my C3, C5). Only (c), whole-record rules with lower-bound ranking, and (d), percent-only display, remain unclaimed. **WEAKENED.** Revised claim: "portability is table stakes; the whole-record rules are the gap."
- **A C13, "likeliest fast follower is a strategy/signal marketplace".** The groups that already have multi-broker sync and a P&L engine are trade journals (500-900+ broker integrations claimed) and linked-broker social apps (my C5, C6). The app broker already has verification at scale (my C1). **WEAKENED.** A's arithmetic ($10,100/month for 10,000 traders) is correct but leaves out the deletion-based billing caveat.
- **A C10, "the only one where neither viewer money nor trader income depends on results".** That is true among copy and signal patterns. It is not true across the whole market: trackers ($7.50-$16) and social apps ($8-$20) also charge viewers without regard to results (my C5, C7). **WEAKENED.** It needs to be scoped to copy and signal venues.
- **A C12(c), coverage of fraud-heavy venues.** The saved vendor list [N5] has 38 entries, 35 typed as traditional brokerages and 3 as crypto exchanges, with 12 allowing crypto symbols and none forex. My pass 1 found "40+ institutions". The crypto pools and forex venues where most enforcement in A C9 sits are therefore barely covered. **STANDS, and sharper.** The "Verified" badge cannot reach the venues where cherry-picking does the most harm.

#### Conflicts resolved
- On how much threat a fast follower poses, A and my file disagree. Mine is better supported for stocks, because the followers I name already have the data pipes. A's candidates have the incentive but not the multi-broker data.
- On the terms, my pass-1 consent reading was too generous, and RT's reading is right and incomplete. The written question to SnapTrade must cover §2(b), §2(d), §2(f) and §7(a) for (1) public closed-trade percents, (2) public open-position percents, and (3) a paid compare tier. It must also ask how §7(g) deletion interacts with TR-5.

#### Items for the fact-checker
1. The developer-terms date (2026-05-22) and the exact text of §2(b), §2(d), §2(f), §7(a), §7(g) and the Market Data definition [N1].
2. That `price` in the positions response is market data from a third party, not a broker-computed value (vendor API docs; [N4] quotes them).
3. Pricing: $1/$2 per connected user, $100 platform fee, $0.05 per connection per sync, the end date of the Personal/OAuth $0 offer, and whether our users count as Personal/OAuth or app-managed [N2].
4. Vendor coverage count (38 vs "40+") and crypto/forex coverage; record where [N5] came from.
5. The app broker's social feed: fully live for all eligible US customers (2026-10-14 trade press), and whether profiles can be seen on the open web (my C1).
6. The copy-trading broker's figures: 1.5% of assets under copy, "over 3,300" traders ≈ 0.1% of funded accounts, 3.81M funded accounts in FY2025 (A C1, my C2).
7. RT C5 inputs: trader_share 0.10, conv 0.015, cac_paying $150 (docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json), and the blended net of $8.80.
9. Linked-broker social app prices and rating counts ($8.99-$19.99; 7.3K ratings) and trade-journal integration counts (500+/900+), the latter possibly counting CSV import.

## E5. User researcher, supply side (traders) (challenges: red team, market sizing)

### Council pass 2: User researcher, supply side, cross-examination

Member: user researcher (supply). Pass: 2. Date: 2026-11-01. Targets: p1-red-team.md (RT) and p1-market-sizing.md (MS).
Fetches this pass (4): vendor pricing page [V1], an academic institute's summary of the finfluencer paper [V2], the NFCS
2024 investor report PDF (text extracted) [V3], and the speculator-skill abstract [V4]. I also read the repo code (lib/connections.ts,
lib/claims.ts, lib/copy.ts) to check one MS claim. No interviews, no invented data.

#### Target 1: Red Team

**RT-C1 "the pool of people whose full record helps them is tiny."** The abstract confirms "less than 1%" of the day-trader
population predictably earns positive abnormal returns net of fees [V4]. Two things are overstated. (a) The CFD figure
(74-89%) is about leveraged products in the EU, not US stocks and ETFs. It supports the general direction but is not
evidence for this product's pool. (b) The claim assumes status is the only reason to publish. My C8/C10 name two segments
that gain from a full record whatever its sign: accountability or process traders (S3), and "I show my losses" sellers.
They are small, but the first 50 only needs about 30 sharers. Also, persistent skill (<1%) is not the same as a record that
looks green. Over a short window, luck alone turns far more records green, and that tension is RT-C2's survivorship point.
**Verdict: WEAKENED.** Revised: few records will be *flattering over a long window*; a minority segment publishes for
non-status reasons; the size of that segment is unmeasured.

**RT-C2 "adverse selection: cherry-picking of traders, not trades."** I agree, and it is the sharpest supply point in
either file. One addition: the selection runs on *timing* as well as on who connects. TR-3 imports full history, so a trader
cannot connect "after a good month" without also showing earlier months. That limits timing selection only to the extent
the broker returns long history. Long history is not certain (F-1 import depth; vendors vary). RT's proposed preview test conflicts
with TR-1 only if the preview lets a trader hide trades. A whole-record preview before publishing is compatible.
**Verdict: STANDS**, with the history-depth caveat.

**RT-C3 "the signals-seller ICP has an incentive against this product."** This is supported for large sellers: 28% skilled,
16% unskilled, 56% antiskilled, and the antiskilled have more followers and influence [V2]. The summary page does not give
the sample's country or size, so neither RT nor MS can call it US evidence (see MS-C9). The refutation bar (a 20% cold
connect rate) is a test of large sellers. The realistic early S1 users are small sellers with a transparency pitch (my C10).
Testing only cold, large sellers would wrongly refute the segment. **Verdict: STANDS for large sellers; WEAKENED as a claim
about S1 as a whole.** Agreed with RT: the F-5 "paid promotion" Terms ban may exclude S1 by contract. That is an owner
decision.

**RT-C5 "each payer carries about 7 traders; paid acquisition loses."** The vendor page confirms $1 per connected user per
month on the read-only daily plan, a $100/month platform fee, and $0.05 per manual sync. A billable user is one who has
linked at least one account; the page defines no activity test [V1]. That makes RT's "zero-audience traders are pure cost"
point stronger: dormant connected traders bill the same as active ones. The 14-month carried-cost life is an assumption. It
deserves a sensitivity check, because traders' and payers' lifetimes are independent. **Verdict: STANDS (arithmetic); inputs
remain Low.** A supply-side design lever neither file names: a "frozen record" state (TR-5) that the trader chooses,
which stops billing while keeping the record public, if the vendor allows it.

**RT-C9 "the kill gates are noisy."** I re-checked the arithmetic: n=50, p=0.55, sd = √(50·0.55·0.45) = 3.52; P(X ≥ 30) with
continuity correction is z = (29.5-27.5)/3.52 = 0.57, which gives about 28%. Correct. This connects directly to my H3,
where I estimated a sharing rate of about 55%. But my H3 used a 15% "positive edge" share from an unopened snippet, which
[V4]'s <1% contradicts. Recomputed with "has a green record at first sync" in place of skill (assumption: 40% green by luck
over short windows, 80% vs 50% share rates): 0.4×0.8 + 0.6×0.5 = 62%. The gate result depends on market conditions and
recruiting mix. Either way, RT is right that a single n=50 reading cannot tell 55% from 62%. **Verdict: STANDS.**

#### Target 2: Market sizing

**MS-C6 "options skipped; 21% of investors have traded options."** The 21% is correct overall, but it understates the
problem for supply: **43% of investors aged 18-34** report trading options (24% at 35-54, 10% at 55+) [V3]. The ICP-S2 hobbyist
and the active creators skew young, so for them the options gap is about twice what MS implies. That confirms my
Simulation B friction (their proudest trades are missing). **Verdict: WEAKENED (understated).** Revised: for the 18-34
creator pool, about 4 in 10 have options activity the sync drops. That makes S2 a weaker first segment than S3/S4.

**MS-C7 "paper-trading entries would show as Verified."** Refuted at the account level. The code reads the vendor's paper flag
(`isPaper`), filters paper accounts out before import (`lib/connections.ts`, `real = accounts.filter(!isPaper)`), re-reads
the flag on every sync (`lib/claims.ts`), and tells the user "simulated trades are never part of a record" (`lib/copy.ts`).
One risk remains: the filter trusts the vendor flag. If a simulated-only brokerage's accounts are not flagged, they would
pass. **Verdict: REFUTED as stated; residual NEEDS FACT-CHECK** (do the 3 simulated brokerages set the flag?).

**MS-C9 "creators who post P&L monthly: 20k / 75k / 250k."** The low bound uses the finfluencer study's population as
"about one microblog". The source summary gives no country and no count [V2], so ~29,000 cannot stand in for US adults. The
base 0.3% and high 1% are assumptions with no anchor. MS is right that size does not bind the week-8 gate. What binds is the
share willing to show losses, and per my C1/C9 and RT-C1 that depends on segment. **Verdict: WEAKENED.** Revised: the size
of the US creator pool is unknown, with no sourced lower bound. Treat 20k-250k as an assumption range only.

**MS-C10 "29% social groups/boards; 30% a video platform, 61% of investors aged 18-34."** The 29% for social media groups or
message boards (2024, down from 32%) checks out [V3]. In [V3] the 61% aged 18-34 figure is about *making decisions based on
finfluencer recommendations*, not about video-platform use. The extracted chart for "online videos about business and finance"
reads 40-43%, not 30%, though the column alignment in the extract is uncertain. **Verdict: NEEDS FACT-CHECK.** The SAM
viewer base (C11) uses 26-30%, so it may be low rather than high.

**MS-C2/C4 "4+ trades: 36%; 18-34 fell 47%→38%".** Confirmed [V3]. The ≥12/yr cut is still an assumption. **STANDS.**

#### Conflicts between files
- Skill share: RT "<1% persistent" [V4] versus MS "28% skilled" [V2]. Both are correct but measure different things:
  day traders' own net returns versus finfluencers' recommended-stock abnormal returns. Neither file should use one to
  rebut the other. My C9 15% figure is withdrawn in favour of the luck-based recomputation above (Low).
- Options gap: MS 21% versus my Simulation B. The age split [V3] supports mine for the creator pool.
- Paper accounts: MS-C7 versus the code. The code is right.
- S1 viability: RT says the incentive runs against S1. I say small transparency sellers are viable. Both hold: RT covers
  the large-audience part, mine the first-50 part. Neither has primary data.

#### Items for the fact-checker
1. Vendor plan: $1/connected user/month daily read-only, $100/month base, $0.05/manual sync, billing on "linked at least one account" (RT-C5, MS-C14, BRIEF) [V1].
2. "<1% of day traders predictably earn positive net abnormal returns" (RT-C1/C3) [V4]; 97% / 0.4% futures figures (RT-C1, my C1).
3. Finfluencer 28/16/56% split, sample size (~29,000?), platform and country (RT-C3, MS-C8/C9) [V2].
4. NFCS 2024: options 21% overall / 43% aged 18-34; social groups 29%; online-video share (30% vs 40-43%); finfluencer use 26% / 61% aged 18-34 (MS-C6, MS-C10, my C7).
5. Do the 3 simulated brokerages set the vendor's `is_paper` flag (MS-C7)?
6. Kill-gate arithmetic: ±13.6 pts, 28% pass at p=0.55 (RT-C9).
7. Payer lifetime inputs: 7% monthly churn, 44% annual renewal, 14-month trader cost life, CAC $150 (RT-C5; docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json).
8. FINRA Foundation: new investors 8% (2024) vs 21% (2021); non-retirement ownership aged 18-34, 26%→21% (RT-C10).
9. Vendor developer terms §2/§7 on public display (RT-C8); this gates supply entirely.

#### Sources (this pass)
- [V1] SnapTrade pricing page (integrated vendor), accessed 2026-11-01. https://snaptrade.com/pricing. Opened: yes.
- [V2] Academic finance institute, summary page for research paper 23-30 on finfluencers, 2023 (rev. 2024). (URL omitted per T1) Opened: yes. Sample size and country are not on the page.
- [V3] FINRA Foundation, "Investors in the United States" (2024 NFCS investor survey), Dec 2025. https://www.finrafoundation.org/sites/finrafoundation/files/2025-11/NFCS_Investor_Survey_Report_White_Paper.pdf. Opened: yes (text extracted).
- [V4] Journal article on the cross-section of speculator skill, 2014, abstract. https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:eee:finmar:v:18:y:2014:i:c:p:1-24. Opened: yes.

## E6. User researcher, demand side (viewers) (challenges: pricing, competitive B)

### Pass 2: User researcher, demand side (viewers): cross-examination

Member: user researcher (demand side). Pass: 2. Date: 2026-11-01. Targets: p1-pricing.md (prefix P-) and
p1-competitive-social-forums.md (prefix F-). Own file: p1-user-viewers.md (prefix U-).
New sources opened this pass: [N1] the full text of the FINRA Foundation brief (April 2026), the same document as U-S1 and F-S1,
extracted with pdftotext; [N2] the FINRA 2025 social-media-influenced investing page; [N3] a National research organization mobile fact sheet;
[N4] arXiv 2006.13420. I also read the council's the dashboard's benchmark list (b10, b11, b15).

#### Target 1: p1-pricing.md

**P-C3 (base conversion should be 0.1-0.5% of monthly viewers, not 1.5%). Verdict: STANDS on direction; the range is unsupported.**
- The strongest argument is the arithmetic: 1.5% of monthly viewers divided by a 15% D7 return rate means 10% of returning viewers pay. That equals the hard-paywall median, for a product whose records are free.
- Two things the file misses:
  - The 15% is a kill-gate threshold, not a measured rate. If D7 comes in lower, the implied paying share of returners goes up.
  - Its newsletter benchmark (5-10%) is the platform's own guide. The council's b15 also lists about 3% observed and 1-2% for broad publications, which supports the target's direction further.
- Counter-evidence on willingness to pay in general: 70% of social-media-using investors, and 70% of finfluencer followers, say they rely on "financial newsletters or online resources that you pay for". The figures for non-users are 29% and 31% [N1, Figs 6-7]. My reading of the chart labels is Medium confidence. The target audience is already a paying audience, but what it pays for is judgment and ideas, not tools over free data.
- Revised claim: plan on roughly 0.25-0.5% of monthly viewers until a painted-door test reports. 0.1% is a floor with no source. This supersedes my U-C8, which used the 1.5% base. The pricing file is closer to right.

**P-C4 (the paid job is weak; side-by-side comparison is a desktop-shaped job). Verdict: STANDS, and it gets stronger.**
- Demand-side support:
  - My U-C9: the record a pre-purchase checker wants, a signal seller or paid group, is usually not on the platform.
  - That leaves a single plausible buyer, the follower-chooser, U-C8 segment 3.
- On mobile vs desktop: 24% of adults aged 18-29 are smartphone-dependent, meaning a smartphone but no home broadband (2026) [N3]. A compare view that works only on desktop would exclude a large share of ICP-D.
- Revised claim: compare must be designed for a phone first, or it is not a product for ICP-D at all.

**P-C2 (the annual plan is 2.5× the $39.99 median yearly app price). Verdict: WEAKENED.**
- The $39.99 median covers all app categories, including cheap utilities.
- In-category annual prices in the council's own benchmarks are $89.99-$100 (b06, b07).
- What stands: the discount is thin. $99.99 against $119.88 is 16.6% off, and there is retention evidence favouring lower-priced yearly plans.
- What doesn't stand: the price is not out of category.

**P-C9 (willingness to pay is $3-6/month or $25-50/year). Verdict: NEEDS FACT-CHECK; probably understates the spread.**
- It is derived from all-category medians.
- Benchmarks b10 and b11 show a minority paying about $300 a year (premium tier) and, in 2018, about $473 per subscriber per year for author marketplaces. The arithmetic: $5.2M ÷ 11,000 subscribers.
- Willingness to pay is likely bimodal: near zero for casual scrollers, and high for viewers who already pay for judgment.
- Revised claim: low willingness to pay for convenience; the real question is whether Record tools can be framed as part of the due-diligence job those viewers already pay for, which it may not be under F-3.

**P-C8.4 (test a card-up-front short trial or a money-back window). Verdict: STANDS, with partial support.**
- A field experiment at a SaaS firm randomized 7-, 14- and 30-day trials. A uniform 7-day trial was the best single policy, with +5.59% subscriptions [N4].
- It did not test "no trial". So it supports a 7-day arm as the one to test, not the claim that a trial beats no trial.
- My personas P2 and P3 (simulation) both stall at "cannot see compare before paying". That is consistent with this, but it is not evidence.

#### Target 2: p1-competitive-social-forums.md

**F-C1 (social channels are mainstream; 29%/26% overall, 60%/61% at ages 18-34). Verdict: STANDS.**
- Re-verified in [N1], n = 2,861. The 24% overall, 35% aged 18-29 and 13% at 65+ figures are confirmed on [N2].
- Caveat that both our files missed: the survey item cannot separate social media from message boards ([N1] footnote 4).
- Also, 68% of "social media users" are also finfluencer followers [N1, Table 1]. The two groups mostly overlap and are not separate segments.

**F-C2 (followers concentrate on long-form video and photo platforms). Verdict: STANDS; it contradicts my U-C1.**
- Channel shares among followers: short video 40%, against 77% for long-form video [N1].
- Among social-media users who do not follow finfluencers, short video is 9% [N1, Fig 1].
- My U-C1 said ICP-D's short-video channel assumption was "supported". That was overstated; the forums file is right.
- Revised U-C1: social media as a channel is supported. Short video specifically is a second-tier channel, mostly for people who already follow finfluencers. Long-form video and discussion boards matter more for reaching viewers.

**F-C3 (the ICP-D audience is the most fraud-exposed). Verdict: STANDS; the implication is incomplete.**
- The same group also does the most due diligence. 36% of social media users check a professional's background, against 14% of non-users, and they use 7.6 sources against 4.0 [N1].
- Exposure and diligence together are the "verify before paying" job (U-C3). The audience is a market for verification, not only a risk.

**F-C4 + F-C12 (the popularity-skill gap; uptake will skew to smaller or more skilled traders). Verdict: STANDS, and it converges with my U-C9.**
- Both files reach the same conclusion from different sides: the most-followed, least-skilled sellers have the least reason to connect.
- Demand consequence: the viewer's due-diligence target is systematically missing. Record tools then compares traders the viewer did not come for.
- Confidence: Medium. This is reasoning from both files; no data supports it yet.

**F-C12 ("no pricing data for paid author marketplaces"). Verdict: REFUTED within the council's own inputs.**
- b10 (2018): 150 authors, 11,000 subscribers, $5.2M a year.
- b11: $299 a year premium, with higher tiers at $499 and $2,400.
- These are dated and third-party-sourced, and both need a fact-check. They size the competing wallet at roughly $300-$470 per payer per year, for a different job (judgment, not records).

**F-C11 (5,000 external views a week works out to about 100-167 per trader). Verdict: STANDS.**
- The arithmetic is correct.
- Demand-side addition: chat groups and threads, the file's assumed unfurl venues, are where all three of my personas land. Viewer D7 then depends on those same links, because F3b forbids push.
- So the referral gate and the D7 gate fail together if sharing is weak. They are not independent kill tests.

#### Conflicts summary
- U-C8 base conversion (1.5%) vs P-C3 (0.25%): P is right; I revise to 0.25-0.5%.
- U-C1 "short video supported" vs F-C2: F is right; I revise U-C1 as above.
- P-C9 low willingness to pay vs the paid-resource reliance in [N1] and F's wallet (b10/b11): both hold, for different segments (bimodal).

#### Items for the fact-checker
1. [N1] Figs 6-7: 70% of social media users and 70% of finfluencer followers rely on paid newsletters or online resources, against 29% and 31% of non-users. I read these from extracted chart labels; confirm the label-to-bar mapping.
2. [N1] Fig 1: short-video channel at 40% among followers and 9% among non-follower users; long-form video at 77% and 45%.
3. Projection benchmark b10: 150 authors, 11,000 subscribers, $5.2M a year (2018). Find the primary source. Also b11, $299 a year.
4. Projection base conversion of 1.5% vs P-C3's 0.1-0.5%. Neither has a direct source. Confirm b16 (2.1% freemium, 10.7% hard paywall) and b15 (5-10% guide vs about 3% observed).
5. P-C2 / b-entries: the $39.99 North America median yearly price and the claim that lower-priced yearly plans keep 53.7% vs 48.3% at year one.
6. P-C6 / b20: is the 3.6% churn median monthly or annual?
7. [N3]: 24% of adults aged 18-29 are smartphone-dependent (national research organization, survey Jan-Jun 2026).
8. [N4]: 7-day trial best, +5.59% subscriptions. Check the sample size and whether it was published in a journal.
9. U-S9: the FTC 2024 investment-scam loss of $5.7B, which was never opened.
10. F-C9: link posts were 1.1% of feed views in Q2 2026 (company transparency report), plus the arXiv 2410.17390 visibility ratios.
11. F-C10: the short-video platform's bio-link threshold of about 1,000 followers plus a business account. Taken from a third-party guide; the official rule is unconfirmed.

#### Sources (new this pass)
- [N1] FINRA Investor Education Foundation research brief, Finfluencer Followers and Social Media Scrollers, April 2026. https://finrafoundation.org/sites/finrafoundation/files/2026-03/FINRA_Foundation_Research_Brief_Social_Media_Finfluencers.pdf (opened: yes, full text extracted)
- [N2] FINRA, Social Media-Influenced Investing, 2025-12-11. https://www.finra.org/rules-guidance/key-topics/fintech/report/social-media-influenced-investing (opened: yes, summary)
- [N3] a national nonpartisan research organization, mobile fact sheet, published 2026-10-19. (URL omitted per T1) (opened: yes)
- [N4] arXiv 2006.13420, field experiment on free-trial length at a SaaS firm, 2020. https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.13420 (opened: yes, abstract)

## E7. Pricing analyst (challenges: data analyst, user researcher (viewers))

### Council pass 2: Pricing analyst, cross-examination

Member: pricing analyst · Pass 2 · 2026-11-01 · Targets: p1-data-analyst.md (DA), p1-user-viewers.md (UV).
Own file: p1-pricing.md (PR). Fetches this pass: 3 (data vendor pricing page, the investor-education foundation brief
[UV S1], the FTC 2024 fraud release [UV S9]). I re-ran `docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/model.mjs`. Nothing here recommends a security or trade.

#### A. Data analyst (p1-data-analyst.md)

**DA-C1 (break-even 1.2% at scale for base, 1.8-5.6% at 1k): STANDS.** I re-ran the model and got the same output. The owner's
"1.7-2.8%" summary describes only the 1k-viewer case. One point is missing: at 1k viewers the base case is "1.5%
short of 2.89%". That gap closes from about 5,400 viewers up. It closes only if conv holds at 1.5%, and PR-C3 argues it will not.

**DA-C3 ("price ... no effect on steady-state break-even"): REFUTED as worded.** The tornado shows a 0.00pp swing for price
because the conservative, base and strong values are all $9.99 and $99.99. Nothing varies, so the swing is zero. It does not
mean price doesn't matter. Break-even conv at scale is variable cost per viewer divided by net revenue per payer, so it moves
roughly in inverse proportion to price. The model also has no price elasticity: conv is fixed whatever the price, which is
the very question F-3 leaves open.
Estimate (arithmetic): the alternative anchor is $4.99/month and $39.99/year at a 35% annual mix. The fixed $0.30 card fee
pushes fees to about 10.1% on $4.99 (2.9% + $0.30/4.99 + 0.7% + 0.5%) and 4.85% on $39.99. Net revenue per payer is then
0.65·4.99·0.899 + 0.35·3.33·0.952 ≈ $4.03. Base break-even at scale rises from 1.18% to 0.104/4.03 ≈ **2.6%**.
Revised claim: price is a first-order input. The dashboard needs a price input with stated scenario values. It should also
carry a conv-by-price assumption until the PR painted-door test supplies one. This also corrects my own PR-C8.1: a lower price
helps only if conversion more than doubles.

**DA-C2 (at most ~8.5 traders per payer): STANDS, and it depends on price.** At scale the condition is
trader_share/conv ≤ net revenue per payer − hosting/conv ≈ 8.8 − 0.27 at the base case. At $4.99 the ceiling falls to about
4 traders per payer (estimate). The ratio is the right lens, and price sets its ceiling.

**DA-C4 (broker $1/user, $100 floor, $0.50 unsupported): STANDS; fact-checked.** I reopened the data vendor's page. It lists
the $100/month platform fee, $1 per connected user on the read-only daily plan and $2 for real-time. Manual sync on the daily
plan costs $0.05 per connection per sync. Volume discounts exist only on the custom plan, with no published rates. Personal
and OAuth users are "free for a limited time", with no end date given. A user with several connections is billed once.
Pricing implication: if the free-user offer covers early traders, the broker term drops out of the early economics. Fixed cost
and conv then dominate, and the $9.99 price matters more, not less.

**DA-C5 (wrong denominators; stock vs flow): STANDS, and it exposes an error in my PR-C3.** I compared a stock
(1.5% of monthly viewers paying) to a flow (10.7% of installs paying within 35 days). My range of 0.1-0.5% was framed against
that mismatch. Revised PR-C3: my range is a **stock** of active payers ÷ monthly unique viewers. That is the model's definition,
and payer build-up over a 10-26 month life is already inside it. On that definition I keep 0.1-0.5% as an estimate. Confidence: Low.

**DA-C9 (trader_share maybe 0.2-0.7%, not 5-15%): WEAKENED but important.** The arithmetic is sound. The inputs, though,
are a week-8 snapshot of a design target, not a steady state, and the gate counts only external-referrer profile views,
not feed sessions. Still, the conflict between DA and PR resolves here.
Estimate: with trader_share at 0.5%, variable cost per viewer is about $0.009 (0.005·$1 + $0.004 hosting). Break-even conv at
scale is then about 0.10% at $9.99 and 0.22% at $4.99. Fixed cost of $150 at conv 0.25% and $9.99 is covered from about 11.5k
viewers (150 / (0.0025·8.8 − 0.009)). My conservative conv would then be viable, but only if trader_share is that low.
Revised: break-even depends on the ratio of conv to trader_share, not on conv alone. Measure trader_share first, as DA says.

**DA-C6 (base churn 7% likely too low): STANDS with a caveat.** The 13.7%/month figure assumes constant hazard. App
retention losses are front-loaded, so churn among survivors after month 3 is probably lower than 13.7%. A no-trial offer
removes the trial drop-off, so first-month churn may be higher still. Net: 7% is optimistic for an add-on to free data, and
10% is a fairer base (assumption).

#### B. User researcher, viewers (p1-user-viewers.md)

**UV-C8 ("base case sits below break-even, 0.2-1.3 points short"): WEAKENED.** It compares base conv to the owner's 1k-viewer
range. Per DA-C1, base conv of 1.5% clears the 1.18% floor beyond about 5.4k viewers. The conclusion may still be right, but
for a different reason: 1.5% itself is likely too high (PR-C3, DA-C5). Revised: "Base conv is unsupported; at a plausible
0.1-0.5%, break-even depends on trader_share being far below the dashboard's range."

**UV-C3 and segment 2 ("verify before I pay someone": the clearest paying job): REFUTED as a paying segment.**
The data are confirmed. The brief reports 7.6 vs 4.0 information sources, 36% vs 14% checking a professional's background,
and 68-69% vs 26-29% losing money among those targeted for fraud. The FTC reports $5.7B in 2024 investment-scam losses,
within $12.5B of total fraud losses. But POLICY F4 and F-3 keep the whole record free. A checker gets the full verification
answer without paying, and the UV-C9 problem (the target is usually absent) applies on top. That job drives traffic and trust,
not revenue. Revised: the only paying segment is UV segment 3 (follower-chooser: compare, saved views, notes, annual plan).
That agrees with PR-C4.

**UV-C1 (social media as a mainstream channel for adults 18-34): STANDS; fact-checked, with a pricing addition.**
The brief confirms 29% overall, 60% among investors aged 18-34 and 9% at 55+, plus 26% and 61% for acting on a personality's
recommendation (2024 investor survey). The same brief also shows reliance falls with portfolio size. It is 48% under $50K
versus 20% at $500K or more. Raw rates also fall with income (32% under $50K vs 20% at $100K or more), though income stops
mattering once portfolio value is controlled for. So the audience that the share-card channel reaches holds smaller
portfolios on average. That is more evidence that $9.99/$99.99 is a ceiling, not a mass price (PR-C9).
Confidence: Medium (an association, not willingness-to-pay data).

**UV-C4 (entertainment and relatability over returns): STANDS, and it strengthens PR-C4.** Entertainment-motivated swipe
viewers are the least likely buyers of a desktop-style comparison tool. Viewer revenue depends on a minority segment
whose size nobody has measured.

**UV-C7 (D7 ≥ 15% plausible only with a reason to return): NEEDS FACT-CHECK.** No benchmark has been opened. PR-C3 used
D7 = 15% as the payer pool, so if D7 is lower, my 0.25% base falls too.

**UV persona P2/P3 (no trial blocks a one-off job; cannot see compare before paying): STANDS as simulation.**
It supports PR-C8.3/C8.4: a free two-trader compare, or a money-back window, as a test arm.

#### C. Conflicts and who is right
- Break-even level: DA is right (1.2% at scale, base). UV and the owner's summary quote the 1k-viewer case.
- Conversion basis: DA is right that the benchmarks are flows. PR's range survives once it is redefined as a stock.
- Paying segment: UV segments 2 and 3 versus PR's "small research segment". PR and UV segment 3 are right, and segment 2 does not pay.
- Price effect: DA's tornado shows no price effect, but that comes from scenario values that never vary. Price roughly
  doubles or halves break-even (PR is right).

#### Items for the fact-checker
1. Data vendor pricing: $100 floor, $1 daily / $2 real-time, $0.05 per manual sync on the daily plan, and how long the
   "personal/OAuth users free for a limited time" offer lasts (DA-C4; reopened by me, no end date shown).
2. The churn benchmark of 3.6% "median": is it monthly or annual (DA-C6, PR-C6)?
3. App report figures: 2.1% freemium vs 10.7% hard-paywall conversion by day 35; 17.0% of monthly and 44.1% of yearly plans
   retained at 12 months; plan mix of 42%/34% globally and 36%/40% in North America; $9.99 monthly and $39.99 yearly medians
   in North America (PR-C1-C3, DA-C5-C7).
4. Newsletter 5-10% free-to-paid applies to email sign-ups; the "1-3% of free readers" in the projection basis is unsourced (DA-C5).
5. Card fees of 2.9% + $0.30, billing 0.7% and tax 0.5% (DA-C8); these drive my $4.99 break-even arithmetic.
6. trader_share: the gate-implied 0.2-0.7% versus the dashboard's 5-15% (DA-C9); measurable today.
7. The CAC source (an ad-network report, HTTP 403, 2022) is unopened (DA-C10).
8. The foundation brief figures are confirmed by me: 29%/60%, 26%/61%, 7.6 vs 4.0, 36% vs 14%, 68-69% vs 26-29%, and
   portfolio <$50K 48% vs ≥$500K 20%. FTC 2024: $12.5B in fraud losses, $5.7B of it investment scams (confirmed). Still
   unopened: the international regulator-body report (UV S8) and the UK regulator figures' underlying survey (UV-C1).
9. No D7 benchmark exists for finance-content apps (UV-C7). My conv estimate depends on it.

## E8. Distribution and growth analyst (challenges: user researcher (traders), competitive B)

### Pass 2: Distribution and growth analyst, cross-examination

Member: distribution and growth analyst. Pass 2, 2026-11-01. Targets: p1-user-traders.md (UT), p1-competitive-social-forums.md (SF).
Fetches used: 5 (FINRA Foundation brief text, arXiv 2410.17390 abstract, Brazil day-trading abstract, FINRA sweep update; one failed render re-read locally).

#### 0. Re-check of my own C1 (repo, read-only, commit 15ca6c3)

- **Referrer-view gate: CONFIRMED unmeasurable.** No code reads the HTTP Referer. The only matches are a `referrerPolicy` attribute on the vendor iframe and `rel="noreferrer"` on links. None of the 21 tables in `db/schema.sql` stores page views. The funnel `landed` event (`lib/auth/attempts.ts:24`) takes its `source` from the CTA `?ref`/surface (`app/auth/actions.ts:59`), not from the Referer, and fires only when someone starts signing up.
- **D7: CONFIRMED for signed-out viewers, PARTLY WRONG for signed-in ones.** `sessions.last_seen_at` refreshes at most hourly (`lib/auth/sessions.ts:46`) but is overwritten each time. That supports a rough "seen 7+ days after first session" proxy, not true day-7 return. `follows.created_at` is a second weak signal. Signed-out viewers leave no trace.
- **Share gate: my pass-1 claim is REFUTED.** Sharing is recorded in `users.share_prompt_action` ('copied'/'shared'), `share_image_saved_at`, `trade_shared_at` and `badge_copied_at`. Despite its name, `share_image_saved_at` is set by the profile "Share my record" button (`app/u/[handle]/share-card.tsx`). The count only includes completed share sheets or clipboard copies; cancelled sheets are excluded (`app/share-button.tsx`). Gaps: the month-recap share button records nothing, and a copy is not the same as a post.
- **Revised C1:** one gate (external-referrer views) cannot be measured at all. D7 can be measured only roughly, and only for signed-in viewers. The share gate can be measured as a proxy: connected users with any of the four columns set.
- **New gap:** the F-1 `declined` funnel event allows no reason key (`lib/funnel.ts` META_KEYS). UT's open question 2 ("why not" on consent) therefore needs a code change first.

#### 1. p1-user-traders.md

**UT-C1 (most records negative or no clear pattern). STANDS.** The Brazil abstract checks out: 97% lost money, 0.4% earned more than a bank teller (US$54/day), among people who started in 2013-2015 and kept going 300+ days. What UT leaves out is the effect on distribution: most cards will carry a thin or negative trust line. Reach will then depend on the minority who share *because* the record is honest (UT H2). That makes the trader mix, not the card, the main growth lever. UT reaches the same conclusion in C9.

**UT-C9 (share rate ≈55%, below the 60% gate). WEAKENED.** The 15% "positive edge" figure comes from a search snippet that UT did not open (UT rates it Low). The 80%/50% split is an assumption. The bigger problem is the measurement: as shown above, the gate would be read from button events. Those overcount (a copy is not a post) and undercount (recap shares, manual URL copies). The 55% and 60% would come from different instruments. Revised claim: the share gate is roughly a coin-flip at these assumptions. It is measurable today as a proxy, and the proxy should be fixed before the gate is read (record the recap button; report copy and share separately).

**UT-C10 (realistic first 50: accountability traders and small transparency sellers; ~100 connect attempts). STANDS, but it conflicts with my C4, and the conflict matters.** UT's cohort (S3, small S1 under ~5k followers, recruited S2) is the trust-friendly one. My C4 arithmetic says the 5,000-views gate needs roughly a quarter of the 50 to be S1 sellers with 10k+ audiences. Both can be right at once, which means **the supply-quality goal and the views gate pull against each other**. A cohort that is safe on trust and law will likely miss the views gate by 3-10x. A cohort that can hit it is the one SF-C7 flags for regulators. The two figures also measure different stages. UT's ~100 counts *connect attempts*. My ~600-1,300 counts *outreach contacts* earlier in the funnel. Both should go into the plan.

**UT-C7 (FINRA Foundation figures). STANDS, verified in the brief.** I checked 29%/60% (18-34), 26%/61%, entertainment 59% vs 18%, social activity 59% vs 11%, and fraud losses 68-69% vs 26-29%. One point no file uses: the brief says investors with *low portfolio value* were more likely to use social media for investment decisions, and that income stops mattering once portfolio value is controlled for. So the audience these channels reach has smaller portfolios, which weighs on paying $9.99 a month (a pricing member's item).

**UT-C6(d) (TR-2 all-accounts rule cuts against the 56% who want per-account control). NEEDS FACT-CHECK.** The source is a 2018 industry-sponsored survey about banking apps. It works as a hypothesis, not as a size. It can be tested only once `declined` accepts a reason (section 0).

#### 2. p1-competitive-social-forums.md

**SF-C2 (channels used by finfluencer followers). STANDS, and it corrects my C6.** Verified: long-form video 77%, photo 51%, general social network 49%, microblog 47%, discussion board 46%, short video 40%, chat server 25%. Across all respondents, long-form video was 30% and discussion boards 15%. In my C6 I used general adult platform use, which is the wrong denominator. SF's investor-specific data is better. Revised implication: the leading channel is long-form video, and its descriptions allow clickable links. "Image apps where links are weak" (my C6, SF-C10) is the second case, not the main one. **SF is right; I withdraw that part of C6.**

**SF-C9 (link suppression). Direction STANDS; size NEEDS FACT-CHECK.** The arXiv abstract says links to external resources lose visibility "by up to a factor of eight" across "over 40 million" posts from 9M+ users, on two political news topics. The 7.5x/4.7x medians are not in the abstract. The design is observational. The 1.1% figure is a share of views, which mixes how few link posts exist with how little reach they get, so it says nothing about click-through on a link. Implication for my C4: my 5-15% "seen" assumption is probably too high for bare link posts. **There is also a measurement trap.** On phones the product already shares the card as an image file plus the URL (`shareMode` 'files'). Many apps keep the image and drop or bury the link. Views that arrive later by typed handle would count as direct, not as external referrals. So the more the product follows SF-C11(a), "images travel", the more the referrer gate undercounts. *Assumption:* some in-app browsers also strip the Referer. A `?via=` parameter on shared URLs, counted first-party, avoids depending on the Referer.

**SF-C10 (bio links gated at ~1,000 followers on short video). NEEDS FACT-CHECK.** The source is a single third-party guide, and the official page did not render. If it holds, small S2 hobbyists may have no clickable slot at all. That supports my C4 that views come from larger accounts.

**SF-C11 (≈100-167 views per trader per week). STANDS as arithmetic, overstated as a typical target.** The even split hides the heavy tail (my C3: most shared links get no clicks). The median sharer will bring far fewer than 100 views, and a handful will bring most of the total. The plan should name those 3-5 traders.

**SF-C7 (EU/UK/US treat monetized posting as recommendation or promotion). STANDS.** The FINRA sweep page confirms 5 formal enforcement actions plus 3 related actions, sweep complete May 2025. I did not verify the $850,000 fine or the ~1,700 influencers. This weakens **my own best channel #1** ("founder-led recruitment of ICP-S1"). Revised: recruit transparency-led traders with existing audiences who do *not* sell signals. Hold paid-signal sellers until the AI legal committee rules (POLICY open item).

**SF-C12 (self-selection: the most-followed creators gain least from a loss-inclusive record). STANDS as a hypothesis.** It agrees with SF-C4 and my C3. Together with UT-C10, it is the strongest reason to expect the views gate to miss.

#### 3. Net conclusion

As built, the week-8 gates are 1 unmeasurable (referrer views), 1 partly measurable (D7, signed-in only), and 2 measurable (traders connected; sharing as a proxy). The views gate is the likeliest to miss, because the cohort that is safe on trust and law has small audiences. Before week 1, the owner needs to (a) add first-party, cookie-free counting of profile views by referrer bucket and `?via=`; (b) decide whether D7 is gated on signed-in viewers; and (c) accept that the cohort mix decides two gates.

#### Items for the fact-checker

1. Trader-skill share (UT-C9 "15% with positive edge"; from a snippet, never opened).
2. SF-C9: the 7.5x/4.7x median visibility gaps in arXiv 2410.17390 (full text), and the 1.1% link-post share of feed views (company report).
3. SF-C10: the short-video platform's official bio-link eligibility (follower threshold, account type, region).
4. SF-C7: the March 2024 FINRA fine of $850,000, ~1,700 paid influencers, 39,400+ accounts.
5. SF-C5: the UK regulator's June 2025 action figures (9 regulators, 50 alerts, 650+ takedown requests, 3 arrests).
6. UT-C6(d): the 2018 fintech-privacy survey (n=1,500, 56% want per-account control); any newer brokerage-specific data.
7. My C3: the 59% of shared news links that are never clicked (primary paper not opened; cited via a blog).
8. My C12: finance-app D7 ≈20% and all-app 10.9% (vendor blog, no stated method).
9. My C5 and owner benchmarks b22 (~$1 per connected trader per month), b27 (~$18 cost per registration), b28 (>80% organic or referral share).
10. SF-C8: the discussion-board operator's Q2 2026 figures (130.3M DAU, $805M revenue).
11. Verified this pass (no further check needed): FINRA Foundation brief figures in UT-C7/SF-C1/SF-C2/SF-C3; Brazil 97% / 0.4% / US$54; FINRA sweep 5+3, complete May 2025.

## E9. Regulatory-market analyst (challenges: red team, competitive C)

### Pass 2: Regulatory-market analyst, cross-examination

**Member:** regulatory-market analyst. **Pass:** 2, 2026-11-01. **Targets:** p1-red-team.md (RT), p1-competitive-broker-trackers.md (BT). These are findings, not legal advice. My pass-1 sources are cited as [R#], and the new pages I opened in pass 2 as [N#].

#### A. Red Team (p1-red-team.md)

**RT-C7 (legal tail: records work as adviser ads and de facto signals). Verdict: WEAKENED. The risk is real, but most of it falls on the trader who posts, not on the platform.**
- The facts check out. Rel. 2023-173 charged nine advisers, with penalties of $850K in total ($50K-$175K each), for putting hypothetical performance on websites for "mass audiences" without the required policies. The release does not allege that any figure was false [N1].
- The rule mechanics are overstated. Staff FAQ guidance (updated Mar. 2025) lets extracted performance be shown over a "single, clearly disclosed period" when total-portfolio gross and net performance appear with equal prominence. The 1/5/10-year periods apply to the total-portfolio figures, not to the extract itself [N2]. So a share card posted by an adviser is likely non-compliant, but for a fixable reason: total-portfolio context is missing. It is not non-compliant by its nature.
- Liability sits with the adviser who uses the card as an advertisement. The platform becomes exposed only if it is paid by advisers or helps prepare their ads, in which case its rankings could become a "third-party rating" with compensation to disclose ([R16], my C6). POLICY F5 already rules that out.
- On scalping (Lit. Rel. 25591): the charged conduct was *recommending* a stock while secretly planning to sell it. A delayed, broker-sourced record of holdings and exits makes that conduct visible after the fact. It does not supply it. The residual risk is a trader who adds their own buy calls in theses (F-5 moderation).
- Revised claim: "Advisers and registered representatives who post cards likely create non-compliant ads unless they add total-portfolio context. The platform stays a disinterested publisher while it takes no adviser money and adds no picks." Medium.

**RT-C8 (vendor terms conflict with public display). Verdict: WEAKENED from "High that the conflict exists" to "High that the terms are ambiguous".**
- The Application Compliance Policy says "the user owns their account data and can choose with whom and how to share it." It requires analysis to be pull-based. It says nothing about public display [N3].
- The "re-package" clause cuts the other way. BT-C11 sets out both readings, and BT is the more accurate of the two files here.
- The §7 market-data clause is the sharper point. Market data "must be used only for the user" [N3]. Current values of open positions shown to strangers depend on vendor market data, so F-6's open-position percentages are the exposed part. Closed-trade percentages are not.
- Correction: RT says it is "an open question" whether brokerage accounts fall under the federal data-access rule. It is not. The 2024 final rule declined to cover investment and retirement accounts and deferred them to future rulemaking [R18]. Today no federal right to brokerage data exists, so vendor pricing and access sit entirely under contract (my C7). That strengthens RT's cost-risk point but removes the 1033 fee route as the mechanism behind it.

**RT-C10 (cycle timing, social media as information source). Verdict: NEEDS FACT-CHECK (figures conflict between sources).**
- The FINRA Foundation release confirms 8% new investors in 2024 against 21% in 2021, and non-retirement ownership of 21% against 26%. It says "young adults" and does not give "aged 18-34" [N4].
- The same release puts social-media reliance at **29%** of investors [N4]. The Dec. 2025 FINRA report gives **24%** overall and 35% for respondents aged 18-29, citing a 2025 study [N5]. These are different surveys, so the council must not mix them.
- RT also says FINRA "is asking whether existing rules cover" this area. The report page shows only a general request for comment and "potential regulatory considerations" [N5]. Revise to: "FINRA has invited comment." RT-C10's conclusion stands; its numbers need relabelling.

**RT-C3 (signals sellers lose by connecting). Verdict: STANDS, with a regulatory counterweight.** The incentive argument holds. RT leaves out the regulatory trend, though. The IAC has recommended disclosure duties for finfluencers and a misconduct database [R2]. ESMA has floated "transparency of previous recommendations" [R9]. FINRA's RN 26-14 treats finfluencer involvement as a supervisory risk factor [R13]. If any of these becomes law, a checkable record shifts from a liability for sellers to a cost of doing business for them. None of it is law yet, so this is upside and not part of the base case. Low-Medium.

**RT-C4/C5 (unit economics).** This is outside my field. One input is mine: the $1 per trader cost is an unregulated contract price that SnapTrade can change on 30 days' notice (RT-C8). Sensitivity runs should use $2 as the stress case. The text is ambiguous on what triggers that price, so it is not a forecast.

#### B. Broker trackers (p1-competitive-broker-trackers.md)

**BT-C1 (app broker's verified feed; "adding public web profiles would be quick"). Verdict: WEAKENED on speed.** The constraint is regulatory, not engineering. Public-web content showing customers' realized P&L is the broker-dealer's own communication under FINRA's communications rules. FINRA ties the firm to any post it "arranged for" [R10]. Its influencer sweep produced eight formal actions [R11]. RN 26-14 adds finfluencer risk to supervision [R13]. Opening the feed to signed-out visitors widens what the firm must supervise and keep records of. That supports my C5: an independent publisher that takes no broker money avoids those costs. Revised claim: "Fast to build, slow to clear through compliance." Medium.

**BT-C2 (copy-trading broker pays traders 1.5% of assets under copy; no US entity listed). Verdict: STANDS, and cuts in our favour in the US.** Pay tied to copiers' assets is "any economic benefit" for advice-like activity [R2], which likely explains why no US entity is listed. That model is hard to bring into the US. Stock Social's unpaid-trader rule (F5) is the US-compatible alternative, not just a gap. Medium.

**BT-C5 (social apps at $8-$20 with 13+ age ratings). Verdict: STANDS on prices, WEAKENED on the implication.** An 18+ rule is a differentiator, but it is not free. After No. 23-1122 and No. 25A97, age-assurance mandates are more likely to hold up [R23][R24]. Attestation-only gating may not satisfy state social-media laws (my C9). The 18+ rule should be costed as a per-signup expense that rivals rated 13+ face too. Note also that BT's price comparison sets trader-side and member subscriptions against a viewer-only tool, so the "bracket $9.99" framing is approximate.

**BT-C11 (deletion on request vs. freezing records on disconnect). Verdict: STANDS, and broader than BT says.** Deletion duties come from state consumer-privacy laws as well as SnapTrade's terms. A trader's request to delete must override TR-5's freeze. Whether a given law applies depends on revenue and volume thresholds, which need checking (see below). Product consequence: a freeze holds the record at disconnect, but deleting it remains the trader's choice. That reopens RT-C2's selection channel (traders delete red records). Medium-High.

**BT-C12 (polling guidance vs. hourly sync). Verdict: STANDS as a platform-terms risk (my reading of the code is light).** In `lib/sync.ts`, users are picked by `last_synced_at` oldest-first under a lease. I saw no per-account 24-hour throttle on activity calls. Going over the vendor's launch-guide limits is a way to lose access (the vendor may end access "for any reason", BT-C11 [S20][S21]). Engineering should confirm.

#### C. Conflicts between files
- **1033 scope:** RT treats it as open; my file says it is settled for now (excluded). **Mine is correct** [R18]. BT does not address it.
- **Vendor terms:** RT says "High conflict"; BT says "ambiguous, ask in writing". **BT is correct.** Both agree that a written answer is the gate before launch.
- **Broker social as a threat:** BT treats it as fast; my C5 treats broker social features as heavy on compliance. Both hold: they would be quick to build and slow to take public.

#### Items for the fact-checker
1. Rel. 2023-173: nine advisers, $850K, no allegation that figures were false (checked [N1]; confirm the per-firm range).
2. Social-media reliance: 24% (FINRA report, Dec. 2025) against 29% (Foundation release, Dec. 2025). Name the survey behind each; 35% is for investors aged 18-29 only.
3. 8%/21% new investors and 26%→21% ownership: confirm the age band for "young adults".
4. CFPB 1033 final rule: investment accounts excluded; stay dated 29 Oct. 2025; no final reconsideration action in the Spring 2026 agenda.
5. SnapTrade pricing: $1 daily / $2 real-time per connected user, $100/month platform fee, $0.05 per manual sync, $0 personal/OAuth "limited time"; 30-day price-change notice (§12).
6. SnapTrade Developer Terms §2 "re-package" and §7 market-data clauses, quoted verbatim, and whether any public-display permission exists in writing.
7. The app broker's feed being "live for everyone" eligible in the US, and the 28.4M funded customers (trade press only).
8. The copy-trading broker's 1.5% AUC payout and the absence of a US entity.
9. FINRA RN 26-14 (9 Jul. 2026) finfluencer risk factor; SEC approval Rel. 34-106675 (15 Oct. 2026).
10. The marketing-rule FAQ on extracted performance (Mar. 2025 update), where the single-period relief is set out.
11. Kill-gate arithmetic (RT-C9): about 28% pass probability for a true 55% sharer base at n=50 (I recomputed: z=(29.5-27.5)/3.52≈0.57, about 28%; confirm).
12. State privacy-law deletion thresholds that would apply to a small, adults-only publisher.
13. The 2021 SEC staff report statement that brokers let customers display trades (RT [S18]).
14. ESMA MiCA-review response (30 Sep. 2026), the "transparency of previous recommendations" wording.

#### Sources opened in pass 2 (2026-11-01)
- [N1] SEC Press Release 2023-173, sec.gov/newsroom/press-releases/2023-173. opened: yes
- [N2] SEC staff Marketing Rule FAQ, sec.gov/investment/marketing-faq. opened: yes
- [N3] SnapTrade Application Compliance Policy, snaptrade.com/compliance-policy. opened: yes
- [N4] FINRA Foundation release, shifting investor behaviors, 4 Dec. 2025, finra.org/media-center/newsreleases/2025/new-finra-foundation-research-examines-shifting-investor-behaviors. opened: yes
- [N5] FINRA report, social-media-influenced investing, 11 Dec. 2025, finra.org/rules-guidance/key-topics/fintech/report/social-media-influenced-investing. opened: yes
- [R#] Pass-1 sources in p1-regulatory.md.

## E10. Data analyst (projection dashboard) (challenges: pricing, market sizing)

### Council pass 2: Data analyst cross-examination

Member: data analyst · Pass 2 · 2026-11-01 · Targets: p1-pricing.md, p1-market-sizing.md
Re-run script: `scratchpad/p2da/run.mjs`. It uses the same steady-state model as pass 1. Fees are computed per plan (2.9% + $0.30 card, 0.7% billing, 0.5% tax), with $150 fixed cost, $4 hosting per 1k viewers and $1 per connected trader. No web fetches were needed: every new number below is arithmetic on figures already in the three files.

#### Re-run: pricing analyst's conversion (0.1/0.25/0.5%) × two price points × kill-gate trader ratio

**Kill-gate trader_share (estimate).** The gates call for 50 traders and 5,000 external views a week, which is 21,667 views a month. If each unique viewer makes 3, 2 or 1 views, that is 7,222, 10,833 or 21,667 uniques. trader_share is then **0.69% / 0.46% / 0.23%**, or 144–433 viewers per trader. The dashboard assumes 5–15%, or 7–20 viewers per trader.

Net revenue per payer per month at 35% annual: **$8.82** at $9.99/$99.99 and **$4.03** at $4.99/$39.99. At the lower price the fixed $0.30 card fee pushes the effective fee to 8.7%, above the dashboard's 6.5%.

Monthly unique viewers needed to break even (∞ means never break even at any scale):

| Price | trader_share | Floor conv (V→∞) | Conv to break even at gate traffic | V* @0.1% | V* @0.25% | V* @0.5% |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| $9.99/$99.99 | 0.69% (gate-low) | 0.12% | 0.36% | ∞ | 13.5k | 4.5k |
| | 0.46% (gate-mid) | 0.10% | 0.25% | ~730k | 11.2k | 4.2k |
| | 0.23% (gate-high) | 0.07% | 0.15% | 60k | 9.5k | 4.0k |
| | 10% (dashboard base) | 1.18% | 4.6% | ∞ | ∞ | ∞ |
| $4.99/$39.99 | 0.69% | 0.27% | 0.79% | ∞ | ∞ | 16.3k |
| | 0.46% | 0.21% | 0.56% | ∞ | 104k | 13.0k |
| | 0.23% | 0.16% | 0.33% | ∞ | 40k | 10.9k |
| | 10% | 2.58% | 10.0% | ∞ | ∞ | ∞ |

Monthly profit at gate traffic, $9.99, gate-mid ratio: −$148, −$4 and +$234 at 0.1%, 0.25% and 0.5% conversion. At $4.99 the same cells come to −$200, −$134 and −$25.

**Findings.**
1. **The trader ratio decides the verdict, not conversion alone.** At the dashboard's 10% ratio, no conversion in the 0.1–0.5% range ever breaks even at either price. At the gate-implied ratio, $9.99 breaks even at 0.25% with about 10–13k monthly uniques, which is the gate's own traffic. Broker cost then shrinks to about $0.005–0.007 per viewer, and the $150 fixed cost becomes the main cost.
2. **$4.99/$39.99 more than doubles the conversion needed** (×2.19, the ratio of net revenue per payer: 8.82 ÷ 4.03). At 0.1% conversion it never breaks even, even with zero broker cost, because $4 hosting per 1k viewers ÷ 0.1% = $4.00 per payer, which equals the $4.03 net. To match $9.99 revenue, the lower price needs conversion to rise ≥2.2×.
3. **Capacity limit.** At $9.99, a payer can carry at most **4.8 / 7.2 / 8.0 connected traders** at 0.1/0.25/0.5% conversion. At $4.99 the limit is **0.0 / 2.4 / 3.2**. The gate ratio needs 0.9–2.8 traders per payer at 0.25% (0.46% ÷ 0.25% = 1.8 at gate-mid). That fits at $9.99 and is marginal at $4.99.
4. If the vendor's "$0 for personal/OAuth users" offer applies, break-even at $9.99 falls to 31k / 8.3k / 3.7k viewers.

#### Target 1: p1-pricing.md

**P-C3, base conversion 1.5% is "3–15× too high"; proposed range 0.1–0.5%.** The direction agrees with my pass-1 C5. The supporting arithmetic is wrong, though. "1.5% ÷ 15% D7 = 10% of returning viewers" treats D7 (a cohort return rate) as the share of monthly uniques who return. Monthly uniques include returners piling up from earlier cohorts, so that share is higher than 15%. Conversion is also a stock in the model and a flow in the benchmarks (my C5). The claim that the base case "does not break even" holds only under the dashboard's trader ratio (table above). **WEAKENED.** Revised: 0.1–0.5% is a reasonable prior with no derivation behind it. At the gate-implied ratio, $9.99 breaks even near 0.25% at gate traffic.

**P-C8.1, $4.99 earns ≈$10.5 vs ≈$22 per 1k viewers at 0.25%.** The $9.99 figure checks: 2.5 × $8.82 = $22.05. The $4.20 net per payer is high. It is $4.03 once the $0.30 fee is counted, which gives $10.1. More important, the claim leaves out that the lower price raises the break-even floor by 2.2× and fails outright at 0.1%. **WEAKENED.** Revised: $4.99 is viable only if conversion more than doubles. Test it as a price arm, not as a fallback.

**P-C2, "yearly plans renew 2.6× better than monthly".** 44.1 ÷ 17.0 = 2.59 is correct arithmetic. But it compares one yearly renewal with twelve monthly renewals over the same year. That is a retention-over-12-months comparison, not a renewal-propensity one. The "$39.99 North America yearly median" is an app-store median across all categories, not a finance-tool median. **WEAKENED.** Revised: yearly plans keep 2.6× more payers at month 12.

**P-C6, 3.6% churn is "most likely monthly".** We agree it is ambiguous. In the model churn affects only LTV, not break-even. **NEEDS FACT-CHECK.**

**P-C9, WTP $3–6/month.** This is a hypothesis with no primary data, and the file says so (Low). **STANDS as a hypothesis.** The painted-door test is the right next step, and the re-run shows why it must measure conversion, not just clicks: $4.99 needs 2.2× the conversion.

#### Target 2: p1-market-sizing.md

**M-C14, SOM table (year 3 base: 10,000 traders → 100k viewers → 1,500 payers, $169k gross; broker $120k).** It uses the dashboard's 10% trader_share and 1.5% conversion. Under the gate-implied ratio (144–433 viewers per trader), 10,000 traders bring 1.4M–4.3M viewers, not 100k. At the pricing analyst's 0.25% that is 3.6k–10.8k payers, $381k–$1.15M net a year, and **$190k–$816k profit a year** (estimate, gate-mid: 2.17M viewers, 5.4k payers, $574k net, $348k profit). The gate ratio is a design target for week 8, though, not a measured scale ratio. **WEAKENED.** Revised: SOM can swing by more than 10× on viewers per trader alone, so the table should show that axis, not trader count.

**M-C14, "1.2% variable-only break-even is consistent with the owner's 1.7–2.8%".** That consistency holds only at about 1k viewers (my C1), and the 1.2% floor exists only at the dashboard ratio. At the gate ratio the floor is 0.07–0.12%. **WEAKENED.**

**M-C15, viewers per trader is "the single largest sensitivity".** On the dashboard's ranges my tornado ranks conversion first, broker fee second and trader_share third. That ranking reflects the narrow assumed range for trader_share. Across the plausible range, from 0.23% to 15% (65×), trader_share moves break-even from 0.07% to 1.75%, more than any other input. **STANDS**, with the reason corrected.

**M-C12, $8.80 net per payer per month.** It matches mine ($8.80–8.82). **STANDS.**

**M-C7, paper-trading brokerages would show as Verified.** I checked the code. `lib/sync.ts` excludes and counts accounts carrying the vendor's `is_paper` flag (lines 40–42, 88, 112, 183, 316), and `lib/broker.ts:88` maps it. The risk remains only if the vendor does not set `is_paper` on all three simulated brokerages. **WEAKENED.** Revised: confirm the flag per brokerage, or add a brokerage deny-list.

#### Conflicts between files

- **Base conversion.** Pricing proposes 0.1–0.5%. Market sizing and the dashboard use 0.5/1.5/3%. My pass 1 said the denominator is wrong but gave no value. Pricing's range is the better prior for signed-out share-link viewers.
- **Trader ratio.** Market sizing and the dashboard use 5–15%. My C9 gate estimate is 0.2–0.7%. Neither is measured. The gate figure comes from the repo's own targets, so it should at least appear as a scenario. Today's verdict rests on this disagreement.
- **Hosting at low conversion.** None of the three files flagged that at 0.1% conversion, $4 per 1k viewers costs $4 per payer. That sinks $4.99 by itself. My pass 1 noted the first ~33k viewers sit inside the included transfer, which softens the effect early.

#### Items for the fact-checker

1. Data vendor pricing: $100/month floor, $1 per user per month (daily plan), $0.05 per manual sync, the "$0 personal/OAuth, limited time" offer and its end date (my S1).
2. Whether the vendor sets `is_paper` on each of the 3 simulated brokerages in its 38-entry list (M-C5/C7).
3. App report figures: 10.7% hard-paywall vs 2.1% freemium day-35 conversion; 44.1% yearly and 17.0% monthly 12-month retention; NA medians of $9.99 monthly and $39.99 yearly; plan mix 42/34 and 36/40 NA; retention by price tier 53.7/48.3 and 22.5/16.4/12.2 (P-C2, P-C6; my C6/C7).
4. Billing vendor churn page: is 3.6% monthly or annual?
5. Card fee schedule: 2.9% + $0.30, 0.7% billing, 0.5% tax (sets the $4.03 vs $4.20 net).
6. Hosting pricing: 1 TB included, $0.15/GB, and ~30 MB per viewer (an assumption; measure the real page weight).
7. FINRA Foundation NFCS: 34% hold non-retirement securities, 36% trade 4+/yr, 26% act on finfluencer recommendations; Census 267M adults (M-C1, C2, C10).
8. Kill-gate arithmetic: 5,000 views/week ↔ 21,667/month and the 1–3 views per unique assumption (DESIGN §3.5). Measure it from referrer logs once traffic exists.

## E11. Red team (challenges: regulatory, competitive A)

### Pass 2: Red Team cross-examination

Member: Red Team. Pass: 2, 2026-11-01. Targets: p1-regulatory.md (REG) and p1-competitive-copy-signals.md (CCS).
Own file: p1-red-team.md (RT). This is market research, not legal advice. New source: [N1] the US Reports text of 472 U.S. 181 (1985), govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-472/pdf/USREPORTS-472-181.pdf, opened: yes (text extracted). [N2] SEC interpretive release IA-1092 (1987), sec.gov/rules/interp/1987/ia-1092.pdf, opened: yes, but the compensation passage did not extract legibly.

#### A. p1-regulatory.md

**REG-C1 (a paid pull-only record lookup "fits the protected side" of the publisher's exclusion). Verdict: WEAKENED.**
- What holds. I read the opinion [N1]. It does protect "factual information about past transactions" (at 210), and footnote 57 leans on the absence of "individualized, investment-related interactions." Stock Social's own aggregate pages fit this well. I concede that point to REG.
- Gap 1: whose content it is. The opinion's "bona fide" test asks whether a publication is "disinterested" and "published by those engaged solely in the publishing business." It contrasts that with material "designed to tout any security in which petitioners had an interest" (at 209). Stock Social's content is not disinterested editorial. It is the traders' own records, theses and open positions with symbols (TR-4, F-6), posted by people who hold those securities and may sell paid groups (CCS-C12a). The holding was about a publisher's own newsletter. A platform that hosts interested parties' live positions is a step outside it.
- Gap 2: timing. "Regular" in the opinion means "not timed to specific market activity" (at 209). Records refresh hourly when trades close. A paid "saved view" of chosen traders is therefore a lookup timed to market activity, even though nothing is pushed. F3b removes the push but not the timing.
- Gap 3: it protects only the platform. The exclusion covers Stock Social under the Advisers Act. It does not cover traders, antifraud and manipulation law (the opinion itself points to mail fraud and Rule 10b-5, at 209-210 n. 58), CEA-covered futures and crypto, or the UK and EU (REG-C10).
- Gap 4: personalization. F1a orders records per viewer based on that viewer's behavior. REG flags this only as "the edge to watch." It is the closest thing in the product to footnote 57's "individualized" interactions.
- Revised claim: the exclusion plausibly covers Stock Social's publication of finished records. It is weaker for live open positions, per-viewer ordering and paid watch-style views, and it does not reach traders. Confidence: Medium on the facts, Low on the legal outcome. Counsel is still needed (RT-C7).

**REG-C2 (F5 "removes the compensation element at the trader level"). Verdict: REFUTED as stated.**
- Compensation is "any economic benefit" from anyone (REG's own [S2]; [N2] is consistent but did not extract legibly). The traders most likely to connect, ICP-S1, are paid elsewhere through groups, ads and platform creator funds. F5 only means Stock Social adds no new compensation.
- The mirror point matters more. Stock Social itself takes compensation, $9.99 a month for Record tools, so it depends entirely on the exclusion.
- Revised claim: F5 avoids adding compensation. It removes none of the compensation that already exists. Confidence: High.

**REG-C3 (enforcement "hits misconduct that verified records rule out" and "supplies the demand thesis"). Verdict: WEAKENED.**
- Verified records rule out invented performance (REG [S6]). They do not rule out scalping. In the 2022 case (REG [S3], RT [S13]) the positions were real, and the fraud was trading against followers. Real, timestamped open positions with a "Verified" badge make such a promoter more credible, not less.
- On demand: the victims paid "hundreds or thousands" of dollars without any verification (CCS [S15]). That is evidence of distrust among regulators, not of viewers being willing to pay to check. No source in any of the three files measures viewer willingness to pay for verification.
- Revised claim: enforcement shows supply-side fraud. It neither proves nor disproves demand. Confidence: Medium.

**REG-C7 (1033 status; "rivals face the same contracts"). Verdict: the rule status STANDS; the implication is REFUTED.**
- The rule status agrees with RT-C8 and RT [S17].
- The implication does not hold. The strongest rival, a commission-free app broker's verified in-app feed (p1-competitive-broker-trackers C1), reads its own ledger at zero aggregator cost. Stock Social pays about $1 per trader per month (RT-C5). The cost asymmetry runs against Stock Social.

**Where REG strengthens RT.** C9 (age-assurance costs could suppress the 60% share gate) and C10 (UK s21, where a disclaimer "will not protect you") add to the legal tail in RT-C7. RT should add both.

#### B. p1-competitive-copy-signals.md

**CCS-C11 (gap filled: portable, read-only, whole-record, percent-only). Verdict: WEAKENED, close to REFUTED on (a) and (b).**
- The council's own file p1-competitive-broker-trackers contradicts (a) and (b):
  - a verified in-app trading feed now live for all eligible US customers of a broker with 28.4M funded customers (its C1);
  - broker-native verified P&L share links, and a white-label verified-holdings layer (its C3);
  - multi-broker social trackers with "verified" portfolios at $8.99 a month (its C5);
  - a forex verified-track-record category running since 2009 (its C8), which checks accounts held at many brokers.
- What is left is (c) and (d): whole-record display, losses ranked like gains, intervals and percent-only. These are policy choices, not capabilities. A gap that has stayed open for 17 years in the closest analogue can mean there is no demand, not an opportunity (RT-C1/C2).
- Revised claim: the only open gap is the trust package. Its value to viewers and traders is untested. Confidence: Medium.

**CCS-C13 (incumbents' incentives cut against a portable record; the likeliest fast follower is a signal or strategy marketplace). Verdict: REFUTED in part.**
- A single-broker incumbent has already shipped verified public-ish records. The fastest serious multi-broker entrant is trade-journal software, which already has sync, a P&L engine and paying traders (broker-trackers C6).
- Incentives keep incumbents away from whole-record rules. They do not keep them away from the feature that viewers actually see.

**CCS-C10 ($9.99 "sits at the mirroring-app price point"). Verdict: WEAKENED as a price anchor.**
- The app at $10 sells an action: automatic portfolio mirroring through a licensed broker (CCS [S9]). Stock Social sells compare, saved views and notes on top of records that stay free (RT-C4).
- The price is the same but the job is smaller. With no free trial, the anchor overstates willingness to pay. A multi-broker tracker at $8.99 is a closer comparison.

**CCS-C1/C2 (paid-trader share about 0.1% of funded accounts; admission screens select on outcomes). Verdict: STANDS, and it supports RT-C1/C5.**
- Even with pay of about 1.5% of copied assets a year, only about 0.1% of accounts take part. The incumbent also keeps only screened, outcome-positive traders.
- Inference, Low: an unpaid, status-only offer that shows the whole record starts from a smaller base. No number can be derived from this.

**CCS-C12 (ICP-S1 sells forward alerts; "Verified" can be borrowed as marketing). Verdict: STANDS.** It agrees with RT-C3 and is the strongest point in CCS.

#### Conflicts
REG-C1 vs RT-C7: both partly right (platform's finished records vs open positions and traders). CCS-C11 and REG-C7 vs broker-trackers C1/C3/C5/C8: broker-trackers is right.

#### Items for the fact-checker
1. Exact opinion text in 472 U.S. 181 at 206-210: "disinterested," "timed to specific market activity," "entirely impersonal," and footnote 57. I verified these [N1]. Re-check any quote REG adds beyond them.
2. The SEC's "any economic benefit" reading of compensation: the IA-1092 text, and the IAC draft of 22 Nov 2024 (REG S2).
3. The "about $1 per connected user per month plus $100 a month" daily plan, and $0.05 per manual sync (SnapTrade pricing; RT S12, CCS S20).
4. The commission-free app broker's verified feed being "live for everyone" and its 28.4M funded customers (broker-trackers S1/S2, trade press). Also whether profiles can be seen on the open web.
5. The large copy broker's "over 3,300" paid traders (about 0.1%) and "over 5,000" in FY2025, and the 1.5% AUC rate (CCS S1-S3).
6. The SEC 2022-221 facts: eight people, about $100M, real positions (RT S13, REG S3/S4; REG marked S4 "not opened").
7. ESMA's 30 Sep 2026 MiCA response wording, "transparency of previous recommendations may also be explored" (REG S9).
8. The FINRA podcast figures: about 70% non-compliant and 55% undisclosed ads (REG S12; a podcast, not a report).
9. The status of the state age-verification laws (REG C9, Low) and No. 25A97.
10. The base-case break-even of about 1.2% (RT-C4) against the owner's 1.7-2.8%, recomputed from docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json.
