# Part F. Pass 1: desk research

## F1. Market sizing analyst

### Market sizing: Pass 1 (independent desk research)

**Member:** Market Sizing Analyst. **Pass:** 1. **Date:** 2026-11-01.
**Method:** I opened every source with WebFetch, or with the vendor's public API, and give each a source id below.
Every derived number shows its arithmetic and is labelled *estimate* or *assumption*. No company is named. I searched
for company investor releases with account counts (a large commission-free app broker, a large discount broker), but
they would not open (403/402 responses), so **no company account figure is used below**.

Shared base: **US adults ≈ 267M** (estimate). This is the Census 2024 total population (61.2M aged 65+ = 18.0% → ≈340M)
minus 73.1M children [S4].

---

#### (a) US adults who trade stocks/ETFs in self-directed accounts

**C1. About a third of US adults hold securities outside retirement accounts: ≈91–94M people.**
34% of adults hold stocks, bonds, mutual funds or other securities outside retirement accounts (2024 NFCS state survey,
n=25,539) [S1]. The Federal Reserve's SHED (fielded Oct 2024) gives 35% for "stocks, bonds, ETFs, or mutual funds held
outside a retirement account". By age: 17% at 18–24, 31% at 25–54, 41% at 55–64 and 48% at 65+ [S3].
267M × 34% = 90.8M; × 35% = 93.5M. The share was flat compared with 2021 [S1]. Confidence: **High**.

**C2. Roughly a third of those investors trade 4 or more times a year; neither survey publishes a monthly cut.**
Among non-retirement investors, trades in the past 12 months were: none 25%, 1–3 times 31%, 4+ times 36%. These shares
are stable across 2015–2024 [S1]. The 4+ rate is 43% for men and 26% for women. It fell 9 points among investors aged 18-34 from
2021 to 2024 [S1]. 90.8M × 36% = **32.7M adults trading 4+ times/year**. Confidence: **High** for the share; the count is
an estimate.

**C3. Trading is mostly self-directed.**
Trade execution methods (multiple answers allowed): website 62%, mobile app 46%, a specific financial professional 45%,
any representative at the firm 39% [S1]. Mobile-app use is 80% among investors aged 18-34 and 19% at 55+ [S1].
46% of SHED adults are "mostly or very comfortable" choosing and managing their own investments [S3].
Confidence: **Medium**. The survey does not publish the union of website-or-app users.

**C4. Size of the monthly-or-more self-directed trader pool (estimate).**

| | Low | Base | High |
|---|---|---|---|
| Investors (C1) | 90.8M | 92M | 93.5M |
| × trade 4+/yr (C2) | 36% | 36% | 36% |
| × self-directed among 4+ traders (*assumption*, C3 floor 62%) | 75% | 82% | 90% |
| × trade ≥12/yr among 4+ traders (*assumption*: no published cut) | 35% | 50% | 65% |
| **= monthly+ self-directed traders** | **8.6M** | **13.6M** | **19.7M** |

All self-directed active traders (4+/yr) come to 24.5M–30.3M. Confidence: **Low–Medium**. The last row rests on an
assumption, and the NFCS questionnaire item that would replace it (B3, trade count) is in the public microdata [S1].

#### (b) Subset reachable through SnapTrade-supported brokers

**C5. SnapTrade's public brokerage list has 38 enabled entries, and all of them allow read-only connections** [S5][S6].
By my own classification of the entry descriptions:
- about 16 serve US self-directed stock/ETF accounts, including most large US discount, app-first and bank-affiliated
  brokers;
- 6 are Canadian, 2 Australian, 2 Indian, 2 UK, 2 continental EU, and 3 are crypto exchanges;
- 1 is a US retirement-plan recordkeeper;
- **3 are simulated or paper-trading accounts.**

I could not confirm coverage of every US account holder. Notable gaps by category: a large bank-owned full-service
brokerage, a payments-app investing feature, and micro-investing/robo apps. Confidence: **Medium** for the list;
**Low** for the coverage share.

**C6. Reachable monthly+ traders: 6.0M / 11.6M / 18.1M (estimate).**
C4 × assumed account coverage of 70% / 85% / 92%: 8.6M × 0.70 = 6.0M; 13.6M × 0.85 = 11.6M; 19.7M × 0.92 = 18.1M.
Two further cuts apply:
- Options are skipped by the sync (repo known limit), and 21% of investors have traded options [S1].
- The 4+ trading rate is highest at 18–34 (47%→38%) [S1], which matches the product's adult ICPs.

Confidence: **Low**.

**C7. Product risk found while sizing: paper-trading entries.**
Three listed entries are simulated or paper-trading brokerages [S5]. Under the current rule ("Verified = every source
activity has source='snaptrade'"), simulated trades would display as Verified. This needs an allow-list or exclusion by
brokerage before real users arrive. Confidence: **High** that the entries exist; whether the app excludes them was not
checked in code.

#### (c) Creator traders (supply candidates)

**C8. No regulator or academic count exists of people who publicly post their own trades or P&L, or who sell paid
groups.** I found only bounds:
- One academic study examined about **29,000 "finfluencers"** posting 72M messages on a single investor microblog.
  It classified 28% as skilled; the majority were antiskilled (−2.3% monthly abnormal returns) and attracted *more*
  followers [S8].
- A US regulator's investor alert (Dec 2025) and enforcement action describe group-chat schemes that promote stock picks.
  I saw these only as search summaries (page returned 403), so they are **not used as evidence**.
- In the UK, 11% of trading-app users have used social or copy-trading features [S9].

Confidence: **Medium** on the bounds themselves.

**C9. Supply estimate (all *estimates/assumptions*).**
- US adults who publicly post their own trades or P&L at least monthly:
  - **low 20k** (about one microblog's study population [S8]);
  - **base 75k** (≈0.3% of 24.5M self-directed 4+ traders);
  - **high 250k** (≈1% of the same pool).
- Paid-group or signal sellers among them (assumption 5–10%): **≈1k / 5k / 25k**.

Confidence: **Low**. The week-8 gate needs only **≥50 connected traders**, so supply size is not binding. Conversion of
creators willing to expose losses is the open variable, and the study in [S8] suggests the most-followed posters have
the most to lose from a full record.

#### (d) Viewers: adults consuming trading/investing content on social/video platforms

**C10. Among investors, about a quarter to a third use social media for investment information.**
- 29% use social media groups or message boards where people post ideas.
- 30% use a large video platform; 61% of investors aged 18-34 do.
- 26% act on finfluencer recommendations (22% sometimes, 4% frequently); 61% of investors aged 18-34 do.

[S1] (n=2,861, 2024). 90.8M × 26–30% = **23.6M–27.2M investor-viewers**. Platform reach is not the constraint: 84% of US
adults use the largest video platform, and 95% of 18–29s do (n=5,511, 2026) [S7]. Confidence: **High** for the shares;
"monthly" frequency is not measured.

**C11. Monthly viewers of trading content: 20M / 30M / 45M (estimate).**
- Low: 23.6M × 85% assumed monthly = 20M.
- Base: 27.2M investor-viewers + 3M non-investor adults (assumption) ≈ 30M.
- High: adds a larger curious non-investor group, mostly 18–34 adults (assumption) = 45M.

Confidence: **Low**.

#### (e) SAM / SOM for Record tools ($9.99/mo, $99.99/yr, no trial)

**C12. Blended revenue per paying viewer ≈ $113/yr gross, ≈ $105 net of billing (estimate).**
With 35% of payers on the annual plan [S12]: 0.65 × $119.88 + 0.35 × $99.99 = $112.9. After 6.5% billing [S12]: $105.6.

**C13. SAM ≈ $0.7B–$1.5B/yr in theory, but it is not a meaningful constraint.**
SAM is defined as monthly trading-content viewers (C11) who are themselves active self-directed traders, the people
with a reason to compare records.
- Assumed overlap of 30% / 40% / 50% of viewers: 20M × 0.3 = 6M; 30M × 0.4 = 12M; 45M × 0.5 = 22.5M.
- Times $112.9: 6M → **$0.68B**; 12M → **$1.35B**; 22.5M → **$2.5B** (high).

Confidence: **Low**. SAM is 3–4 orders of magnitude above any plausible SOM.

**C14. SOM is set by the product's own funnel, not by the market (estimate).**
Inputs:
- Connected traders T are an *assumption*.
- Viewers = T ÷ trader_share, which is 0.15 / 0.10 / 0.05 [S12].
- Conversion is 0.5% / 1.5% / 3% [S12].
- Revenue = payers × $112.9.

| Year | Traders (L/B/H) | Monthly viewers | Payers | Gross revenue/yr |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 150 / 500 / 2,000 | 1.0k / 5k / 40k | 5 / 75 / 1,200 | $0.6k / $8.5k / $135k |
| 2 | 500 / 3,000 / 15,000 | 3.3k / 30k / 300k | 17 / 450 / 9,000 | $1.9k / $51k / $1.0M |
| 3 | 1,000 / 10,000 / 50,000 | 6.7k / 100k / 1M | 33 / 1,500 / 30,000 | $3.8k / $169k / $3.4M |

In year 3 the base case reaches 1,500 payers, 0.0125% of base SAM. Even the high case is 0.25% of SAM.

Cost check at base: broker data of $1/trader/month [S12] × 10,000 traders = $120k/yr, against $169k gross. At base
inputs, variable cost per viewer is 0.10 × $1 + $0.004 hosting = $0.104/month. Net revenue per payer per month is
$105.6/12 = $8.80. The variable-only break-even is therefore 0.104/8.80 ≈ **1.2% conversion**, before fixed costs and
churn. That is consistent with the owner's stated 1.7–2.8% once those are added. Confidence: **Medium** (arithmetic);
**Low** (inputs).

**C15. The numbers the business actually depends on** (ranked; none is a TAM figure):
1. **Viewers per connected trader** (1/trader_share). This drives the broker-data cost that traders never pay for, and
   it is the single largest sensitivity.
2. **Viewer→payer conversion** with no free trial, against a 1.2% variable-cost floor.
3. **Creator connect rate**: traders who will publish a full record including losses (C8–C9).
4. **External profile views per trader** (week-8 gate: ≥5,000/week).
5. **SnapTrade coverage of those creators' actual brokers** (C5), plus exclusion of paper accounts (C7).

TAM figures (C1–C11) only show that the ceiling is far away. Confidence: **High**.

#### International (later option)

**C16. The UK is smaller and regulated more tightly on promotions.**
- 35% of UK adults hold investments excluding property and real assets (19.0M). 20% (10.0M) hold shares directly.
- **3% (1.6M) use a trading app**; 47% of those users are 18–34, and 11% of users have used social or copy-trading
  features. Non-advised platform use is 11% of adults (2024, n=17,950) [S9].
- FG24/1 says unauthorised influencers who promote regulated products without approval "may be committing a criminal
  offence" [S10].

Confidence: **High** (data); **Medium** on how it would apply to a records product.

**C17. In the EU, the market-abuse framework treats any public post with an opinion on a future price as a possible
"investment recommendation".** Such posts carry disclosure duties, and sanctions for manipulation reach up to EUR 5M for
individuals [S11]. Trader theses would need a review before any EU launch. SnapTrade lists 2 UK and 2 EU brokers, plus
global multi-country brokers [S5]. **Canada (6 entries)** may be the closer adjacent market, but I did not research its
rules. Confidence: **Medium**.

---

- [S1] FINRA Foundation, "Investors in the United States: Results from the National Financial Capability Study" (2024
  investor survey, n=2,861; state survey n=25,539), regulator foundation, Dec 2025.
  https://www.finrafoundation.org/sites/finrafoundation/files/2025-11/NFCS_Investor_Survey_Report_White_Paper.pdf
  (opened: yes, text extracted)
- [S2] FINRA news release on the 2024 investor survey, regulator, Dec 2025.

  agency, Jun 2025. https://www.census.gov/newsroom/press-releases/2025/older-adults-outnumber-children.html (opened: yes)

#### Open questions
1. What share of 4+ traders trade ≥12 times a year? The NFCS microdata (item B3) can answer this; it is the weakest link
   in C4.
2. What share of US self-directed *accounts* do SnapTrade's US brokers hold, given that people hold accounts at several
   firms? A vendor question.
3. Does the sync exclude simulated/paper brokerages (C7)? This is a code check for the engineer.
4. How many creators would connect a full record including losses? This needs real outreach, since the ICP-S1 interviews
   in DESIGN.md are unverified.

6. Canada's rules and market size were not researched (6 supported brokers).

## F2. Competitive analyst A: copy-trading apps and signal sellers

### P1: Competitive landscape A: copy-trading / mirroring and signal sellers / strategy marketplaces

Member: Competitive Landscape Analyst A. Pass 1 (independent desk research), 2026-11-01.
Scope: (1) copy-trading and portfolio-mirroring (social copy-trading brokers, disclosure-mirroring apps, crypto-exchange
copy trading); (2) signal sellers and strategy marketplaces (paid chat groups, strategy subscription marketplaces, forex
signal marketplaces). All products are described generically (POLICY T1/T2/T4). Every number below is from a source I
opened, or it is labelled estimate/assumption.

#### Claims

**C1. In the largest copy-trading model, the platform pays traders, copiers don't, and only a tiny share of users earn.**
The pattern is a large multi-asset social copy-trading broker. Its prospectus (May 2025) says that at 2024-12-31 it had "over 3,300"
paid program traders, "approximately 0.1%" of funded accounts. 12 of them had over $10M copying them. The broker pays them "as an
incentive", from fixed amounts at low tiers up to a percentage of the assets copying them [S1]. Its current program page
lists 1.5% a year of average assets-under-copy (AUC), paid monthly. At the top tier the rate is 0.5% above $20M, and above $100M
it is 0.25%. Payments start at the second tier, which needs $50K AUC and 10 copiers [S2]. Its FY2025 release reports 3.81M funded
accounts, $18.5B assets under administration and "over 5,000" members in its renamed trader program [S3]. *Estimate:* a trader
with $1M AUC earns 1.5% × $1M = about $15,000 a year. One at the $50K entry level earns about $750 a year. Confidence: High
(primary filing and company pages).

**C2. That model verifies only its own ledger, and its paid traders work under exclusivity.** To join, a trader needs a real name,
a face photo, ≥2 months of on-platform history, risk-score ≤7, weekly drawdown no worse than −25%, and ≥30% profitable months.
"Activated exclusivity agreements" are also required for every tier [S2]. The record is verified because the platform executes
the trades itself, so it cannot be carried to another platform. The eligibility screens also select traders on outcomes (profitable-months floor,
drawdown cap). A public record built that way is not the whole of a trader's activity. Confidence: High on the rules; Medium on
what "exclusivity" covers (the page does not define it).

**C3. Copy-trading on one platform has its own documented integrity failures.** The same prospectus discloses that some users misused the paid-trader program "by fraudulently copying each other's positions to increase the fees" (described as
infrequent). It also discloses settlement and market-integrity risk when many users copy one trader, and says regulation of copy
trading is "inconsistent across the jurisdictions" [S1]. A lab experiment found that seeing others' success raises risk taking.
The effect was larger when subjects could copy directly, and the authors conclude copy trading "leads to excessive risk taking"
[S4]. Lab only, not field data. Confidence: High (filing); Medium (single lab study).

**C4. EU regulators treat copy trading as portfolio management or advice, and expect fair, five-year performance display.**
The EU securities regulator's briefing (30 March 2023) says automatic copying "falls under the definition of portfolio
management". Where client action is needed it may be advice or order reception/transmission, and copied traders can be
providing advice. It flags trader remuneration as possible inducements. On past performance it expects fair representation:
not the most prominent element, five preceding years (or full 12-month periods), a clear method, and no simulated past
performance [S5]. This is the regulatory cost a pure record publisher avoids by not copying, paying or signalling (POLICY F1/F5).
Confidence: High.

**C5. Crypto-exchange copy trading pays the lead trader a profit share set by the trader, verified only on that exchange.**
One exchange's rules page states a 10% default profit share that lead traders may raise to 50%. Lead traders need basic
identity verification, assets worth ≥200 units of a dollar stablecoin and ≥1 closed futures position in 30 days [S6]. Other exchanges' terms (other caps,
high-water marks) appeared only in search snippets I could not open (403/404), so I do not cite them. Who pays whom: copiers pay the
lead trader out of profits, and the exchange earns fees on the copied volume (inference from the model, not a sourced figure). Entry
bar is very low, so the record proves little. Confidence: Medium (one exchange opened).

**C6. Disclosure-mirroring apps sell delayed public filings, so their "record" is a lagged reconstruction, not a verified account.**
Members of Congress and senior officials must report transactions within 30 days of notice and no later than 45 days after the
transaction. The penalty for a late report is nominal [S7]. A mirroring app that lets users follow four legislators' filings and fund
13F filings reported more than $55M copied and over $100M total invested (press, updated 2024-03-21) [S8]. A creator- and
disclosure-portfolio copying app, which is a licensed broker-dealer, charged $10/month at about 800K downloads and keeps 25% of
creator management fees [S9]. Benchmark b07 ($29/quarter or $100/year) could not be opened (403) and stays unverified.
Confidence: Medium (press sources).

**C7. Forex signal marketplaces verify one live account through a read-only investor password. The marketplace keeps 20%.**
The largest forex-terminal signal marketplace's rules: real accounts only (demo and contest accounts deleted), seller submits account
number, *investor (read-only) password* and server. Balance, equity, deposits and withdrawals and P/L are shown publicly in real time. Leverage
above 1:500 is excluded. The provider gets the fee "less 20% commission", paid about a week after the period ends. Cancellation
gets no refund [S10]. This is the closest existing analogue to read-only verification. It covers one account on one
terminal, one asset class, and it is built to sell copying. *Estimate:* a $30/month signal yields $24 to the provider. Confidence: High.

**C8. Strategy subscription marketplaces mix hypothetical and live records and keep about 13-30%.** A strategy marketplace's
white-label page offers 70-87% of subscription revenue to the creator if it bills. Alternatively the creator bills directly and pays about $33
per trader per month (its calculator). Strategies "can show detailed hypothetical performance history" next to an "execution
transparency" view across connected accounts, with no verification method stated. Its calculator examples use $99-$997/month
and a $297/month default [S11]. Benchmark b08 ($20-$200/month, ~50% cut) could not be opened (403) and stays unverified.
Confidence: Medium (vendor marketing).

**C9. The paid chat-group signal seller (ICP-S1) is a heavily enforced category. The recurring failure is showing wins and hiding
losses.**
- SEC, Dec 2022: eight people charged over about $100M in alleged fraudulent profits. They bought stocks, then urged followers on a public
  social network and in chat-app trading rooms to buy, and sold into the rise without disclosing plans to sell. Parallel criminal
  charges were filed [S12].
- CFTC complaint, Dec 2025: a crypto "pool" promoter promised 1-3.5% daily returns and pledged to "post all trades" in chat
  channels. Weekly summaries "always posted aggregate profits" while real trading lost. At least $10,146,714 came from at least 3,376
  participants [S13].
- CFTC, Apr 2020: a "live" trading-room subscription took about $1.3M from 300+ members. The court found false and misleading statements. $1.3M restitution
  and a $450K penalty [S14].
- FTC, Mar 2022: a stock-trading subscription service settled for $2.425M. Subscriptions cost "hundreds or thousands of dollars", and it made
  "double or triple" claims while it "did not track subscriber results" [S15]. FTC, Apr 2022: a day-trading education seller paid
  $3M. Its ads featured the founder's results while "the vast majority" of customer accounts lost money [S16].
- CFTC advisory: signal and bot sellers claim "100 percent 'win' rate" and similar. One scheme took over $1.7B from at least 23,000 people [S17].
- UK regulator, Apr 2026: a 17-regulator week of action. In the UK it produced a guilty plea, 120 account takedown requests and 1,267 illegal ads reaching
  at least 2.34M UK accounts [S18]. US self-regulator: 5 formal actions came out of its influencer and customer-acquisition sweep [S19].
Confidence: High (primary releases).

**C10. Price map (who pays whom).** Copiers pay nothing directly. The broker pays the trader about 1.5%/yr of AUC [S2].
Exchange copiers pay the trader 10-50% of profits [S6]. Signal-marketplace subscribers pay a seller-set monthly fee, minus 20% [S10]. Strategy
marketplaces charge $99-$997/month in their calculator examples, with the creator keeping 70-87% [S11]. Mirroring apps charge about $10/month [S9].
Enforced chat-group and education sellers charge "hundreds or thousands" [S15][S16]. Stock Social's $9.99/month ($99.99/year) viewer
tool sits at the mirroring-app price point. It is the only one of these where neither the viewer's money nor the trader's income depends on
the trader's results. Confidence: Medium (prices are heterogeneous and partly vendor claims).

**C11. Gap filled.** No pattern reviewed offers a record that is (a) portable across brokers, (b) read-only with no execution or payout
attached, (c) whole-record, with losses ranked like gains and no outcome-based admission screen, and (d) percent-only. Each verified model
verifies only the ledger it executes on (C2, C5, C7), or it shows hypothetical history (C8). The enforcement record (C9) shows the
buyer's core problem is selective disclosure, which TR-1..TR-8 target directly. Confidence: Medium. This is an absence-of-evidence
claim from a sample of the patterns, not an exhaustive survey.

**C12. Gap not filled / limits.** (a) ICP-S1 sells *forward* alerts. Stock Social forbids alerts and pushing (F3b), so it can prove
a seller's past but cannot carry the product being sold. The seller still needs the chat group, and "Verified" can be borrowed as
marketing for it. (b) Traders can omit unconnected accounts (TR-8), which is the same selective-disclosure vector at account level. (c) Most
fraud in C9 is in crypto pools, forex and penny-stock promotion. Coverage depends on which brokers and exchanges the data vendor supports
(not checked here). (d) In the incumbents, traders are paid (C1, C5, C7), and Stock Social pays nothing (F5). The status-only motive (ICP-S2) is
untested. Confidence: Medium.

**C13. Threat: incumbents could add a portable record cheaply, but their incentives cut against it.** The integration cost is low.
The aggregator Stock Social uses lists $1 per connected user per month for daily read-only data, plus $100/month [S20]. *Estimate:* 10,000
traders ≈ $10,100/month. Against that: the largest broker pays traders to stay exclusive (C2), and earns on activity on its own ledger
(prospectus "Total Commission" from trading [S1]). Importing outside records would promote rivals' accounts. Signal and strategy marketplaces
earn a 13-30% cut of fees (C7, C8), so a free public record undercuts their paywall. The likeliest fast follower is a strategy or signal
marketplace adding "linked external account" badges. A disclosure-mirroring app is another candidate. Mitigation: whole-record rules and lower-bound
ranking are policy choices incumbents' revenue models resist, not technology. Confidence: Medium (incentive reasoning; costs sourced).

#### Sources (all opened: yes, 2026-11-01)

(the 2021 filing exceeded fetch size). b07 and b08 not verified (403). Treat them as Low.

#### Open questions
1. Does the large broker's "exclusivity agreement" bar its paid traders from publishing records elsewhere? If so, that supply is
   closed to Stock Social. (Needs the agreement text.)
2. Which crypto exchanges and forex brokers does the data vendor actually cover? The fraud in C9 is concentrated there.
3. Would ICP-S1 publish a whole record when the record must show losses and cannot carry their alerts? This is untested. DESIGN's ICP-S1
   interviews have no record in the repo.
4. Could a seller cite a Stock Social record in a paid-alert pitch, which would make Stock Social part of a promotion? This needs the legal
   committee's view (COMPLIANCE §2) and a terms-of-use rule.
5. Unverified prices: the strategy marketplace's typical per-strategy price and cut (b08) and the mirroring-app price (b07).

## F3. Competitive analyst B: social investing feeds, forums, creators

### P1: Competitive landscape B: social feeds, forums, finance creators

**Member:** Competitive Landscape Analyst B. **Pass:** 1 (independent desk research). **Date:** 2026-11-01.
**Scope:** (1) investor microblogs and charting-idea communities; (2) discussion-board finance communities and research/forum sites with paid author marketplaces; (3) finance creators on short-video, photo and long-video platforms. All third parties are described generically (POLICY T1/T2/T4). Platform names that appear in sources are replaced with generic descriptions.

#### Claims

**C1. Social channels are a mainstream input for US retail investors, and much more so for younger adults. Stock Social's audience is already on these channels.**
In the 2024 NFCS Investor Survey (n = 2,861 US investors with non-retirement accounts), 29% relied on "social media groups or message boards" for investment decisions (30% in 2021), and 26% made decisions based on a social media personality. Among investors aged 18-34 the figures were 60% and 61%. For investors aged 55+ they were 9% and 6% [S1]. A separate FINRA staff report puts social media at 24% overall, with 35% for investors aged 18-29 versus 13% for 65+ (2025 Foundation study) [S2]. The wording of the two surveys differs, so the figures are not directly comparable. *Confidence: High.*

**C2. Finfluencer followers concentrate on long-form video and photo platforms. Discussion boards and microblogs come next, then short video.**
Among finfluencer followers who also use social media, the channels cited were: a long-form video platform 77%, a photo-sharing platform 51%, a large general social network 49%, a microblog 47%, a large discussion-board community 46%, a short-video platform 40%, and a chat/community server app 25% [S1]. Across all respondents, the long-form video platform led at 30% and the discussion-board community came second at 15% [S1]. *Implication:* one share surface is not enough. The card has to work in video descriptions, photo bios, microblog posts and board threads, and each handles links differently (see C9-C11). *Confidence: High (survey); Medium (implication).*

**C3. Viewers come for entertainment and social belonging as much as for returns. This fits a swipe feed, and it is also a risk.**
Among social-media-informed investors, 59% cited entertainment as an investing motive (18% for non-users) and 59% cited social activity (11% for non-users). The same group scored lower on objective knowledge (42% vs 47% correct) while more often rating their own knowledge as high (63% vs 53%) [S1]. Fraud victimization was also higher: of those targeted, 68-69% of users/followers lost money versus 26-29% of non-users [S1]. *Implication:* demand for "watch real traders" is real, but the audience the ICP-D targets is the most fraud-exposed segment. That supports POLICY's no-signals/no-advice stance and the confidence-interval framing. *Confidence: High (data); Medium (implication).*

**C4. On investor microblogs, popularity and skill are inversely related. The academic remedy points to verification and quality tracking, which is Stock Social's core.**
A study of 72 million posts by more than 29,000 finfluencers on a ticker-tagged investor microblog [S3][S4] found average skill near zero. 28% of finfluencers were skilled, with about 2.6%/month abnormal returns. 56% were "antiskilled", with about −2.3%/month. Unskilled and antiskilled finfluencers had more followers and more influence than skilled ones, and the crowd did not correct them. The authors' institute summary recommends "improved transparency, active quality tracking, and verification" [S3]. *Caveat:* the study scores *recommendations* (post sentiment vs subsequent returns), not broker-verified trades. *Confidence: High (finding); Medium (fit).*

**C5. Claims on these platforms are not verified. "Proof" is a screenshot or lifestyle imagery, and regulators treat staged success as a fraud marker.**
None of the surface categories in scope verifies trading records. The relevant artefacts are user-generated posts, screenshots and chart annotations (repo premise, DESIGN §1.1; consistent with the sources below). The UK regulator's June 2025 international action against unauthorised finfluencers, involving 9 regulators, described individuals using a showy lifestyle "often falsely" to suggest success. The UK side alone issued 50 warning alerts, which the regulator said would lead to 650+ takedown requests and 50+ website takedowns. It also made 3 arrests, authorised criminal proceedings against 3 people and sent 7 cease-and-desist letters [S5]. *Confidence: High.*

**C6. Documented pump-and-dump schemes run through microblogs and chat communities, and the "skilled trader" persona is the tool.**
In December 2022 the SEC charged eight individuals over an alleged roughly $100M manipulation scheme. Seven of them were presented as skilled traders with large followings on a microblog and in chat-server trading communities. They allegedly posted buy/hold/price-target messages on stocks they already held, then sold without disclosure [S6]. An academic study of millions of microblog messages about OTC stocks found that abnormal message spikes come with a same-day price jump and a sharp reversal within a week. It also found clusters of promoters, fake accounts and automated posting [S7]. The SEC staff report on the early-2021 meme-stock episode named social-media sentiment, including frequent mentions on a discussion board, as one of the factors, and listed digital engagement practices for further study [S8]. *Confidence: High.*

**C7. Regulators in the EU and UK treat monetized, follower-driven posting as regulated recommendation or promotion. This weighs on DESIGN's ICP-S1 "signals seller".**
Under the EU Market Abuse Regulation, ESMA warns that any post or video giving direct or indirect buy/sell ideas, or an opinion on a future price, may be an "investment recommendation". Follower or view counts, as drivers of influencer revenue, are listed as an indicator that a person counts as a *professional* producer of recommendations, which triggers additional duties. Sanctions for breaches of the recommendation regime reach EUR 500,000 for natural persons [S9]. In the UK, unauthorised influencers promoting regulated products without approval "may be committing a criminal offence", and each communication must comply on its own [S10]. In the US, a broker-dealer was fined $850,000 in March 2024 over about 1,700 paid influencers. They were paid a flat fee per funded account through referral links, with no cap, and 39,400+ accounts were opened. FINRA found the posts were not fair and balanced and were not reviewed or retained [S11]. The FINRA influencer sweep closed with 5 enforcement actions plus 3 related matters [S12]. *Implication:* if the product verifies a paid-signals seller's record, Stock Social could be seen as adding credibility to an unregistered recommendation business. POLICY's no-signals rule cuts against ICP-S1 as worded in DESIGN §1.2, and counsel/committee should look at this. *Confidence: High (regulation); Medium (implication).*

**C8. Scale: the large discussion-board community is large and growing. We found no primary scale figures for the investor microblog or the research/author-marketplace sites.**
The discussion-board operator's Q2 2026 release reports 130.3M daily active uniques (+18% y/y), 514.6M weekly active uniques and $805M revenue, of which $762M is advertising [S13]. That is total platform scale, not finance communities. Earlier peer-reviewed work, cited in [S1], found that opinions on a research/forum site predict returns. We did not open it, so that finding is not used here. *Confidence: High for the board figure; scale for the other categories is not established.*

**C9. Feed link suppression is measurable on large platforms. A link post is the weakest way to reach an audience.**
- On a large general social network, only **1.1%** of US feed content views in Q2 2026 were of posts containing an external link, down from 1.3% in Q1 2026, according to the platform's own transparency report [S14].
- On a microblog, an observational arXiv study normalized views by followers. Across 4.3M and 34.8M posts, posts linking to outside news sites had median visibility about 7.5× and 4.7× lower than posts with no link. The study cannot prove the algorithm causes this [S15].
- A non-peer-reviewed vendor analysis of 18.8M microblog posts from 71,000 accounts reported a 0% median engagement rate for link posts from non-paying accounts since March 2025. Paid accounts got about 0.28% on link posts versus about 0.9% on text posts [S16].

*Confidence: High that links are a small, low-reach share of feed views; Medium on mechanism.*

**C10. On short-video and photo platforms the norm is "link in bio". Caption links are not clickable, and bio links may be gated.**
A third-party how-to guide (updated 2026-10-16) reports that the leading short-video platform requires roughly 1,000 followers plus a business account before a bio website link is allowed. Below that, only non-clickable text is possible. The platform's own help page did not render for us [S17]. *Confidence: Low-Medium; the official rule needs confirming.*

**C11. The share card is best positioned as a complement: a bio link, a pinned reply, or a link dropped in chat or a thread. A link in the main feed is the weak case.**
Unfurls rely on the Open Graph standard (og:title/type/image/url) [S18], which the product already implements via `opengraph-image`. Given C9 and C10:
- **(a) Images travel, links don't.** A card posted as a native image with the link in bio or a reply fits platform norms better than a bare link post.
- **(b) Bio links are a favorable slot.** Creators already treat them as a "proof" slot, and a verified record does not compete there with their main content.
- **(c) Chat groups and discussion threads are the likely unfurl venues.** Chat/community servers are used by 25% of followers [S1], and discussion boards are used heavily. *Assumption:* these surfaces render link previews more readily. We found no measured source for this.

**Estimate:** the week-8 gate of 5,000 external-referrer profile views per week, spread over 50 traders, works out to about 100 views per trader per week (5,000 ÷ 50). Counting only the 30 traders who meet the 60% share gate (0.6 × 50 = 30), it is about 167 each (5,000 ÷ 30). For bio-link traffic from small accounts with gated bio links, that is a demanding target. *Confidence: Medium.*

**C12. Competing versus complementing, by category.**
- **Microblogs:** complement. These are recommendation posts with no record behind them; a verified link in the bio or a reply adds the missing proof.
- **Charting-idea communities:** mostly complement. They publish ideas, not trades. Some overlap exists because they compete for viewers' attention.
- **Discussion boards:** complement for "show your record" norms, with possible friction. Board-specific rules on self-promotion were not researched (open question).
- **Paid author marketplaces:** they compete for paying viewers' research budget, since both sell access to a person's judgment. The $9.99/month "Record tools" offer sits in the same wallet. We found no pricing data for these marketplaces.
- **Creators:** complement for creators who want proof. Expect self-selection: the popularity-skill gap in C4 suggests the most-followed creators gain least from a full, loss-inclusive record. *Hypothesis (not data):* uptake will skew toward smaller or more skilled traders, and the external referral traffic they can bring is smaller as a result.

*Confidence: Medium.*

#### Open questions
1. What are the official bio-link eligibility rules on the short-video and photo platforms (follower thresholds, age, account type)? Do they apply per region?
2. Do chat/community-server apps and discussion boards render link previews for new or low-karma accounts, and do board rules on self-promotion allow posting your own record link? No measured source was found.
3. What are the scale and pricing of paid author marketplaces on research/forum sites? This bears directly on the $9.99 "Record tools" wallet; no primary source was opened.
4. What are the user counts of the investor microblog and the charting-idea community? Not found in primary sources.
5. How does verifying ICP-S1 ("signals seller") interact with ESMA's "professional" test and with POLICY's no-signals rule? This goes to the AI legal committee.
6. Self-selection (C12): will the traders with the largest audiences avoid full, loss-inclusive records? Test this in early outreach rather than assume it.
7. The DESIGN.md "interviews of ICP-S1" have no record in the repo and are treated as unverified.

## F4. Competitive analyst C: broker-native features, trackers, data vendors

### P1: Competitive landscape C (broker-native social, trackers and journals, data vendors, verified track records)

Member: Competitive Landscape Analyst C. Pass 1 (independent desk research), 2026-11-01.
Rules: docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/BRIEF.md and POLICY T1/T2/T4 apply. No third-party names; every product is described generically. Company pages are cited without URL or name.
Every number below comes from a page I opened (opened: yes), or is labelled as an estimate or assumption.

#### Claims

##### A. Broker-native social features

**C1. The biggest new threat is a large commission-free app broker. It now runs a verified in-app trading feed for all of its eligible US customers.** The beta was announced 2026-03-18 for about 1,000 invited customers, with 10,000 more to follow. It verified profiles by identity check, verified trades live, and showed entry and exit times [S1]. Trade press dated 2026-10-14 says the feed is now "live for everyone" eligible in the US. Shared items are realized P&L, allocations, charts and verified trades. Copying is manual, and the feature's user agreement says it is not a copy-trading platform. The same report puts the broker at 28.4M funded customers at end of June 2026 [S2]. Neither source says whether non-customers can view profiles on the open web, or whether trades from other brokers can be included. Confidence: High that the feature exists and is broadly available; Low on public-web visibility.
- *Gap closure:* it already covers "verified, single broker" for a very large base, at no cost to the trader. Adding public web profiles would be quick for it. Taking in other brokers' data runs against its incentive to keep activity in-house (inference, Low). Our wedge narrows to traders with **several** brokers, or traders who want a link that works outside one app.

**C2. A large social copy-trading broker pays its traders. Stock Social does not.** Its program pays 1.5% a year of average assets under copy, paid monthly, starting at the second tier. The tiers have minimums for assets under copy, equity and copiers. Traders must keep a daily risk score of 7 or lower, a weekly drawdown no worse than -25%, and at least 30% profitable months [S3]. The page cites 40M+ registered users in 75+ countries. It does not show a US entity among the program's regulated entities [S3]. Confidence: High.
- *Implication:* the signals-seller persona (ICP-S1) can earn money from a verified record elsewhere today. Stock Social offers only proof. Under POLICY F5 it may never offer payment or copying, so this gap cannot be closed from our side. It is a positioning fact to accept.

**C3. Broker-native "verified P&L share links" and broker-embedded verified forums already exist outside the US.**
- A large broker in India offers a read-only, unique public link to a trader's P&L, pulled from its own backend, meant for posting on social media [S4].
- A Nordic broker announced on 2026-06-26 that it had embedded a third-party verified-holdings layer in its investor forum, which has 2M+ users. The layer shows performance and holdings anonymously and hides net worth and cash [S5]. That vendor is a white-label "verification layer" that could extend to other brokers. Multi-broker coverage is not stated.

Confidence: High that these exist; Low on their scale of use.

**C4. One broker that sold itself as "the social investing app" shut its social trading features in June 2025.** It said AI-generated content had replaced the feed [S6]. This is evidence that broker-run social feeds can be dropped when they don't pay. It does not show that demand is missing. Confidence: Medium (trade press only; no primary post opened).

##### B. Social-investing apps, portfolio trackers, trade journals

**C5. Third-party social-investing apps with brokerage linking are the closest substitutes. Their viewer-side prices bracket $9.99.** These figures come from app-store listings I opened:
- A portfolio-tracker social app claims 900,000+ investors and "verified" portfolios. It charges $8.99/month or $59.99/year, and $19.99/month or $119.99/year for its top tier. It has 7.3K ratings averaging 4.8 and is age-rated 13+ [S7].
- A newer app auto-posts linked-broker trades and offers a "discreet mode" that hides totals plus an inner circle that sees full detail. It sells in-app items at $2.99-$7.99 and has 14 ratings [S8].
- A third app checks trades against market data. Broker linking is optional for it [S9]. It charges $8/month or $84/year, and $299.99/year for its top tier. It claims a 45,000-member chat community [S9].
- A pseudonymous app lets users post positions from linked brokers. Trade press reported 23,000+ users and a $4.5M seed round in June 2024 [S10].

Confidence: High on prices; Low on user counts, which are self-reported.
- *Gap closure:* these apps already link several brokers, so "portable multi-broker" alone is **not** a durable wedge. They could copy it in weeks (estimate). What none of them claims is our trust package: no per-trade hiding, losses ranked like gains, confidence intervals, rankings by lower bound, and frozen records. Several do the opposite and let users hide selectively. That package is the defensible part. Their 13+ age ratings also differ from our 18+ rule (P4).

**C6. Trade-journal software is trader-paid and costs much more than our viewer price. It already syncs with many brokers.** One journal charges $35, $59 or $99 per month, or $26, $44 or $74 per month on annual billing. It claims 500+ broker and prop-firm integrations, and "trade sharing" and community features come on every plan. Its pricing page mentions no public profile and no verification label [S11]. A review dated 2026-08-14 lists another journal at $29.95, $49.95 or $79.95 per month, claims 900+ brokers, and offers a 7-day trial but no free plan. The same review says that journal has no community engagement [S12]. A third-party guide warns that "broker counts" often include CSV templates, not live sync (search result only, not opened; Low). Confidence: High on prices.
- *Gap closure:* journals have the sync, the P&L engine and paying traders. A "verified public profile" toggle would be a small addition for them (estimate). Their buyers pay to analyze privately, though, and their sharing is not labelled broker-verified. A move to public records would still be the fastest serious entry of any group here (Medium).

**C7. Portfolio trackers charge $7.50-$16 a month for consumers and list "portfolio sharing" without explaining it.** One tracker charges $7.50, $10.50 or $16 per month ($90, $126 or $192 a year), includes broker integration on every plan and lists "Portfolio Sharing" with no detail [S13]. Trackers focus on holdings and dividends, not closed-trade records. Confidence: High on prices; Low on what the sharing does.

##### C. Track-record verifiers

**C8. A "verified track record" category has existed in forex since 2009. Stock Social's design copies its mechanics for stocks.** A forex analytics site checks a trader's history by logging in with the read-only investor password of a widely used forex trading terminal, several times a day. It compares that history with what the trader submitted, corrects the figures if they differ, and marks the record verified. A separate "trading privileges" check has the trader place a minimal trade tagged with a key. Its systems page lists only verified accounts [S14]. Pricing was not found. Confidence: High on mechanics (from a 2010 source); Low on current state.
- *Implication:* this is the "forex-only performance tracker" in DESIGN §1.3. Its users already know the "verified badge plus equity curve" pattern, so that interface is familiar, not new.

##### D. Data vendors (SnapTrade and alternatives)

**C9. SnapTrade's cost structure supports the owner's $1/trader and $100/month inputs, with three caveats.** SnapTrade's published pricing [S15]:
- **Build (free):** up to 5 connections, for development and testing.
- **Launch:** a $100/month platform fee plus usage. Daily plan: $1 per connected user per month, read-only, with manual sync at $0.05 each. Real-time plan: $2 per connected user per month.
- **Custom:** volume discounts, price on request.
- Billing is **per connected user, not per connection or broker**.
- Personal/OAuth users are $0 "for a limited time", with no end date given.

The three caveats:
- (a) A user stays billable until their last connection is deleted [S17]. A trader who stops using the app but leaves the connection in place keeps costing $1/month (estimate: churned-but-connected traders × $1).
- (b) On **both** plans, transactions are cached, refreshed once a day and delayed by one day. Intraday transactions are not available. Only holdings refresh on request on the real-time plan [S16]. Closed trades, the core record, are therefore T+1 on either plan, so the $2 plan buys only fresher open positions. The "$2 conservative" case in the projection only matters if F-6 needs intraday positions.
- (c) A disabled connection stops all fresh data until the user re-authenticates [S16][S17].

Confidence: High.

**C10. SnapTrade's coverage is about 40+ institutions, with uneven history depth.** Its docs say "450M+ retail investor accounts across 40+ financial institutions" [S18]. Its marketing page filters by US, Canada, Europe, Australia and India, but gives no total count [S19]. Some brokers require an application before access. History depth depends on the broker; one large US broker returns only about 4 years [S17]. Confidence: High.
- *Implication for TR rules:* "full history" should mean "the full history the broker provides", and the product should say so (F2 completeness rule).

**C11. SnapTrade's terms allow public display only on the user's say-so, and contain one clause that needs a written answer.**
- *Supports us:* the Application Compliance Policy says "the user owns their account data and can choose with whom and how to share it." It says analysis and signals must be **pull-based** and never pushed, which matches F-3's pull-only Record tools. It forbids claiming endorsement by financial institutions [S20].
- *Needs a written answer:* the Developer Terms forbid selling, licensing or buying data obtained from SnapTrade, and say "not to mine, re-sell, or re-package any End User data." Market data may be used only to serve the end user [S21].
- *Why the clause matters:* a paid viewer subscription built on traders' derived returns could be read as "re-packaging". The opposing reading is that the trader consents to publish, and viewers pay for tools, not data.

The terms also require us to:
- delete **all** end-user data on request from the end user or SnapTrade [S21]. This limits "records freeze on disconnect" and needs counsel (POLICY), since a deletion request must win.
- tell users we use a third-party service, and require them to accept SnapTrade's end-user terms [S21].
- accept that SnapTrade may end access "at any time, for any reason", or cut off the API for non-compliance [S20][S21].
- be 18+ as the developer.

The end-user terms (updated 2025-01-08) limit use to "providing services to you" and do not address public display [S22]. Confidence: High on the text; Low on how SnapTrade would interpret it. **Ask SnapTrade in writing before launch.**

**C12. SnapTrade's polling guidance may conflict with the current hourly sync.** The launch checklist says to call activities no more than once per account every 24 hours, and to poll balances, positions and orders in the background no more than 4 times a day per user [S23]. CLAUDE.md describes an hourly `POST /api/sync` that re-reads 7 days. A quick grep of `lib/sync.ts` found no 24-hour throttle, only an oldest-first order on `last_synced_at`. Confidence: Medium on the guidance; Low on whether the code breaks it (not traced end to end). This also bears on C9 (b): polling hourly gains nothing, because transactions update daily.

**C13. Other aggregation vendors don't publish prices for investment data.** A major bank-data aggregator lists its investment products as "Included" in pay-as-you-go plans but shows no prices; quotes come from sales [S24]. Search snippets showed per-user prices from regional vendors, but I did not open those pages, so I don't use them. Confidence: Medium. Only SnapTrade's investment-data price could be checked; a second vendor costs a sales cycle to price.

#### Synthesis: who could close "portable multi-broker public record" fast

| Group | Has multi-broker data | Verified label | Trader pays / is paid | Speed to close gap (estimate) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| App broker's in-app social (C1) | No, own broker only | Yes | Free | Fast on public web; slow or unlikely on multi-broker |
| Copy-trading broker (C2) | No | Yes | Paid 1.5%/yr of AUC | Unlikely; its model needs in-house trades |
| Social-investing apps (C5) | Yes | Claimed | Free; viewers $8-$20/mo | Weeks; they lack the trust rules, not the data |
| Trade journals (C6) | Yes (500-900+ claimed) | No | Trader $26-$99/mo | Weeks to months; strongest engine |
| Trackers (C7) | Yes | No | $7.50-$16/mo | Months; holdings focus |
| Forex verifiers (C8) | Forex only | Yes | Unknown | Not in stocks today |

Net: portability is table stakes. The defensible difference is the **whole-record rigor** (TR-1..TR-8, CIs, lower-bound rank, no hiding) on an open-web link, adults only. Viewer price $9.99 sits inside the $8-$20 band of comparable apps (C5). Confidence: Medium.

#### Open questions
1. Does SnapTrade read "re-package End User data" as covering a public profile, and a paid viewer tier built on derived returns? Get the answer in writing (C11).
2. How does "records freeze on disconnect" square with the duty to delete on request? Counsel and the AI legal committee need to rule (C11).
3. Are the app broker's social profiles visible to signed-out web visitors? This decides how much open-web-link value we keep (C1).
4. Does the hourly sync throttle activities calls to one per account per day? Engineering should trace it (C12).
5. How long does the $0 Personal/OAuth price last, and do our users fall under it (C9)?
6. What share of the target traders (ICP-S1/S2) hold accounts at two or more brokers? No source found. This decides how big the multi-broker wedge is.

## F5. User researcher, supply side (traders)

### Council pass 1: User researcher, supply side (traders who publish)

Member: user researcher (supply). Pass: 1 (independent desk research). Date: 2026-11-01.
Scope: who publishes P&L and why, what the evidence says about the records they would publish, what they fear,
jobs-to-be-done per segment, what makes a record worth sharing, three simulated walkthroughs, and the realistic first 50.
Inputs read: DESIGN.md Stage 1 (ICP-S1, ICP-S2, BL resolution) and §3.5, Stage 5 TR-1..TR-9; F-1, F-4, F-5, F-6;
POLICY P3/P4/F1-F3; COMPLIANCE §2 and §11; `lib/share-prompt.ts`. DESIGN's "interviews of ICP-S1" have no record in the
repo, so I treat them as unverified (BRIEF).

#### Claims

**C1. Most of the active traders the product targets will publish records that are negative or show no clear pattern.
The product's honesty rules make that visible by design.** Brazil: of the individuals who began day trading equity futures
in 2013-2015 and kept going for at least 300 days, 97% lost money and 0.4% earned more than about US$54/day [S1].
Taiwan, 1992-2006: day traders' combined performance is negative, 74% of day-trading volume comes from traders with a
history of losses, and 97% are likely to lose money in future day trading [S2]. A related paper finds large, persistent
differences in skill: the top 500 by prior-year return keep earning positive after-fee returns the next year, while the
bottom-ranked keep losing [S3]. So a small skilled tail does exist.
Implication: under F-4's fixed trust-line table, most new records will read "Still early, and results could go either way"
or "with a clear pattern of losses". Few will reach "consistent gains". Confidence: **High** for the direction (several
markets and decades). **Medium** for the size in this product's user base (US equities/ETFs, not only day traders).

**C2. People pass on the trades that went well. That is why screenshots exist, and it is also why a full record is harder
to share.** A peer-reviewed model treats investors as converting others with a probability that rises with realized
returns, convexly. Senders selectively report profits ("self-enhancing transmission"), which skews what listeners hear
upward and spreads high-variance, high-skew strategies [S4]. Separately, joining an investment social network roughly
doubles a trader's disposition effect (selling winners, holding losers), and connected traders converge on similar
levels [S5]. Implication: the posting habit ICP-S2 already has (wins only) is the exact behaviour TR-1/TR-4/TR-7 remove.
The product is asking these traders to give up their current status tool. The disposition-effect evidence also makes
F-6/TR-4 (open positions on every surface) a high-salience element. On a record from a trader prone to the disposition
effect, the losses in open positions will show up. Confidence: **High** (peer-reviewed theory plus causal evidence).

**C3. Attention drives retail buying, and a swipe feed of trades is an attention surface.** Individuals are net buyers of
attention-grabbing stocks: stocks in the news, with unusual volume, or with extreme one-day returns [S6]. For supply this
cuts two ways. Big single-trade moves make the most eye-catching cards (F-4 judges the chart the eye-catching element). But
the swipe feed can also drive attention trading among viewers, which keeps F-5's ban on live sizes, and the no-alerts rule,
load-bearing. Confidence: **Medium** (strong paper, transfer to this surface is inference).

**C4. Publishing P&L is tied to selling access, and regulators have acted against the false-results version of it.** FTC
(2022): a day-trading education seller marketed its founder's own trading results as proof the strategy was profitable and
scalable, while the vast majority of customer accounts lost money; it paid $3M [S7]. SEC (2022): eight people built
followings of hundreds of thousands on a microblog and in chat-server trading rooms by presenting themselves as successful
traders. Seven of them are alleged to have bought, urged followers to buy, then sold without disclosure, for about $100M
in alleged profits [S8]. So ICP-S1's job ("prove I'm real so more people pay me") is real. It also sits next to the legal
risk COMPLIANCE §2 names: POLICY lists "paid signal sellers as users" as an open counsel topic. Confidence: **High** that the
behaviour exists. **Unknown**: whether S1 can be welcomed. That is the owner's and the legal committee's decision.

**C5. Audiences don't trust screenshots, and traders know it.** In a public retail-trading Q&A forum (2022-2023, not linked
per T1) the replies converge on four points: profit screenshots are used to lure beginners into courses and paid channels;
screenshots, videos and even tax documents can be faked (browser balance-editing extensions, several accounts, a
relative's account); refusing to show proof is a red flag; and only independent verification counts [S9]. A broker help
page in another market (not linked) tells users to share a verified URL instead of a screenshot. It also notes that
verified P&L may not show full history, because people show their best performance [S10]. This supports the wedge in
DESIGN §1.1. Confidence: **Medium** (forum themes, not a sample).

**C6. What traders fear about publishing, ranked by how much evidence I found.**
(a) *Dollar amounts and account size.* P3 already removes these. F-5's exact share-of-account size would bring the fear
back: one dollar figure posted elsewhere reveals the account's value (F-5's own finding). Confidence **Medium** (consistent
with forum themes [S9][S11] and DESIGN's unverified interviews).
(b) *Being judged on losses, and pressure to perform.* A trader's blog (2026) describes years of refusing to publish
because public numbers push you to trade for the audience: cutting winners early, hiding drawdowns, adding risk. It
describes publishing later, all accounts and the losing periods, once the operation could take scrutiny of its worst
periods [S11]. Confidence **Medium**.
(c) *Alpha theft / reverse engineering.* The same blog keeps results public and portfolio composition private [S11]. Forum
replies say profitable traders rarely share methods [S9]. The product's per-trade symbols and the open positions in F-6
expose more than results. Confidence **Medium**.
(d) *Broker-link security.* In an industry-association survey of 1,500 US fintech-app users (2018): two-thirds are very or
extremely concerned about data privacy, about half are uncomfortable sharing financial information, nearly a quarter
would not use an app that stores bank credentials, and 56% want to control which accounts and data types are accessed
[S12]. The CFPB calls credential screen-scraping "a still common but risky practice", and its 2024 rule limits data use to
what the consumer asked for and makes revocation easy [S13]. The rule is now under reconsideration [S14]. The TR-2 rule
(every account under a login, no opt-out) cuts against the 56% who want per-account control. Confidence **Medium**
(the survey is old and industry-sponsored, and it covers banking, not brokerage).

**C7. Many of the people who would share already use social media for investing and are often overconfident.** FINRA
Foundation 2024 survey: 29% of retail investors use social media or message boards for investment decisions, 60% among
ages 18-34. 26% have acted on a social-media personality's recommendation, 61% among 18-34. These users are more often
overconfident (high self-rated knowledge, low tested knowledge), report far more fraud losses when targeted (68-69% vs
26-29%), and say they invest for entertainment (59% vs 18%) and social activity (59% vs 11%) [S15]. FINRA's 2025 report:
35% of investors aged 18-29 rely on social media [S16]. Implication: ICP-S2's "status" job is well supported. The same
segment's overconfidence predicts that the gap between the record they expect and the record they see will be the main
churn point right after import. Confidence: **High** for the numbers, **Medium** for the implication.

**C8. Jobs-to-be-done and the features that serve them** (inference from C1-C7 and the docs, Confidence **Medium**):

| Segment | Job | Serves it | Works against it |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 signals seller | Prove I'm real so buyers pay and stay | Verified badge, full-history "Record since" (TR-3), share card with range, broker list (TR-8) | No link-out to paid group designed; open counsel item; F-5 Terms ban adviser advertising/paid promotion; record shows bad months |
| S2 proud hobbyist | Status among peers | Chart on card, short period, story-size card, swipe feed reach | Wins-only habit is impossible (TR-1); "New record" badge; thin-tier "could go either way" line |
| S3 (proposed) accountability / process trader | Discipline: a public record I can't edit | Freeze on disconnect (TR-5), no hiding, confidence range, open positions | Very little; small audience, so fewer external referrals |
| S4 (proposed) long-history steady investor | Show a long, calm record | Strong tier "long record" line, years unit | Low trade frequency; ranks only from 10 bets; feed favours big moves |

**C9. What makes a verified record worth sharing (hypotheses, Confidence Low until measured).**
H1: Shares are driven by the *chart's shape and the headline sign*, not the range. A positive, rising line gets shared.
H2: A losing or "no clear pattern" record still gets shared in three cases: (i) as proof of honesty by S1 sellers whose
pitch is "I show my losses" (the blog [S11] is an example of this pitch); (ii) as self-deprecating humour by S2 (fits the
entertainment motive in [S15]); (iii) by accountability traders (S3). Each of these happens far less than sharing
a winning record. H3: The 60% gate is most at risk for S2 records in the thin tier. Under C1 most records will
sit there. Estimate: if about 15% of active traders have a positive edge [S3 abstract context] and, say, 80% of those
share versus 50% of the rest (assumption), the share rate is 0.15×0.8 + 0.85×0.5 ≈ 55%, below the 60% gate. Recruiting
mix, not card design, decides this gate.
How the product handles it today: the one-time "Your record is live" share prompt fires once after first sync
(`lib/share-prompt.ts`), whatever the result. The trust lines are symmetric (F-4 2026-10-31). No sharing reward exists
(correct under F5). Open design gap: no per-trade card path is decided for "share one honest trade" (F-4 open question).
That would be the policy-compliant way to share a highlight. TR-7 keeps the all-time record on the card.

**C10. The realistic first 50** (inference, Confidence **Low-Medium**): not S1 at scale and not random S2s. In order:
(1) traders who already publish full records or statements by hand (the "I publish everything" niche, [S11]-type), because
the product saves them work; (2) process and accountability traders in journaling and discipline communities (S3); (3) small
S1 sellers (under ~5k followers) whose pitch is transparency, once counsel clears them; (4) S2 traders with a good recent
run who are recruited one-to-one. Each needs a supported broker and an 18+ adult. Recruiting is manual (no social sign-in;
F-1 relies on shared links). A gate of 50 connected traders at 60% sharing means about 30 sharers. Plan for about 100
connect attempts if F-1's consent step loses half (assumption; F-1 instruments the funnel, so measure it).

#### SIMULATION walkthroughs (not research data)

**SIMULATION A: small signals seller (S1), about 8k followers, two broker logins.** Arrives from a peer's card → sign-in
(no social option; uses email link). Trust notice → vendor portal popup on mobile in-app browser, blocked → redirect
fallback (F-1 handles it; friction: context switch). Consent screen: "full history, every account under the login". Pauses,
because one account under the login is a family or old account. **Friction 1: TR-2 has no per-account opt-out.** Likely
outcome: abandons, or connects only the second login (TR-8 lists brokers, so the gap is disclosed only by omission).
Profile: 140 bets, "fair sample, no clear pattern yet". Shares the card in the paid group, not publicly. **Friction 2: no
designed place to say "my record" on a paid offer, and the legal status is open.** External views: low.

**SIMULATION B: proud hobbyist (S2), 24, one account, 3 months of options and equities.** Options are skipped
(`fetchActivities` limitation); 9 equity closes import. Card shows "Too few trades to say much yet" or "range still too wide",
plus "New record" and "position size not shown". **Friction 3: the trades they are proudest of (options) are missing.
Friction 4: under 10 bets, so not ranked in the feed.** Open positions show −18% unrealized (F-6). Share prompt appears;
dismisses. Most likely outcome: never shares. This is the H3 risk.

**SIMULATION C: accountability trader (S3), 3-year history, about 400 closes.** Consents, waits for the import banner,
gets "A long record, with no clear pattern" and a chart near zero. Shares it in a discipline community as a public
commitment. **Friction 5: the card's running sum is "position size not shown"; this trader sizes carefully, and F-5 (which
would show it) is blocked on vendor permission.** Outcome: shares, few external views, high retention.

Opened: yes (abstract and intro). The 15% figure in C9 comes from a search snippet of this paper's published version, which I did not open; treat it as Low.

#### Open questions
1. Can ICP-S1 (paid group sellers) be users at all, and can a profile mention a paid offer? (POLICY "paid signal sellers as users" is still open with counsel/the legal committee.)
2. Does TR-2's all-accounts rule cost more connections than it saves in trust? Measure decline reasons on the F-1 consent screen; add a non-identifying "why not" option.
3. Options skipped: what share of S2 activity is options? If it is high, S2 is the wrong early segment.
4. Is a per-trade card ("one honest trade", losses equal) a compliant share path for thin records? (F-4 open question.)
5. No primary research exists: run 10-15 real interviews per segment before week 4, so the week-8 "re-interview" has a baseline.
6. Current consumer-comfort data on brokerage (not bank) aggregation; none found from a government source.

## F6. User researcher, demand side (viewers)

### Pass 1: User researcher, demand side (viewers of verified records)

Member: user researcher (demand side). Pass: 1, independent desk research. Date: 2026-11-01.
Scope: how adults use social media for investing information, trust in finfluencers, what viewers want verified,
D7 return drivers, who pays for Record tools, and the herding/copying risk. Repo inputs read: DESIGN.md Stage 1
(ICP-D), F-2, F-3, F-4, council projection inputs. No interviews were run; none exist in the repo.

#### Claims

**C1. Social media is a mainstream investing information channel for adult investors aged 18-34, not a niche.**
In the 2024 national investor survey, 29% of US retail investors relied on social media or message boards for
investment decisions, 60% among investors aged 18-34 (vs 9% at 55+); 26% made decisions based on a social media
personality's recommendation, 61% among 18-34 [S1][S2]. UK regulator data: 62% of 18-29 year-olds follow social
media influencers, 74% of those trust their advice, 9 in 10 were encouraged to change financial behaviour [S3]
(the release names no underlying survey; treat as Medium). Among US investors aged 18-25 (2023), social media was
the top learning source (48%), ahead of internet search (47%) and family (45%); 50% report a FOMO-driven
investment [S4]. Adults 18-29 also take news from influencers far more than older adults (37% vs 21% of all
adults) [S5]. Confidence: High (US), Medium (UK). Implication: ICP-D's channel assumption (short video) is
supported; the 18-27 range in DESIGN should be read as "18-34 adults" when sizing.

**C2. The core viewer problem is real: popular finfluencer claims are unverified and, on average, unreliable.**
A study of stock-opinion posts on an investor microblog classifies most finfluencers as unskilled or antiskilled,
finds the less skilled ones are more engaging and attract larger followings, and argues social media rewards
engagement over skill [S6]. A content review across five countries found only 20% of finfluencer content with
investment recommendations carried any disclosure [S7]. An international regulator body reports that most
surveyed regulators cite promotion of paid courses, newsletters, paid forums and "tips" as a harm (abstract seen
via search only; not opened) [S8-unopened]. Confidence: High for the gap; Medium that viewers feel it.

**C3. Users of social media for investing already do more due diligence than non-users, yet lose more to fraud,
which is the clearest paying "job".** Social-media-using investors consulted 7.6 information sources vs 4.0 for
non-users and were more likely to check a professional's background (36% vs 14%); among those targeted for fraud,
68-69% of users/followers lost money vs 26-29% of non-users [S1]. They score 42% on objective knowledge while 63%
rate their knowledge high [S2]. US reported investment-scam losses were $5.7B in 2024 [S9]; scams starting on
social media lost $2.1B in 2025, $1.1B of it investment scams, including chat groups where scammers posed as
successful investors and fake platforms that showed fake profits [S10]. Confidence: High (data); Medium that a
verified-record product reduces that harm (untested). Job: "before I pay or send money to someone who claims
results, let me check a record that cannot be faked."

**C4. Viewers do not mainly want returns; they want relatable, engaging explanation, and entertainment is a
stated motive.** Focus groups (adults roughly 18-27 in five countries) describe finfluencers as free, instantly
accessible, relatable and engaging, used as a substitute for advisers they see as conflicted [S7]. Social media
investors cite entertainment (59% vs 18%) and social activity (59% vs 11%) as investing motives [S1]. The
regulator-body guidance and the professional-association report both tell investors to check motivation,
qualifications and consistency, i.e. verification is what experts want; viewers reward relatability [S7].
Confidence: Medium. Implication: D7 depends on the thesis/explanation layer and the swipe "outcome reveal", not on
stats; stats are the trust anchor that differentiates.

**C5. What viewers would want verified (inferred from the harms above, not measured directly):** (a) the trades
happened in a real account (not screenshots), (b) losses are included (survivorship), (c) sample size and time
span (one lucky streak vs a record), (d) whether the person is paid to promote. Stock Social covers (a)-(c) via
read-only pull, TR rules and the F-4 interval line; (d) is not covered. Confidence: Medium (inference from
[S1][S6][S7][S10]); no viewer survey checks the ranking.

**C6. Herding risk is evidenced: showing others' results increases risk taking and copying, and followers chase
raw past returns.** In a lab asset market, information on others' success raised risk taking, more so with a copy
option, and the authors conclude copy trading leads to excessive risk taking [S11]. Access to a social trading
network nearly doubled traders' disposition effect, with connected traders converging [S12]. Heavy buying by
users of a commission-free app broker, driven by attention lists, predicted -4.7% average 20-day abnormal returns
for the top stocks bought each day [S13]. On social trading platforms, inflows chase raw past returns, nearly only
for top performers, not risk-adjusted alpha [S14]; following the highest accumulated returns produced large
losses and follower counts were not positively related to returns [S15]. Confidence: High that the risk exists;
Low-Medium on size for a percent-only, closed-trades, no-alerts product. Mitigations already in design (closed
trades only, no alerts, lower-bound ranking, losses ranked like gains, no sort-by-return in Record tools) map
directly onto these findings, especially [S14][S15] (raw-return chasing). Residual risk: the swipe feed is itself
an attention list in the sense of [S13].

**C7. D7 >= 15% is plausible only for viewers with a reason to return; a feed of closed trades has weak novelty
for casual scrollers.** No source opened gives D7 for finance-content apps; this is an open gap. Estimate of
return drivers (assumption): new closed trades from followed traders (pull-only, no push allowed by F3b), new
theses, and the outcome reveal. Because no notifications are allowed, return must be habit- or link-driven; share
cards (F-4) re-inject viewers. Confidence: Low.

**C8. Payers are a small due-diligence segment, not casual scrollers, and the base case sits below break-even.**
Projection base conversion is 1.5% of monthly viewers (assumption, from app and newsletter benchmarks) vs owner
break-even 1.7-2.8% (estimate: base is 0.2-1.3 points short). Segments and jobs (assumption, built from C3/C4):
1) casual scroller (largest; job: be entertained, learn what real traders do; will not pay); 2) pre-purchase
checker about to pay a signal seller or paid group (job: verify before paying; high intent, one-off, likely
churns after one month, and only helps if the seller is on the platform); 3) follower-chooser / hobbyist
comparing several traders over time (job: keep a private shortlist with notes; best fit for saved views +
notes, annual plan); 4) creator-adjacent (traders checking peers; traders never pay, so out of scope).
Confidence: Medium on segments, Low on sizes.

**C9. Biggest demand-side friction: the record a due-diligence viewer wants is usually not on the platform.**
Segment 2's target (a seller of signals or a paid group) has an incentive not to connect. Record tools only
compares connected traders. Unless "not on Stock Social" itself becomes a signal viewers can ask for, segment 2
cannot be served. Confidence: Medium (reasoning; no data).

#### Persona walkthroughs (SIMULATION — not research data)

**P1. Casual scroller, 23, adult, mobile only.** Taps a share card in a group chat: chart, trade count, period,
"+1.3% per trade, likely between -0.1% and +2.7%". Profile loads signed out (good). Reads "Still early" line,
does not understand the interval but likes the honesty. Swipes into the feed, enjoys 6-8 outcome reveals, leaves.
Never sees a paywall (Subscribe lives only where a paid feature appears, not on profiles). Friction: no reason to
return in 7 days without push; theses are the hook but sparse early; interval wording may read as a forecast
(risk under C6). Would pay: no.

**P2. Pre-purchase checker, 34, about to pay a monthly fee to a trading chat group.** Lands from a search or a
link someone posted about verified records. Searches for the group's host: not found. Browses lower-bound ranked
traders instead; notices losses are shown and returns are percent-only, which builds trust in the product but
does not answer the question. Reaches Record tools paywall: $9.99, no free trial, cannot see the compare view
before paying. Friction: target absent (C9); no trial blocks a one-off job; compare cannot be sorted by return,
which this persona expects (correct per policy, but needs a one-line reason). Would pay: unlikely; at most one
month.

**P3. Follower-chooser, 41, self-directed hobbyist.** Arrives via a profile link, signs up, follows four traders,
returns weekly to read new closed trades and theses. After two weeks wants to line up the four with intervals and
keep notes on why each was followed. Hits the paywall at "Compare". Friction: no trial, but the free profile
already showed enough to judge value; annual plan is attractive if notes persist; disappointed nothing is
shareable (by design). Would pay: plausible, annual. Risk: treats the shortlist as a signal list (C6); disclaimer
on every surface is required.

- [S7] Global investment-professional association report on finfluencer appeal, January 2024, content review of 110
  pieces plus focus groups in five countries (URL omitted per T1: domain carries the association's name). (opened: yes, full PDF)

- [S9] US Federal Trade Commission press release, 2024 fraud losses, March 2025. https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2025/03/new-ftc-data-show-big-jump-reported-losses-fraud-125-billion-2024 (opened: no; figure from search result, Medium)
- [S10] FTC data spotlight, social-media scam losses, April 2026. https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/data-visualizations/data-spotlight/2026/04/reported-losses-scams-social-media-eight-times-higher-2020 (opened: yes)
