- [S12] Academic paper on social interaction and the disposition effect, Review of Financial Studies 29(11), 2016. https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:oup:rfinst:v:29:y:2016:i:11:p:3177-3209. (opened: yes, abstract)
- [S13] Academic paper on attention-induced trading by users of a commission-free app broker, Journal of Finance 77(6),
  2022. https://doi.org/10.1111/jofi.13183 (opened: yes, abstract via econpapers)
- [S14] Academic paper on investment flows into social trading portfolios, Journal of Financial Research 42(2), 2019. https://doi.org/10.1111/jfir.12174 (opened: yes, summary page)
- [S15] Academic paper on returns of actors on social trading platforms, Quarterly Review of Economics and Finance 70,
  2018. https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:eee:quaeco:v:70:y:2018:i:c:p:160-171 (opened: yes, abstract)
- Repo: docs/DESIGN.md Stage 1; docs/features/F-2, F-3, F-4; docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json (conv base 0.015).

#### Open questions
1. No D7 benchmark for finance-content or swipe-feed apps was found in an openable public source; who can supply one?
2. Can a viewer request that a named profile connect ("not on Stock Social yet") without becoming a pressure or
   shaming tool? Needs legal committee view (C9).
3. Does "no free trial" suppress segment 3 conversion? Is a free, limited compare (e.g. two traders) policy-safe
   and enough to show value?
4. Does the F-4 interval line get read as a forecast by casual viewers? Needs a comprehension test with adults.
5. Should a "paid to promote" disclosure field (C5 d) exist on profiles? Not covered by any current feature.
6. Measure herding: share of viewers who view a trader's closed trade and then open the same symbol elsewhere is not
   observable; what proxy is acceptable without tracking symbols (F-3 forbids symbol-keyed entitlements)?

## F7. Pricing analyst

### Market research council, Pass 1: Pricing analyst

Member: Pricing analyst · Pass: 1 (independent desk research) · Date: 2026-11-01
Scope: willingness to pay (WTP) for "Record tools" (compare traders side by side, saved views, private notes; pull-only)
at $9.99/month and $99.99/year with no free trial (F-3, decisions 2026-10-25, 10-26, 11-01). Inputs read: F-3 in full,
POLICY F1-F6/P4, DESIGN Stage 1 ICPs and §3.5 gates, COMPLIANCE auto-renewal row, docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json
and the dashboard's benchmark list. Nothing here recommends any security or trade.

#### Claims

**C1. The $9.99/month list price sits inside the range of comparable viewer-paid products; the price is not the main problem.**
Ranges from pages I opened (vendor and affiliate pages, so prices are indicative only):
- Portfolio trackers and investing dashboards: about $3-$25/month, $25-$250/year at the core tiers; many have a free tier
  capped by holdings or portfolios [S3].
- Charting/screening communities: entry about $13-$15/month, upper tiers $35-$240/month; free tier with ads [S3].
- Investing research subscriptions: about $200-$300/year at the consumer tier, $2,000+ at professional tiers [S3][S4].
- Trade-journal SaaS (a trader's tool for their own trades, a different buyer): about $16-$99/month; mostly no free tier,
  some 7-14 day trials or money-back windows [S5].
- Copy-trading/portfolio-copying apps: about $10/month or $90-$100/year (projection benchmark b06/b07, not reopened by me).
- App-store medians: North America monthly $9.99 and yearly $39.99 [S1].
Confidence: Medium (prices from vendor-authored comparison pages; several internal inconsistencies flagged by the pages).

**C2. The annual plan is priced high relative to benchmarks and gives a thin discount.**
$99.99 vs 12 × $9.99 = $119.88, a 16.6% discount (estimate, arithmetic shown). Trade-journal annual billing in [S5] runs
about 10-50% below monthly (e.g. $29.95 → $16.47/month equivalent ≈ 45% off at one vendor); one budgeting/tracker product
lists $14.99/month vs $69.99/year (≈61% off) [S3]. North America's app median yearly price is $39.99 [S1], so $99.99 is
2.5× the median yearly plan for a feature set that is a convenience layer (C4). Lower-priced yearly plans retain better at
year 1 (53.7% vs 48.3% high-priced) and monthly plans likewise (22.5% low, 16.4% mid, 12.2% high) [S2].
Confidence: Medium.

**C3. "No free trial" is defensible at launch volume but removes the best-documented conversion lever; the projection's
base conversion (1.5% of monthly viewers) is likely 3-15× too high for this offer.**
Evidence: day-35 download-to-paid is 10.7% median under a hard paywall vs 2.1% freemium [S1] (12.1% vs 2.2% prior year
[S2]); trial-to-paid runs 25.5% (≤4-day trials) to 42.5% (17-32 days) [S1]. Neither report I opened compares trial vs
no-trial conversion directly [S1][S2], so "no trial" cannot be priced from them. Stock Social is structurally freemium with
a thin premium: the whole record is free and must stay free (F-3, POLICY F4), so the 10.7% hard-paywall figure does not
apply. Newsletter free-to-paid of 5-10% [S6] is for email subscribers who opted in, a far more committed base than
monthly viewers arriving by share link.
Estimate (arithmetic): the week-8 gate expects viewer D7 return ≥15%. If payers come almost only from returning viewers,
1.5% of all monthly viewers = 1.5 / 15 = 10% of returning viewers paying, which equals the median hard-paywall result for
apps people chose to download. That is not plausible for an add-on to free data. My working range: **0.1%-0.5% of monthly
viewers** (conservative 0.1%, base 0.25%, strong 0.5%), i.e. roughly 1-3% of returning viewers. This is an estimate,
not data. Against the owner's break-even of ~1.7-2.8%, the base case does not break even at this price on conversion
alone; break-even depends more on the trader-to-viewer ratio and the $1/trader/month data cost (projection inputs).
Confidence: Medium on direction, Low on the exact range.

**C4. The paid job is weak as specified, because POLICY strips out the parts of "analytics" people usually pay for.**
What viewers pay for in comparables (C1) is mostly screening, sorting, ranking, alerts and data depth. Record tools may not
sort by return or lower bound, highlight, give verdicts, list open positions, push anything, or be shared (F-3 acceptance
criteria; POLICY F1, F3b). What remains:
- Compare side by side: the free profiles already show every number; a viewer can open two tabs. Value = layout and time saved.
- Saved views: overlaps with free follows/bookmarks.
- Private notes: replicable in any notes app; useful mainly if tied to a record's history (e.g. a note pinned to a date).
This is a "convenience over free data" job, which typically supports low prices and low conversion. The discovery ICP
(DESIGN ICP-D: mobile-only, short-video discovery, one-swipe attention) is the least likely buyer of a desktop-style
comparison tool; the plausible buyer is a smaller segment of repeat, research-minded adult viewers, whose size is unknown.
Confidence: Medium (reasoning from the spec; no user evidence exists in the repo; DESIGN's ICP-S1 interviews are unverified).

**C5. The value metric is "depth of research on traders I track over time", not access.** Access to records cannot be
the metric (free by rule). Candidate metrics consistent with POLICY: number of traders in a comparison set, number of
saved views, notes history length, or exports of the viewer's own notes and comparison sets. F-3 requires follow and
watchlist limits to be equal for all viewers; it does not bar a free tier of Record tools with a small compare/saved-view
limit, but that needs the legal committee's check that a limit on compare sets is not a disguised paid-access gate.
Confidence: Low-Medium.

**C6. Plan mix and renewal assumptions in the projection are consistent with the sources, with one source ambiguity.**
Plan mix 42% monthly / 34% yearly globally, 36% / 40% in North America [S1]; yearly plans 44.1% still paying at 12 months,
monthly 17.0% [S2]. The web-subscription churn page [S7] reports a 3.6% median (2.34% voluntary, 1.25% involuntary); my
fetch summarised it as an annual figure, which would be implausibly low, so it is most likely a monthly rate as the
projection assumes; re-check before relying on it. The app-store 17% 12-month retention implies about 14%/month on monthly
plans (projection's arithmetic), so the projection's 7% base monthly cancellation is on the optimistic side for a
low-engagement add-on. Hard-paywall refunds run 5.8% vs 3.4% freemium [S2].
Confidence: Medium.

**C7. Auto-renewal compliance shapes the annual plan more than price does.** The federal "click to cancel" amendment was
vacated by an appeals court on 7/8/25 (FTC litigation status report [S8]); ROSCA and state auto-renewal laws still apply
(COMPLIANCE.md row on automatic-renewal laws: checkout must show price, interval, cancellation method and next-charge date;
reminders for annual plans; California's "unconditional gift" consequence). An annual plan therefore needs a pre-renewal
reminder, which POLICY F3b allows as a billing notice with no record fields. Confidence: High (repo + regulator source).

**C8. Alternative packaging consistent with POLICY (no paying for visibility, no paid trader access, traders never
charged, nothing paid hides the record, no alerts):**
1. Lower anchor: $4.99/month or $39.99/year (≈33% off), aligned with the North America yearly median [S1]. Estimate: at
   0.25% conversion, revenue per 1,000 viewers ≈ 2.5 payers × ~$4.20 net/month ≈ $10.5 vs ≈ $22 at $9.99 only if
   conversion holds; the test (below) decides, not this arithmetic.
2. Annual-first presentation with a deeper discount ($79.99/year ≈ 33% off $119.88), since yearly plans renew 2.6× better
   than monthly at 12 months [S2].
3. Freemium Record tools: free compare of 2 traders and 1 saved view; paid unlocks larger sets, notes history and export
   of the viewer's own notes. Needs legal-committee review (C5).
4. Card-up-front 7-day trial or a 14-day money-back window (common among trade-journal vendors [S5]) as a test arm vs
   no trial; F-3's data model already supports `trialing` with a 3-day reminder.
5. "Supporter" plan (pays for the platform, identical features to free, no badge visible to others): low conversion,
   zero advice risk; any public supporter badge must be checked against F5/F6 and avoided if it reads as status.
6. Multi-seat plan for adult investment clubs (shared notes inside the club only, still not public): untested; needs
   legal review because shared notes edge toward publishing commentary.
Excluded by rule: paid alerts (F3b), paid follow/watchlist limits (F-3), boosted profiles (F5), paid access to open
positions or theses (F5/counsel), anything charged to traders.
Confidence: Medium (rule fit), Low (demand).

**C9. Honest WTP range.** For the core convenience job: roughly **$3-$6/month or $25-$50/year** for the median interested
viewer, with a minority of research-heavy viewers accepting $9.99/month (estimate, reasoning from C1-C4 and the [S1]
medians; no primary data). $9.99/$99.99 is plausible as a ceiling, not as a mass price. Confidence: Low.

#### Cheapest pricing tests (in order, all before real charging, which F-3 already blocks until P6)
1. **Instrument free proxies (cost: analytics only).** Share of sessions opening ≥2 profiles within 10 minutes, returning
   viewers per week, follows per returning viewer. If <5% of returning viewers show multi-profile sessions, the compare
   job is weak regardless of price.
2. **Painted door with clear disclosure.** A "Compare traders" entry point leading to a page that states plainly "Record
   tools isn't available yet. No charge. Join the waitlist?" with the price shown (split $4.99 / $9.99 by random
   assignment, annual price beside each). Measure click-through and waitlist sign-up per price. Waitlist emails may only
   announce the product launch (no handle, symbol or record field, F3b). Disclosure on the same screen avoids deceptive
   design; log only aggregate counts (P rules).
3. **four-question price-sensitivity survey plus one purchase-likelihood question at set prices**, shown only to signed-in returning
   adult viewers after the painted-door click; target ≥200 responses per segment (practitioner guidance that ~40 responses
   are unstable [S9]). Treat stated thresholds as upper bounds (hypothetical bias [S9]).
4. **Test-mode checkout walk-through** (vendor test mode, F-3): count who reach the hosted checkout from the waitlist
   invite; no money moves.
5. Once P6 lands: a real-money launch to the waitlist with a 14-day money-back window vs no trial, two price arms.
Decision rule (assumption, to be agreed): proceed at a price arm only if painted-door waitlist rate among returning
viewers is ≥3% and the price-sensitivity survey's acceptable range includes that price.

#### Sources
- [S1] Subscription-app industry report 2026 (subscription infrastructure vendor), 2025 data; URL omitted per T1 (vendor brand in domain). Opened: yes.
- [S2] Same vendor's 2025 report (retention by plan and price, refunds, hard paywall vs freemium). URL omitted per T1. Opened: yes.
- [S3] Portfolio tracker comparison 2026, published by a tracker vendor that ranks itself first (not independent). URL omitted per T1. Opened: yes.
- [S4] Investing research site's premium price-update page ($299/year renewal), undated. URL omitted per T1. Opened: yes.
- [S5] Trade-journal comparison blog by a trade-journal vendor (prices, trials, money-back). URL omitted per T1. Opened: yes.
- [S6] Paid-newsletter platform's "going paid" guide (5-10% free-to-paid). URL omitted per T1. Opened: yes.
- [S7] Subscription-billing vendor's churn benchmarks page, July 2026 data. URL omitted per T1. Opened: yes (metric period ambiguous, see C6).
- [S8] FTC Semiannual Federal Court Litigation Status Report, Dec 2025, entry on the Negative Option Rule petitions, https://search.ftc.gov/system/files/ftc_gov/pdf/2025-12-Final-Public.pdf. Opened: yes.
- [S9] Practitioner guides on the four-question price-sensitivity method (survey vendors' help pages); search result summaries only, no peer-reviewed validation found. Opened: search results only (Low).
- Repo: docs/features/F-3-viewer-subscriptions.md, docs/POLICY.md, docs/DESIGN.md, docs/COMPLIANCE.md, docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json and the dashboard's benchmark list. Opened: yes.

#### Open questions
- Is there any evidence (not the unverified ICP-S1 interviews) that viewers want to compare traders, versus only browse?
- Does the legal committee accept a free Record tools tier with a compare-set limit (C5, C8.3)?
- Should the projection's base conversion drop from 1.5% to ~0.25% of monthly viewers? If so, break-even moves almost
  entirely onto the connected-trader ratio and the broker data cost.
- Confirm [S7]'s churn metric is monthly.
- Is the buyer mobile (ICP-D) or desktop? A side-by-side comparison tool is a desktop-shaped job.

## F8. Distribution and growth analyst

### Market research council, pass 1: Distribution and growth analyst

Member: Distribution and growth analyst. Pass 1 (independent desk research), 2026-11-01.
Repo inputs read: docs/DESIGN.md (Stage 1, §3.4, §3.5), docs/features/F-2, F-4, docs/POLICY.md (P4, T5, F1-F6),
docs/COMPLIANCE.md §2, §5, §11, docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json and the dashboard's benchmark list, and the shipped code for referral attribution
(`lib/referral.ts`), `app/robots.ts`, `app/sitemap.ts`.

#### Claims

**C1. The week-8 gate "5,000 external-referrer profile views a week" cannot be measured by the product as built.**
No code reads the HTTP Referer of profile requests or stores page views; the only attribution is `lib/referral.ts`,
which records which profile's call to action brought a *signup* (owner-only, never shown). POLICY P6 bans third-party
trackers, so the count has to be first-party (a referrer-domain bucket per profile view, no user id, no IP stored).
The same gap applies to "shared at least once" (the Share button and copy-link are not counted) and to viewer D7
(signed-out viewers have no identifier; a first-party, cookie-free or consented first-party cookie scheme is needed,
COMPLIANCE §5 prefers cookie-free analytics). Without this, two of the four gates are unknowable at week 8.
Evidence: repo grep (no `referer` handling in app/ or lib/; no view table in db/schema.sql). Confidence: High.

**C2. The share-card unfurl is the right primary mechanism, and passive broadcast is the stronger lever for total reach.**
A randomized field experiment on a large social network (1.4 million friends of 9,687 users) found passive-broadcast
viral features raised peer influence and contagion by 246%, while adding active personalized invitations added a
further 98%; personalized messages converted better per message, but broadcasts were used more and produced more total
adoption [S1]. For Stock Social the "passive broadcast" analogues are a link in the trader's bio and a pinned post,
both unfurling to the F-4 card, rather than one-off posts. The card must carry the Open Graph minimum (title, type,
image, url) plus image width/height and `og:image:alt`, which the protocol says should accompany any image [S2] (also an
accessibility requirement in COMPLIANCE §9). Confidence: Medium (one experiment, different product, 2011).

**C3. Most shared links earn few or no clicks; expect a heavy-tailed distribution where a handful of sharers produce most views.**
A measurement study of links shared on a microblog to five news sites (9.6 million clicks to about 59,000 resources)
reported that 59% of URLs shared by users other than the publisher were never clicked [S3][S4]. The figure is about
links, not about sharers' reading habits [S4]. Implication: "60% of traders share at least once" does not imply
traffic. Week-8 traffic will come from 2-5 traders with engaged audiences, not from the average sharer. Confidence:
Medium (old, news links, one platform; I found no peer-reviewed link-CTR study for finance or for image unfurls).

**C4. Funnel arithmetic: the 5,000-views gate needs roughly 100-250 effective shares a week, or 1-3 large audiences. The gate is plausible only with deliberate recruitment of ICP-S1 (paid-chat-group sellers).**
All rates below are **assumptions** (no source opened gives organic reach or link CTR for this setting):
- Views per share = audience x share of audience that sees the post (assumption 5-15%) x click-through on the
  unfurl (assumption 1-3%).
- Hobbyist (ICP-S2), 1,000 followers: 1,000 x 10% x 2% = **2 views per post**.
- Signals seller (ICP-S1), 10,000 followers: 10,000 x 10% x 2% = **20 views per post**; 50,000: **100**.
- Seller posting the link inside their own paid chat group (small, engaged, opted-in audience), assumption 30%
  click: 500 members x 30% = **150 views per post**.
- Gate: 50 connected traders x 60% = **30 sharers** minimum. If 30 sharers post once a week at a blended ~25 views,
  that is ~750 views a week: **about 15% of the gate**. Reaching 5,000 a week needs any one of: ~200 shares a week at
  25 views; or ~10 ICP-S1 traders with 20k-50k audiences posting twice a week (10 x 2 x ~60 = 1,200) plus persistent
  bio links; or one or two short-video posts that travel (heavy tail, C3).
- Persistent bio links add steady traffic that this per-post math omits; I found no non-vendor evidence on bio-link
  click volumes (open question Q2).
Estimate: the gate is reachable only if a quarter or more of the 50 traders are ICP-S1 with 10k+ audiences. With a
hobbyist-heavy cohort it is likely missed by 3-10x. Confidence: Low (assumption-driven; arithmetic shown).

**C5. The supply gate itself (50 connected traders) needs about 500-1,000 targeted contacts, by estimate.**
Assumption chain: direct outreach to traders who already post records, 20% reply, 40% of replies sign up,
50% of signups complete a broker connection: 1,000 x 0.2 x 0.4 x 0.5 = 40; so ~600-1,300 contacts for 50 with
a 20-40% buffer. Every connect costs SnapTrade about $1 a month [projection b22]; 50 traders = ~$50 a month plus the
platform fee, trivial. The reference that organic and referral channels can dominate acquisition (over 80% of new
funded accounts at a commission-free broker, owner's projection b28, regulator filing, not re-opened by me) supports
organic-first. Confidence: Low (assumptions).

**C6. Demand exists on the channels the card travels through: adult investors aged 18-34 rely heavily on social media.**
The FINRA Foundation (2024 survey, released April 2026) reports 60% of investors aged 18-34 use social media for
investing, versus 9% of those 55+, and that 61% of 18-34 investors said they made an investment decision based on a
social media personality's recommendation [S5]. A December 2025 FINRA report gives 24% of all investors getting
information from social media, 35% of those aged 18-29 (adult investor survey) [S6]. A government survey of US adults
(fielded Jan-Jun 2026) shows 18-29 year-old adults use a large video platform at 95%, a photo-sharing app at 79%, and
a short-video platform at 65%, versus 31% for the main microblog [S7]. Implication: the card has to read well as an
image inside short-video and photo apps, where links are weak and bio links carry the click (see C2, Q2). Confidence:
High for the survey figures; Medium for the implication.

**C7. PR angle "broker-sourced records vs. screenshots" is timely and regulator-backed.**
The SEC charged eight social-media traders in an alleged ~$100 million scheme: they built followings of hundreds of
thousands by presenting themselves as successful traders, posted that they were buying or holding, and sold without
disclosure [S8]. The SEC Investor Advisory Committee (Nov 2024) recommended finfluencer disclosure rules and cited a
study finding only 20% of finfluencer content with recommendations carried any disclosure [S9]. A press angle that
says what the product *is* (records imported read-only from the broker, losses shown, whole record) is supported;
it must not claim to detect fraud, verify identity, be complete or "the only" (POLICY F3a), and must not name or
disparage any person or company (T2, T4). Confidence: Medium.

**C8. Paid creator partnerships are high-cost in compliance and mostly closed by POLICY.**
- Traders: POLICY F5 bars paying or rewarding traders for performance or frequency; the brief states traders are
  never paid. So a trader-creator can be recruited, not sponsored.
- Non-trader creators paid to promote the product: the FTC Endorsement Guides require clear disclosure of any
  material connection (cash, free product, perks), placed at the start, audible and visual in video, not only in
  a bio or behind "more"; "Ad"/"Sponsored" style wording; the advertiser is responsible for monitoring endorsers [S10].
  Any such post showing a record is the company's ad and must not cherry-pick or present results as typical
  (COMPLIANCE §11).
- A free Record-tools subscription given to a creator is itself a material connection needing disclosure [S10].
Confidence: High (rules), Medium (application).

**C9. Registered representatives who share their record are a distinct risk.**
FINRA Rule 2210 treats material sent to more than 25 retail investors in 30 days as a retail communication needing
principal pre-approval, bars implying past performance will recur, and requires testimonial disclaimers [S11].
FINRA Notice 17-18: a rep's personal post is generally outside 2210 if it does not concern the firm's products or
services; sharing or linking to third-party content can "adopt" it; a firm that arranges an influencer post is
"entangled" and it should be labeled an advertisement [S12]. FINRA's influencer sweep (2021-2025) produced five formal
enforcement actions and recommended vetting, training and recordkeeping for influencer and referral programs [S13].
Implication: Stock Social should never recruit or feature people as registered reps, and the connect flow could carry a
plain note that people in the securities industry should check their firm's rules before sharing. Whether a rep's
personal trading record is "about the firm's products" is unresolved (Q4). Confidence: Medium.

**C10. Referral programs: trader rewards are out; viewer rewards are possible but low value now.**
F5/F6 forbid rewards tied to trading, points convertible to money, prizes, and paying traders; connecting a broker
is close enough to trading activity that a trader-side reward should be treated as out. A viewer-to-viewer reward
(e.g. a month of Record tools for the referrer) is not tied to trading, but it is a material connection the referrer
must disclose next to the link [S10], and the referral link itself becomes a promotion. Evidence on reward design is
thin from sources I could open; the existing unrewarded attribution in `lib/referral.ts` is the right Phase 1 state.
Confidence: Medium.

**C11. SEO will not matter by week 8; it may matter later, and the current sitemap omits profiles.**
`app/sitemap.ts` lists ticker pages only; profiles are not in it (robots allows them). Profiles with under 5 bets
show "Too few trades to say much yet" (F-4) and are thin pages; indexing them may hurt site quality. Suggest listing
only profiles past the ranking floor (10 bets, F-4 2026-11-01) and the "How records are calculated" page. Search
referrals do count as external referrers for the gate. Ticker pages must never rate a security (F3b). Evidence:
repo; the sitemap protocol permits per-URL lastmod [S14]. Confidence: Medium (no external traffic evidence).

**C12. Retention gate (viewer D7 ≥ 15%) is in range for installed finance apps but likely above web-link visitors.**
An undated-method industry blog compilation (2026) lists D7 at 10.9% across apps and ~20% for finance apps [S15]; I
could not verify its underlying data. Link visitors who never sign in are a weaker cohort than app installs
(assumption). And F3b forbids pushing records by notification or email, removing the usual D7 lever. D7 should be
measured on viewers who signed in or followed someone, reported alongside all-visitor D7. Confidence: Low.

#### Best 3 channels
1. **Founder-led recruitment of ICP-S1 traders**, asking for a bio link and a pinned post, plus the link in their own
   chat group. Highest views per share (C4), persistent (C2), aligned with their job "prove I'm real" (DESIGN 1.2).
2. **Share-card unfurl via system share sheet and copy link (T5)**, tuned for image-first apps (C6): readable at 375px,
   alt text, the trust line visible in the image. It is the loop the gates measure.
3. **Earned media and educational content on reading records honestly** ("what a range around an average means",
   "why losses are shown"), tied to the regulator-documented screenshot problem (C7). Cheap, compliant if F2/F3a copy
   rules apply, and it builds the viewer side.

#### Worst 3 channels
1. **Paid performance ads**: fintech cost per registration around $18 [projection b27, not re-opened], ads with
   returns run into COMPLIANCE §11, and the viewer side has no paid product to recover cost before the gates.
2. **Sponsored creators / paid influencers**: F5 closes traders; non-traders need FTC disclosure and monitoring [S10];
   reps raise FINRA entanglement [S12]; high cost for audiences that may not connect a broker.
3. **Incentivized referral or "invite traders" rewards**: barred for traders (F5/F6); viewer rewards add disclosure
   duties for little gain before there is supply. (Runner-up worst: SEO as a week-8 channel; too slow.)

#### Open questions
- Q1. Who builds first-party measurement for the three unmeasured gates (C1) before week 1, and does the Privacy
  Policy cover referrer-domain logging?
- Q2. Bio-link click volumes and unfurl CTR in image-first apps: no non-vendor evidence found; measure in week 1-2.

## F9. Regulatory-market analyst

### Pass 1: Regulatory-market analyst

**Member:** regulatory-market analyst. **Pass:** 1 (independent desk research), 2026-11-01.
**Scope:** which rules shape the *market* for public, broker-sourced trading records: demand, how competitors behave, costs and the moat. This is not a legal opinion on the product (docs/COMPLIANCE.md covers that) and it is not legal advice. Cases are cited by reporter, docket or release number only (POLICY T1/T2).

---

#### Claims

**C1. The publisher's exclusion protects impersonal, disinterested, regularly published content. It does not protect timed alerts or touting, and that split runs straight through this market.** The Supreme Court held that the exclusion's two conditions, "bona fide" and "of general and regular circulation", "precisely differentiate 'hit and run tipsters' and 'touts' from genuine publishers". It said "bona fide" means "disinterested commentary and analysis as opposed to promotional material", and that "regular" means "not timed to specific market activity". Protection holds only while communications "remain entirely impersonal" and do not become "person-to-person relationships" (472 U.S. 181, at 206-210). The SEC Investor Advisory Committee restated these hallmarks in 2024. *What this means for the market:* paid real-time trade alerts, signal rooms and "follow my entries" subscriptions sit on the unprotected side. A paid product that sells *lookup of finished records* (pull-only, no push, no per-viewer picks) fits the protected side better. F3b and F5 match these hallmarks. F1a's personalized ordering is the edge to watch. [S1][S2] Confidence: **High** on what the case says; **Medium** on applying it to this market.

**C2. Creators who are paid by the platform risk meeting the adviser definition. Unpaid creators carry less of that risk, and this is a structural difference between business models.** The IAC records that the SEC treats the "compensation" element as met by "receipt of any economic benefit", from anyone. The IAC notes that this could arguably include payments from social platforms or advertisers. [S2]. *What this means for the market:* rivals that pay creators per subscriber or share revenue with them give each creator a compensation element. Stock Social's rule that traders "never pay and are not paid" (F5) removes that element at the trader level. **Medium.**

**C3. Enforcement keeps hitting the same misconduct that verified records rule out: self-promotion as a successful trader, scalping, and invented performance.** Examples:
- SEC Lit. Rel. No. 25591 / Press Rel. 2022-221 (S.D. Tex., No. 4:22-cv-04306, Dec. 2022). Self-described "successful traders" with large followings posted buy and price-target calls while selling; about $100M alleged [S3][S4].
- Press Rel. 2026-34 (FY2025 results). A jury found a microblog user liable for urging followers to buy microcaps he held and then selling, across more than 30 stocks and about $2.6M [S5].
- Press Rel. 2025-141 (E.D.N.Y., Dec. 2025). A chat-community promoter falsely claimed to manage more than $1B and to earn double-digit monthly returns. (actual about 1.4%); more than $18M raised [S6].
- CFTC Rel. 9039-25 (S.D. Fla., Jan. 2025). A forex "signals" subscription was sold on social-media profit claims. About $685K in restitution and penalties was ordered [S7].
- Celebrity crypto touting without disclosing pay: Press Rel. 2023-59, with eight promoters charged; Securities Act Rel. No. 11116 (Oct. 2022) [S8][S2].
*What this means for the market:* these cases supply the demand thesis. Viewers have a documented reason to distrust performance claims they cannot check. **High** on the facts; **Medium** that this turns into willingness to pay.

**C4. Regulators in the US, EU and UK all treat finfluencers as an open regulatory gap, and some proposals point toward track-record transparency.** The IAC (Dec. 2024 meeting draft) recommended a rule under Advisers Act §206(4). It would require disclosure of conflicts, compensation, qualifications and the "impersonal nature of the advice". The IAC also recommended published data on finfluencer misconduct "similar to" a broker-check database [S2]. In its MiCA review response (30 Sep. 2026), ESMA said MiCA "does not apply" to influencers who promote crypto for issuers or service providers. ESMA proposed new obligations for them, and suggested that "transparency of previous recommendations may also be explored" [S9]. *What this means for the market:* if any of this becomes law, demand rises for neutral, broker-sourced history and for proof of conflicts. Compliance costs for paid promoters rise too. None of it is law yet. **Medium.**

**C5. FINRA ties broker-dealers to influencer content when the firm pays for it or helps prepare it. That limits how brokers can co-market with a record platform.** Regulatory Notice 17-18 says that where a firm "arranged for" a post, the firm is "entangled" with it, and that influencer posts must be labelled as ads [S10]. FINRA's 2021 sweep of influencer programs ended with eight formal actions [S11]. FINRA's 2024 podcast reported that firms paid per funded account with no cap. It said about 70% of the communications reviewed were non-compliant, 55% of them over undisclosed paid ads. (podcast, not a report) [S12]. Regulatory Notice 26-14 (9 Jul. 2026, comments closed 11 Sep. 2026) proposes risk-based supervision, with finfluencer involvement as an explicit risk factor [S13]. The SEC approved Rule 2210 performance projections on 15 Oct. 2026 (Rel. 34-106675) [S14]. Note that Notice 24-09 covers generative AI, not influencers [S15]. *What this means for the market:* a broker that sponsors share cards, pays per referral or reposts records takes on supervision and record-keeping duties for them. The same rules keep broker-run social features compliance-heavy, which is a moat for an independent publisher that takes no broker money. **High** on the rules; **Medium** on the market effect.

**C6. Under the SEC Marketing Rule, advisers can use third-party rankings only with due diligence and disclosure, and per-trade percentage records do not meet the rule's performance format. A B2B "adviser badge" channel is narrow.** Release IA-5653 (adopted Dec. 2020, effective May 4, 2021) defines a third-party rating as a rating or ranking of an adviser by an unrelated person "in the ordinary course of its business". Advisers must reasonably believe any survey makes favorable and unfavorable answers "equally easy". They must disclose the date and period, the identity of the rater, and any compensation [S16]. Ad performance needs 1-, 5- and 10-year periods [S16]. Hypothetical performance shown to "mass audiences" without the required policies led to nine settled cases and $850K in penalties (Rel. 2023-173) [S17]. *What this means for the market:* Stock Social's rankings could become a "third-party rating" if advisers cite them in ads. Any payment from advisers would then have to be disclosed, and POLICY F5 bars paid visibility anyway. Selling to advisers is unlikely to be a near-term revenue line. **Medium.**

**C7. The CFPB's open-banking rule does not cover brokerage data, its compliance dates are stayed, and its ban on access fees is being reconsidered. Read-only investment-data access stays a matter of private contracts.** The 2024 final rule covers Regulation E accounts, Regulation Z credit cards and payment facilitation. Commenters asked for "investment products" and "retirement accounts" to be included. The CFPB declined and deferred them to "future rulemaking" [S18]. The rule bars data providers from charging fees for interface access (§1033.301(c)) [S18]. Compliance dates were stayed on 29 Oct. 2025 in No. 5:24-cv-304-DCR (E.D. Ky.) [S19]. The August 2025 advance notice asks about "the assessment of fees" and about who counts as a consumer's representative [S20]. The Spring 2026 regulatory agenda still lists reconsideration, with no final action [S21]. *What this means for the market:* there is no federal right to free brokerage-data access, so brokers can price, limit or refuse aggregator access. The owner's figure of about $1 per connected trader per month (docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json) is therefore a vendor-contract price that can change, not a regulated ceiling. Rivals face the same contracts. **High** on rule status; **Medium** on the cost effect.

**C8. Brokers' own data-security duties push them to control third-party access.** The 2024 Regulation S-P amendments (Rel. 2024-58; adopted May 16, 2024) require broker-dealers to run incident-response programs and to notify affected customers within 30 days. Compliance has phased in for larger and then smaller firms, with 18- and 24-month periods [S22]. *What this means for the market:* brokers have a regulatory reason to restrict access through credential sharing or screen-scraping and to favor vetted API partners. That favors vendor-mediated, read-only connections and raises the bar for new entrants. **Medium.**

**C9. Courts have made state age-verification mandates easier to sustain, so attestation-only "adults only" gating could become a per-user cost.** In No. 23-1122 (U.S., decided 27 Jun. 2025), the Court applied intermediate scrutiny and held that "adults have no First Amendment right to avoid age verification". (content obscene to minors) [S23]. In No. 25A97 (14 Aug. 2025), the Court left a state social-media age-verification law enforceable while the appeal proceeds. A concurrence said the law is "likely unconstitutional" but that the equities did not support relief [S24]. Secondary reports say that app-store age-verification laws in two states were enjoined, then reinstated or delayed. These reports are unverified, so confidence is **Low**. *What this means for the market:* a service with profiles, a feed and sharing may fall under state social-media laws even if it is adults-only. Where states require age assurance, it adds a vendor cost and sign-up friction at the moment of sharing, which threatens the "≥60% share" kill gate. **Medium.**

**C10. In the UK the financial promotion regime is criminal, reaches across borders, and is actively enforced against finfluencers. Expanding there is a regulatory project, not just a localization task.** Under FSMA s21, communicating "an invitation or inducement to engage in investment activity" in the course of business is restricted. A breach carries up to 2 years' imprisonment, an unlimited fine, or both. The restriction applies "even where a communication originates outside the UK if it is capable of having an effect in the UK". (FG24/1, Mar. 2024) [S25]. The FCA's June 2025 international action involved nine regulators. In the UK it produced 3 arrests, 50 alerts and more than 650 takedown requests [S26]. ESMA's 2026 factsheet says that public opinions on whether an asset "will go up or down" can be investment recommendations, and that a "not investment advice" disclaimer "will not protect you" [S27]. *What this means for the market:* Stock Social relies on its US disclaimer (F3). That does not carry over. A share card that shows a ticker and its return, seen in the UK, could be argued to be an inducement. Paid UK marketing would need an exemption or an approver with FCA permission. Crypto would bring in MiCA's market-abuse regime for the EU, under ESMA guidelines of April 2025 that cite "intensive use of social media" [S28]. **Medium.**

**C11. FTC endorsement and review rules make paid creator promotion expensive to run, and they reward organic sharing.** The Endorsement Guides FAQ calls for clear and conspicuous disclosure of material connections. It says platform disclosure tools alone are not enough and that advertisers must monitor their endorsers. If an endorser's results are atypical, the ad must disclose generally expected results [S29]. The Consumer Reviews and Testimonials Rule (16 CFR Part 465, effective 21 Oct. 2024) allows civil penalties for knowing violations. The FTC sent warning letters to 10 companies in December 2025 [S30]. *What this means for the market:* a trader's share card counts as an "endorsement" of Stock Social only if Stock Social gives the trader something. With no trader rewards (F5/F6), cards need no disclosure. Rivals that buy creator promotion carry disclosure and "typical results" duties. **Medium.**

---

#### Open questions
1. Is a share card that shows a ticker and its percentage return an "inducement" under FSMA s21 when UK users see it? Is an independent publisher's ranking an investment recommendation under EU market-abuse rules (MAR)? Neither regulator addresses records-only publishers.
2. Does F1a's per-viewer ordering of records count as "personalized" under 472 U.S. 181? The case law covers personal advice about securities, not personalized ranking of other people's records.
3. Which state social-media laws define covered services in a way that reaches adults-only, attestation-gated services? The status of the state app-store age laws was verified only from secondary sources.
4. If the 1033 reconsideration allows access fees, will vendors pass them through for bank data only, or will brokers follow and start charging for investment data? There is no primary source on broker pricing.
5. MiCA's transitional deadline for crypto-asset service providers and the exact Title VI article text could not be fetched (EUR-Lex was unreachable). Verify both before any crypto scope.

## F10. Data analyst (projection dashboard)

### Council pass 1: Data analyst (projection dashboard assumptions)

Member: data analyst · Pass 1 (independent desk research) · 2026-11-01
Model: `docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/model.mjs` (node; reads `projection-assumptions.json`; re-run with `node model.mjs`).
Steady state per month: revenue = V·conv·[(1−a)·9.99 + a·99.99/12]·(1−billing); cost = V·trader_share·broker_per_trader + 100 + platform_fixed + V/1000·hosting_per_1k; payouts 0.

#### Results

| Scenario | Net revenue per payer/mo | Variable cost per viewer | Fixed/mo | Break-even conv @1k | @10k | @50k | @100k | @1M | Limit (V→∞) | Viewers to break even at its own conv |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Conservative | $8.79 | $0.310 | $180 | 5.58% | 3.73% | 3.57% | 3.55% | 3.53% | 3.53% | never (conv 0.5% < 3.53%) |
| Base | $8.80 | $0.104 | $150 | 2.89% | 1.35% | 1.22% | 1.20% | 1.18% | 1.18% | ~5,400 |
| Strong | $8.75 | $0.026 | $130 | 1.78% | 0.45% | 0.33% | 0.31% | 0.30% | 0.30% | ~550 |

Monthly profit, base: −$122 at 1k viewers, +$130 at 10k, +$2,647 at 100k. Conservative loses money at every scale (−$2,841 at 10k).

| Unit economics (blended plan mix) | Conservative | Base | Strong |
|---|---|---|---|
| Expected paying life (months) | 10.4 | 16.8 | 25.8 |
| Revenue LTV after card/billing/tax fees | $91 | $146 | $222 |
| Broker+hosting cost carried per payer/mo (trader_share/conv × fee) | $62.00 | $6.93 | $0.87 |
| Contribution per payer/mo | −$53.21 | $1.86 | $7.88 |
| Contribution LTV | −$551 | $31 | $204 |
| CAC (ad-acquired payer) | $400 | $150 | $60 |
| Blended CAC (cac × paid_share, organic assumed free) | $200 | $45 | $12 |
| Payback on ad payer, revenue basis (months) | 45.5 | 17.0 | 6.9 |
| Payback on ad payer, contribution basis | never | 80.4 | 7.6 |
| Payback, blended CAC, contribution basis | never | 24.1 | 1.5 |
| Contribution LTV / ad CAC | <0 | 0.21 | 3.39 |

#### Claims

**C1. The owner's "break-even at roughly 1.7–2.8%" holds only at 1,000 monthly viewers, and the upper end is 2.9%, not 2.8%.** At 1k viewers the strong case needs 1.78% and the base 2.89%. The range leaves out the conservative case (5.6% at 1k; never below 3.5% at any scale). Fixed costs ($130–180) dominate at 1k. From about 10k viewers up, break-even settles near a floor set by variable cost alone: 1.2% base, 0.3% strong, 3.5% conservative. A fairer summary: "about 1.2% of monthly viewers paying once past ~10k viewers (base); 0.3%–3.5% across scenarios; 1.8%–5.6% at 1k." Evidence: model output. Confidence: High (arithmetic on the owner's inputs).

**C2. The real break-even condition is a ratio: at most ~8.5 connected traders per paying viewer at $1 per trader (~4.3 at $2).** At scale, profit ≥ 0 when conv·$8.80 ≥ trader_share·fee + hosting. The base case has 6.7 traders per payer, so each payer clears only $1.86/month after carrying broker data. Conservative has 30 traders per payer. "Viewers" is not the binding count. What matters is payers per connected trader. Confidence: High (arithmetic).

**C3. Tornado: three inputs dominate: conv, broker_per_trader, trader_share.** Profit swing at 100k viewers (base, each input moved to its conservative and strong values): conv −$6,151 to +$15,845; broker_per_trader −$7,353 to +$7,647; trader_share −$2,353 to +$7,647. Next: hosting_per_1k (±$450) and billing_pct (±$320). At 1k viewers, platform_fixed comes third for break-even conv (±0.3pp). Price, churn, renewal, CAC and payout inputs have no effect on steady-state break-even. Churn and CAC matter only through LTV/payback. Caveat: trader_share's swing is narrow only because its range is an assumption (C9). Confidence: High for the model, Medium for real-world ranking.

**C4. broker_per_trader = $1 and the $100 floor are confirmed. The $0.50 strong value is unsupported.** The data vendor's page lists a $100/month platform fee, $1 per connected user/month on the read-only daily plan and $2 for real-time. Billing counts users, not connections (one charge per user, however many accounts). Volume discounts exist only on a custom plan with no published rates, so $0.50 is a hope. The page also lists $0.05 per manual sync on the daily plan, and $0 per user "for a limited time" for personal/OAuth users. I checked `lib/broker.ts`: it calls only the account-list, activities and positions reads, no refresh call, so the hourly cron should not trigger per-sync charges. It does mean data are at most daily fresh. [S1][S10] Confidence: High.

**C5. The conversion basis uses the wrong denominators.** The 2.1% figure is installs that start a paid subscription within 35 days, freemium (hard paywall: 10.7%) [S2]. The newsletter platform's guide says 5–10% of *free subscribers* (email sign-ups), not readers [S7]. The "1–3% of free readers" in the basis is not what the guide says; it comes from a third-party note I did not open. Both denominators are committed users (an install, an email). "Monthly viewers" here are mostly signed-out share-link visitors with no account. The model also treats conv as a stock (active payers ÷ monthly viewers), while the benchmarks are flows (new payers per cohort). Payers pile up over a life of 10–26 months, so the two are not comparable without a viewer-retention curve. The 0.5%/1.5%/3% range is a guess, not something derived from the sources. Confidence: High on the mismatch, Low on any corrected value.

**C6. Churn: the 3.6% web figure is ambiguous, and app data imply a higher base than 7%.** The billing vendor's benchmark page shows "Overall churn rate 3.60%" with no stated period. Nearby text calls the vertical figures "median annual" while the chart reads like monthly, so the labels are inconsistent. Digital media 4.14%, education 4.99% [S4]. The 2025 app report gives 17.0% of monthly plans retained after 1 year [S3], which is about 13.7%/month (1 − 0.17^(1/12)). That is near the conservative 12%, not the base 7%. Churn doesn't move break-even. It does halve or double LTV. Confidence: Medium.

**C7. Annual renewal at 44% is sourced. Annual mix at 35% is if anything low, but immaterial.** Yearly plans retained after 1 year: 44.1% (2025, down from 47.1%) [S3]. The 2026 report gives the plan mix as 42% monthly and 34% yearly across all plan lengths [S2]. Among monthly+yearly only, yearly is about 45%. The tornado swing from annual_share is ±0.15pp. Confidence: High (sourced), immaterial.

**C8. Billing fees check out for domestic cards.** Card fee 2.9% + $0.30, billing 0.7%, tax 0.5% [S5] gives 7.10% on $9.99 and 4.40% on $99.99. Revenue-weighted at the base mix that is about 6.3%, so the base 6.5% is fine. Foreign cards add 1.5% and currency conversion 1%. A dispute costs $15, plus $15 for a manual response. The basis text still says "$90 a year"; the price is $99.99. Confidence: High.

**C9. trader_share (0.05–0.15) has no benchmark, and the repo's own kill gates imply a far lower ratio early on.** Week-8 gates: ≥50 connected traders and ≥5,000 external profile views per week [S10]. That is roughly 21,700 views a month. Assuming each unique viewer makes ~1–3 views, the implied ratio is about 0.2%–0.7% traders per monthly viewer, one to two orders of magnitude below the dashboard's range (estimate: 50 ÷ (21,700 ÷ 1..3)). If that held, broker cost would be minor. The risk would then be whether traders connect at all, not what they cost. Either way it is the input most open to error, and nothing published pins it down. Confidence: Low (estimate built on a design target, not data).

**C10. Paid acquisition does not pay at base.** Ad-acquired payer CAC is $150, revenue LTV $146, contribution LTV $31. Payback on contribution takes 80 months against a 17-month expected life. Even with 70% of payers organic (blended CAC $45), the contribution LTV/CAC is 0.70. Only the strong case works (3.4×). I could not open the CAC source (mobile ad network report, 2021–22; HTTP 403) [S9], and it is dated. Also, "registrations ÷ 3–8% paying" uses a sign-up step this funnel may not have. The paid_share basis is real: a commission-free broker's prospectus says over 80% of new funded accounts joined organically or by referral (2020–Q1 2021) [S8]. That is a different product and era. Confidence: Medium on the conclusion, Low on the CAC input.

**C11. Hosting inputs are plausible.** The hosting platform charges $20 per seat with a $20 usage credit and 1 TB transfer, then $0.15/GB [S6]. At ~30 MB per viewer, the first ~33k viewers fit inside the included transfer. After that, transfer runs about $4.50 per 1k viewers, so the base $4 per 1k is reasonable. I did not open the database vendor's page. Confidence: Medium.

#### What to measure first

**Connected traders per monthly unique viewer (trader_share), then paying viewers per connected trader.** Billing runs in test mode only (F-3), so conv can't be measured yet. trader_share can be measured today from existing data: count connected users (each is billed at $1) and unique external viewers per month. It is one of the three dominant inputs, the only one with no benchmark, and with conv it sets the C2 ratio. The second step, once billing is live, is conv measured as active payers ÷ monthly unique viewers (a stock, matching the model).

#### Sources

- [S2] Subscription-app industry report 2026, company research report (no URL per T1), accessed 2026-11-01, opened: yes (web summary; retention/refund figures not visible).
- [S3] Same publisher's 2025 report, company research report (no URL per T1), opened: yes.
- [S4] Subscription billing vendor churn benchmarks, July 2026 network data, company research page (no URL per T1), opened: yes.
- [S5] Card/billing vendor public pricing page, 2026, company page (no URL per T1), opened: yes.
- [S6] Hosting (Vercel) pricing page, 2026, https://vercel.com/pricing, opened: yes.
- [S7] Paid-newsletter platform "going paid" guide, company page (no URL per T1), opened: yes.
- [S8] Commission-free broker IPO prospectus (Form S-1), 2021, SEC EDGAR filing (URL path contains a brand, omitted per T1), opened: yes (first part).
- [S9] Mobile ad network industry report via a press-release wire, Sep 2022, opened: no (HTTP 403). Unverified.
- [S10] Repo: docs/DESIGN.md §3.5 kill gates, docs/features/F-3-viewer-subscriptions.md, lib/broker.ts, docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json, opened: yes.

#### Open questions
1. Does the data vendor keep billing a user whose connection lapsed but whose frozen record stays public (TR "records freeze on disconnect")? If not, the billed count is live connections, not lifetime traders.
2. How long does the vendor's "$0 for personal/OAuth users" offer last, and which supported brokers qualify? It could zero out broker cost early on.
3. Is conv meant as a stock (active payers ÷ monthly viewers) or a monthly flow? The dashboard should say which and model payer accumulation if it is a flow.
4. Is trader_share linked to viewer growth (traders bring the share links that bring viewers)? Treating it as an independent cost input may hide that link.
5. What custom-plan rate does the vendor offer at 1k / 10k connected users?

## F11. Red team

### Council pass 1: Red Team (independent desk research)

Member: Red Team. Pass: 1. Date: 2026-11-01. Mandate: the strongest evidence-based case that Stock Social fails as a business.
Every number is sourced [S#], taken from the repo or council files (cited by path or benchmark id), or labelled **estimate** with the arithmetic shown.
No interviews were run. DESIGN.md's "interviews of ICP-S1" have no record in the repo, so I treat them as unverified.

#### Verdict in one paragraph
The product's honesty rules (whole record, losses ranked like gains, intervals, lower-bound ranking) work against its supply wedge. The research says most active retail traders lose money and fewer than 1% show persistent skill [S1–S4]. It also says the people with the biggest audiences are on average the least skilled [S5]. A complete, unflattering record is therefore something almost nobody with an audience gains from publishing. Every trader who does publish costs about $1 a month and pays nothing. Meanwhile the only revenue comes from viewers paying for compare and notes on top of records that stay free. The kill gates test neither the viewer-to-trader ratio nor willingness to pay, and those are the two numbers that decide survival.

#### Claims

**C1. Supply: the pool of people whose full record helps them is tiny.** (Confidence: High on the evidence, Medium on how it applies here)
- Across 66,465 discount-broker households (1991–96), the most active traders earned 11.4% a year against the market's 17.9% [S1].
- Using every trade on one national exchange, individual investors' aggregate portfolio lost 3.8 points a year [S2].
- Among day traders on that exchange in 1992–2006, fewer than 1% earned positive abnormal returns after fees in a predictable, reliable way [S3].
- Among futures day traders who kept going for at least 300 sessions, 97% lost money and 0.4% earned more than a bank-teller wage [S4].
- National regulators found that 74–89% of retail CFD accounts lose money [S6].
- Implication (inference): if the evidence of TR-9 is applied honestly, most records will read "Edge: unclear" or show a negative edge. A label that says "unclear" is a weak status good for ICP-S2.
- Cheapest refutation: a private "see your record before publishing" preview for about 30 recruited traders, then measure the publish rate by record quality. If traders with negative or unclear records publish at no lower a rate than positive ones, C1 and C2 weaken.

**C2. Adverse selection runs against the brand.** (Medium)
- Textbook unravelling, where everyone but the worst discloses, needs disclosure to be expected and cheap. Here, not disclosing is the norm everywhere else, and disclosing exposes losses and open positions (TR-4, F-6).
- The likely outcome is a self-selected set of currently-green records, which is survivorship at the profile level. TR-1..TR-8 stop cherry-picking of trades. Nothing stops cherry-picking of traders: who connects, and when.
- TR-5 freezes a record on disconnect, so traders will be slower to connect at all.
- When the market cycle turns, the feed fills with frozen red records or empties out.
- Cheapest refutation: compare the record-quality distribution of people who connect with that of people who decline (the preview from C1), and track connects against market drawdowns.

**C3. The "signals seller" ICP has an incentive against this product.** (Medium–High)
- The evidence on investment influencers says most are unskilled or "antiskilled," and the less skilled ones attract larger followings [S5].
- Selling a paid group depends on the claim not being checkable. A losses-shown, interval-labelled record helps only the skilled minority, who by [S5] have smaller audiences.
- The loudest sellers, who reach the most viewers, are the ones who lose most by connecting.
- The repo works against the ICP in two more ways:
  - F-5's accepted safeguards have the Terms ban "adviser advertising, paid promotion". A seller using the card to market a paid group sits close to that line.
  - POLICY F5 bars paying traders.
- So ICP-S1 gets no money from the product and faces new restrictions.
- Seeing others' success raises risk-taking, and copying raises it further [S7]. Access to a social trading network nearly doubled the disposition effect [S8]. The traders such a community produces are not the ones whose records sell.
- Cheapest refutation: 50 cold outreach messages to people who publicly sell paid groups, offering free connection. A connect rate of 20% or more would refute C3.

**C4. Demand: the free product already does the job the paid one sells.** (Medium)
- F-3 keeps every public record, chart, list, interval and share card free. That is correct for trust, but it leaves "Record tools" (compare side by side, saved views, private notes) as the only paid value.
- A viewer can get most of that with two tabs and a notes app.
- Benchmarks:
  - 2.1% of freemium app downloads convert to paid within 35 days (b16).
  - Paid newsletters convert about 1–3% of free readers (b15).
  - Both bases are more committed than people landing from a shared link.
- The plan launches with no free trial, so it gives up the 42.5% median trial-to-paid path (b17).
- **Estimate**, at the projection's base-case variable costs:
  - Per monthly viewer: 0.10 traders × $1 + $0.004 hosting = $0.104 a month.
  - Net revenue per payer: blended price 0.65 × $9.99 + 0.35 × $8.33 = $9.41, less 6.5% billing = $8.80.
  - Break-even before fixed costs, people and legal: 0.104 / 8.80 ≈ **1.2% of all monthly viewers paying**. The owner's 1.7–2.8% includes more cost lines.
  - The product must reach the top of the newsletter range from a colder audience.
- Cheapest refutation: a fake-door test. Put the locked Record tools button on profiles in test mode and count checkout starts per 1,000 viewers. At least 30 starts per 1,000 would refute C4.

**C5. Unit economics: each trader is a cost centre and each payer has to carry about 7 of them.** (High on the arithmetic, Low on the inputs)
- **Estimate**: at base case (trader_share 0.10, conv 0.015), each payer carries 0.10 / 0.015 ≈ 6.7 connected traders, which is about $6.70 a month of broker data against $8.80 net revenue.
- **Estimate**, lifetime value against acquisition cost:
  - Monthly-plan lifetime net ≈ $9.34 / 0.07 ≈ $133.
  - Annual plan ≈ $93.5 / (1 − 0.44) ≈ $167.
  - Blended ≈ $145.
  - Minus the broker cost carried over the same life (≈ $6.70 × 14 months ≈ $94) leaves a contribution of about **$50 against a base acquisition cost of $150** (cac_paying).
  - Paid acquisition loses money at every base-case setting.
- SnapTrade's daily plan is $1 per connected user plus $100 a month [S12]. A manual sync costs $0.05 per connection per sync [S12], so any "refresh now" feature multiplies the cost.
- The trader count can grow faster than viewers. Traders who connect and share to an audience of zero are pure cost.
- Cheapest refutation: measure monthly viewers per connected trader in weeks 1–8. A sustained 15 or more (trader_share at or below 0.067) moves C5 to Low.

**C6. Two-sided cold start with no subsidy on either side.** (Medium)
- Traders aren't paid (F5), viewers have nothing to watch until traders arrive, and the ranking floor (10 bets, F-4) hides new records from the feed.
- Engagement loops are off the table under POLICY: no alerts (F3b), no streaks or points (F6), nothing pushed.
- Each of these rules is right on its own terms. Together they remove most of the retention tools that consumer finance apps rely on.
- Cheapest refutation: seed one community concierge-style (20 traders, one niche) and measure viewer D7 and viewers per trader after 4 weeks.

**C7. Legal tail: records can work as de facto signals and as adviser advertising.** (Medium)
- Under the adviser marketing rule [S9, S10]:
  - Performance must come with net figures of at least equal prominence.
  - Most ads must show 1-, 5- and 10-year periods.
  - Extracted performance has its own conditions.
- A registered adviser or representative who posts a share card (partial accounts, closed trades only, per-trade equal weight) is very likely posting a non-compliant ad.
- The SEC settled with nine advisers just for putting hypothetical performance on public websites without the required procedures, with no claim that any number was wrong [S11]. Enforcement in this area does not wait for fraud.
- The SEC charged eight people, seven of them presented as successful-trader influencers. They allegedly posted their own buying, holding and price targets to large followings, then sold, for about $100M [S13].
- Public open positions with symbols (TR-4, F-6) plus follows reproduce that pattern's raw material.
- FINRA (Dec 2025) is asking whether existing rules cover social-media-influenced investing [S14].
- The FTC keeps pursuing trading-education sellers' earnings claims; one 2025 settlement totalled $10.5M [S15].
- The owner has replaced counsel with AI findings (POLICY top), so this tail carries no attorney's opinion behind it.
- Cheapest refutation: an hour of securities counsel on two questions, the share card as an adviser ad and scalping via open positions. Add a signup question ("registered adviser or representative?") and count the yes answers.

**C8. Platform risk: the vendor's own terms appear to conflict with public display.** (High that the conflict exists in the text, Medium on how it will be enforced)
- SnapTrade's developer terms [S16] bar clients from re-packaging "End User data ... to third parties" without written consent from both the user and SnapTrade (§2).
- They also say market data "will only be used to serve the End User for which that data is licensed" (§7). A public page that shows open positions' current values to strangers is hard to square with §7.
- SnapTrade may suspend or terminate "at any time" (§4), change the brokerage list "with or without notice" (§2), and change prices on 30 days' notice (§12).
- The repo already treats written permission as a gate (POLICY top, F-5 blocked, F-3 waiting on the "market-data answer"). Until it arrives, the core public product rests on unwritten tolerance.
- The federal data-access rule is under reconsideration, including whether data providers may charge fees [S17]. That is a route to higher per-user costs. Whether brokerage accounts are in scope at all is an open question.
- One vendor still holds every connection (DESIGN Stage 2 CEO concern).
- A securities regulator's staff noted in 2021 that some retail brokers already let customers "display their trades and portfolios to others" [S18]. An incumbent can add verified single-broker records as a feature.
- Cheapest refutation: an email to SnapTrade asking for written permission for public display of closed-trade percentages and open-position percentages. Cost: about zero.

**C9. The kill gates are noisy, gameable, and miss the money question.** (High)
- **Estimate**: with n = 50 traders, the 95% interval on a 60% share rate is ±13.6 points (√(0.6 × 0.4 / 50) = 0.069 × 1.96). A true 55% sharer base still passes about 28% of the time (normal approximation: mean 27.5, sd 3.52, needs 30 or more).
- The first 50 traders will be the owner's own recruits, so the gate measures how warm the network is, not market pull.
- Link-preview crawlers fetch profile pages when a link is pasted, so "external referrer views" will be inflated unless bots are filtered.
- D7 has no minimum cohort size.
- "Miss two or more" lets one gate fail outright.
- No gate covers viewers per trader or willingness to pay, the two inputs that decide C4 and C5.
- Fix: pre-register metric definitions and bot filtering, and add (a) monthly viewers per connected trader of 10 or more and (b) fake-door checkout starts of at least 1.5% of viewers.

**C10. Market timing: launching into the fade of a retail cycle.** (Medium)
- In 2020 the off-exchange share of equity volume, a partial proxy for retail, rose from 38.4% to 46.5% and passed 50% on one day; options volume jumped more than 50% [S18].
- By 2024, only 8% of investors had begun investing in the prior two years, down from 21% in 2021. Non-retirement ownership among adults aged 18-34 fell from 26% to 21% [S19].
- Secondary press reports a renewed retail surge in 2025. I did not open a primary source for it, so it carries Low confidence and is not used as a number.
- Inference: supply of willing, green-record traders is pro-cyclical. A drawdown both shrinks sharing and fills the feed with frozen losses (TR-5).
- Social media is a source of investment information for 24% of investors, and for 35% of those aged 18-29 [S14]. The audience exists, but it is reached on platforms the product doesn't own.

- Repo and council inputs: docs/DESIGN.md (§1.2, §3.5, Stage 5), docs/features/F-3, F-4, F-5, F-6, docs/POLICY.md (top, F3b, F5, F6), docs/DECISIONS.md, docs/direction/market-2026-11-01/projection-assumptions.json, the dashboard's benchmark list (not committed: its links carry third-party names) (b15–b17).

#### Open questions
1. Has SnapTrade given anything in writing on public display (§2 and §7)? If not, C8 is the gate before every other gate.
2. What is the real viewers-per-trader ratio? Nothing in the repo measures it yet, and it drives C5.
3. Do brokerage accounts fall under the federal data-access rule, and could brokers charge aggregators for access?
4. What is the retail share of volume for 2024–2026 from a primary source? I opened none, so C10 rests on survey data.
5. What share of likely ICP-S1 traders are registered representatives or advisers, who would be bound by the marketing rule?
6. Would the Terms' "paid promotion" ban (F-5 safeguards) cover a trader linking a paid group? If it would, ICP-S1 is excluded by contract.

# Appendix G. The data analyst's model

Steady state per month; run with `node`. Inputs are the projection dashboard's three scenarios.

```js
import fs from 'node:fs';
const A = JSON.parse(fs.readFileSync(new URL('./projection-assumptions.json', import.meta.url)));
const S = ['conservative','base','strong'];
const get = s => Object.fromEntries(A.map(a=>[a.key,a[s]]));
const arpu = p => ((1-p.annual_share)*p.price_monthly + p.annual_share*p.price_annual/12)*(1-p.billing_pct);
const varPerViewer = p => p.trader_share*p.broker_per_trader + p.hosting_per_1k/1000;
const fixed = p => p.broker_floor + p.platform_fixed;
const profit = (p,V) => V*p.conv*arpu(p) - (V*varPerViewer(p)+fixed(p));
const be = (p,V) => (V*varPerViewer(p)+fixed(p))/(V*arpu(p));
const beViewers = p => { const m = p.conv*arpu(p)-varPerViewer(p); return m>0? fixed(p)/m : Infinity; };
const f = (x,d=2)=>x.toFixed(d);
console.log('## ARPU, break-even');
for (const s of S){ const p=get(s);
  console.log(s, 'netARPU', f(arpu(p)), 'var/viewer', f(varPerViewer(p),4), 'fixed', fixed(p),
   'BE conv @', [1e3,1e4,5e4,1e5,1e6].map(V=>`${V}:${f(be(p,V)*100)}%`).join(' '),
   'limit', f(varPerViewer(p)/arpu(p)*100)+'%', 'profit@1k', f(profit(p,1e3)), '@10k', f(profit(p,1e4)), '@100k', f(profit(p,1e5)),
   'BE viewers at own conv', isFinite(beViewers(p))?Math.round(beViewers(p)):'never');
  console.log('  per payer: trader cost/payer/mo', f(p.trader_share/p.conv*p.broker_per_trader), 'traders per payer', f(p.trader_share/p.conv,1));
}
// mixed: base everything, conv varied
// tornado on BE conv at 1k and 100k, base, swing each input to cons/strong
console.log('## Tornado (base, swing each input to conservative/strong)');
for (const V of [1e3,1e5]) {
  const b=get('base'); const base=be(b,V); const rows=[];
  for (const a of A){ if(a.key==='conv')continue; const lo={...b,[a.key]:a.conservative}, hi={...b,[a.key]:a.strong};
    rows.push([a.key, be(lo,V)*100, be(hi,V)*100]); }
  rows.sort((x,y)=>Math.abs(y[1]-y[2])-Math.abs(x[1]-x[2]));
  console.log(`V=${V} base BE ${f(base*100)}%`); rows.forEach(r=>console.log(`  ${r[0]}: cons ${f(r[1])}% strong ${f(r[2])}% swing ${f(Math.abs(r[1]-r[2]))}pp`));
  // profit tornado including conv
  const rp=[]; for (const a of A){ const lo={...b,[a.key]:a.conservative}, hi={...b,[a.key]:a.strong}; rp.push([a.key,profit(lo,V),profit(hi,V)]);}
  rp.sort((x,y)=>Math.abs(y[1]-y[2])-Math.abs(x[1]-x[2]));
  console.log(`  profit/mo base ${f(profit(b,V))}`); rp.slice(0,6).forEach(r=>console.log(`   ${r[0]}: ${f(r[1])} .. ${f(r[2])}`));
}
// ratio threshold: trader_share/conv break-even
console.log('## break-even traders per payer (scale limit, base other inputs):', f((arpu(get('base'))-0.004/0.015)/1,2),'USD margin -> traders/payer at $1 =', f(arpu(get('base'))-get('base').hosting_per_1k/1000/get('base').conv,2));
// LTV & CAC
console.log('## LTV / CAC');
for (const s of S){ const p=get(s);
  const mNet = p.price_monthly*(1-(0.029+0.007+0.005)) - 0.30;
  const aNet = p.price_annual*(1-(0.029+0.007+0.005)) - 0.30;
  const lifeM = 1/p.churn_monthly; const ltvM = mNet*lifeM;
  const lifeYrs = 1/(1-p.annual_renewal); const ltvA = aNet*lifeYrs;
  const ltvBlend = (1-p.annual_share)*ltvM + p.annual_share*ltvA;
  const lifeBlendMonths = (1-p.annual_share)*lifeM + p.annual_share*12*lifeYrs;
  // contribution: allocate trader+hosting variable cost per payer
  const allocPerPayerMo = varPerViewer(p)/p.conv;
  const contribMo = arpu(p) - allocPerPayerMo;
  const ltvContrib = contribMo*lifeBlendMonths;
  const cacBlended = p.cac_paying*p.paid_share;
  console.log(s, {mNet:f(mNet), aNet:f(aNet), lifeM:f(lifeM,1), lifeYrs:f(lifeYrs,2), ltvM:f(ltvM), ltvA:f(ltvA), ltvBlend:f(ltvBlend), lifeBlendMonths:f(lifeBlendMonths,1),
    allocPerPayerMo:f(allocPerPayerMo), contribMo:f(contribMo), ltvContrib:f(ltvContrib), cacPaid:p.cac_paying, cacBlended:f(cacBlended),
    paybackPaidRevMonths:f(p.cac_paying/arpu(p),1), paybackPaidContribMonths: contribMo>0?f(p.cac_paying/contribMo,1):'never',
    paybackBlendedContrib: contribMo>0?f(cacBlended/contribMo,1):'never', ltvRevToCacPaid:f(ltvBlend/p.cac_paying), ltvContribToCacPaid:f(ltvContrib/p.cac_paying), ltvContribToCacBlend:f(ltvContrib/cacBlended)});
}
// implied monthly churn from 17% 12-mo retention
console.log('implied monthly churn from 17% 1y retention:', f((1-Math.pow(0.17,1/12))*100,1)+'%', 'from 44.1% yearly ->', f((1-0.441)*100,1)+'% annual churn');
// billing fee check
for (const pr of [9.99,99.99]) console.log('fee% at', pr, f(((pr*0.041)+0.30)/pr*100,2)+'%');
```

# Proposals for the idea board

The same table as Part A §13, for the idea board session that owns the register in `docs/IDEAS.md`. Every row is
raised by the market research council with status "proposed" and links this record (`docs/direction/market-2026-11-01.md`).
Rows marked **flag for owner** would change an owner decision and are the owner's call alone.

"MRC" means market research council. A proposal that changes an owner decision says **flag for owner**.
